- **HIM (Talk Nine, about a friend)** — *The truck-trailer maker leaves California and Texas.* Situation: “I've got a friend whose family controls about eight percent of the truck trailer market. He just closed his last factory in California, and he had one in Texas that was even worse. The workers comp cost in his Texas plant got to be double digit percentages of payroll.” / Did: “He closed his plant and moved it to Ogden, Utah, where a bunch of believing Mormons are raising big families and don't game the workers' comp system. The workers' comp expense is two percent of payroll.” (p. 438) Munger's reading: “It's just the incentive structure that so rewards all this fraud is put in place by these ignorant legislatures” (p. 438) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 438)
- **HIM (Talk Nine, about Buffett on the Washington Post board)** — *The Post's buyback, and the McKinsey adviser who opposed it.* Situation: a partner at McKinsey, taught the efficient-market line at business school, was a paid consultant to the Post and “told the Washington Post It shouldn't buy its own stock.” Munger's valuation: “Washington Post stock was selling at a fifth of what an orangutan could figure was the plain value per share by just counting up the values and dividing.” (p. 415) / Did (Buffett's act, as Munger reports it): “Well, fortunately, they put Warren Buffett on the board, and he convinced them to buy back more than half of the outstanding stock, which enriched the remaining shareholders by much more than a billion dollars.” (pp. 415–416) Munger's comment: “So, there was at least one instance of a place that quickly killed a wrong academic theory.” (p. 416) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 415–416)
- **HIM (Talk Ten)** — *Choosing to work under people he admired.* “You particularly want to avoid working directly under somebody you don't admire and don't want to be like.” / Did: “I coped in my time by identifying people I admired and by maneuvering, mostly without criticizing anybody, so that I was usually working under the right sort of people.” (p. 472) Of his partners: “I think I got them partly because I tried to deserve them and partly because I was shrewd enough to select them, and partly there was some luck.” (p. 476) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 472, 476)
- **HIM (Talk Ten, about his grandfather)** — *Judge Munger saved Charlie's uncle's tiny bank at a cost to himself.* Situation: “Along the way, in the '30's, my uncle's tiny bank failed and couldn't reopen without help.” (pp. 478–479; the .txt has “couldn’t” with a curly apostrophe) / Did: “My grandfather saved the bank by exchanging over a third of his good assets for horrible bank assets.” Why he could: “One such value was prudence as the servant of duty.” Being the kind of man he was, “he underspent his income all his life and left his widow in comfortable circumstances.” (p. 478) Munger: “I've always remembered the event.” (p. 479) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 478–479; the grandfather's being a judge is also in Damn Right!, p. 44, see TEMPERAMENT)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven, Preface)** — *Deciding to publish the long psychology talk, against four objections he lists.* Situation: reading transcripts of his psychology talks from fifteen years earlier, “I realized that I could now create a more logical but much longer ‘talk,’ including most of what I had earlier said.” (p. 482) / The four objections, HIM: (1) “would be more boring and confusing to many people than any earlier talk.” (2) “my formal psychological knowledge came only from skimming three psychology textbooks about fifteen years ago, I know virtually nothing about any academic psychology later developed.” (p. 482) (3) “the shoe button complex,” his Harvard Law classmate Ed Rothschild's name for “a popping-off” in oracular style. (p. 483) (4) “Fourth, I might make a fool of myself.” (p. 483) / Did: “Despite these four very considerable objections, I decided to publish the much-expanded version.” “Thus, after many decades in which I have succeeded mostly by restricting action to jobs and methods in which I was unlikely to fail, I have now chosen a course of action in which (1) I have no significant personal benefit to gain, (2) I will surely give some pain to family members and friends, and (3) I may make myself ridiculous.” (p. 483) / Reasons: “my nature makes me incline toward diagnosing and talking about errors in conventional wisdom” (pp. 483–484); Diogenes: “Of what use is a philosopher who never offends anybody?” (p. 484); and “I have fallen in love with my way of laying out psychology because it has been so useful for me.” (p. 484) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 482–484)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — *Skimming three textbooks, and giving Cialdini's book to his children.* “With the push given by Cialdini's book, I soon skimmed through three much used textbooks covering introductory psychology.” (p. 492) “This material was stolen from its various discoverers (most of whose names I didn't even try to learn), often with new descriptions and titles selected to fit Munger's notion of what makes recall easy for Munger, then revised to make Munger's use easy as he seeks to avoid errors.” (p. 493) And: “I immediately sent copies of Cialdini's book to all my children.” (p. 491) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 491–493)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — *Belridge Oil: bought 300 shares, declined 1,500 more — admitted error.* Situation: “A friendly broker called and offered me 300 shares of ridiculously underpriced, very thinly traded Belridge Oil at $115 per share, which I purchased using cash I had on hand.” “The next day, he offered me 1,500 more shares at the same price, which I declined to buy partly because I could only have made the purchase had I sold something or borrowed the required $173,000.” / Did: bought the 300, declined the 1,500. / Reason he gives for calling it a mistake: “This was a very irrational decision. I was a well-to-do man with no debt; there was no risk of loss; and similar no risk opportunities were not likely to come along.” (p. 571) / Result: “Within two years, Belridge Oil sold out to Shell at a price of about $3,700 per share, which made me about $5.4 million poorer than I would have been had I then been psychologically acute.” “As this tale demonstrates, psychological ignorance can be very expensive.” (p. 571) He puts the cause partly on Deprival-Super Reaction Tendency: “I myself, the would-be instructor here, many decades ago made a big mistake caused in part by subconscious operation of my Deprival-Super reaction Tendency.” (p. 570) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 570–571)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — *Chairman of an academic search committee: stopped the interviews and appointed on the record.* Situation: “I once chose exactly this course of action while I served as chairman of an academic search committee.” (the course: underweigh face-to-face impressions, overweigh the applicant's past record, p. 560) / Did: “I convinced fellow committee members to stop all further interviews and simply appoint a person whose achievement record was much better than that of any other applicant.” (p. 560) / Reason, answering the objection: “And when it was suggested to me that I wasn't giving “academic due process,” I replied that I was the one being true to academic values because I was using academic research showing poor predictive value of impressions from face-to-face interviews.” (p. 560) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 560)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — *Bought a house from one of two neighbors at war over a tree.* The only facts given: “as I once observed when I bought a house from one of two neighbors locked into hatred by a tiny tree newly installed by one of them.” (p. 568) The price, the reason for buying and the outcome are not given; he uses it as his example of “Even a one-degree loss from a 1SO-degree view will sometime create enough Deprival-Super reaction Tendency to turn a neighbor into an enemy” (the .txt prints “1SO” and “sometime”). (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 568)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven, Questions and Answers)** — *The McDonnell Douglas airliner evacuation test: what the company did, and why, by his tendency checklist.* Situation: “One of my favorite cases involves the McDonnell Douglas airliner evacuation test.” (p. 601) “Before a new airliner can be sold, the government requires that it pass an evacuation test, during which a full load of passengers must get our in some short period of time.” (pp. 601–602; “our” as printed) “So McDonnell Douglas scheduled such a test in a darkened hangar using a lot of old people as evacuees.” (p. 602) What the company did: “The first test was made in the morning. There were about twenty very serious injuries, and the evacuation took so long it flunked the time test. So what did McDonnell Douglas next do? It repeated the test in the afternoon, and this time there was another failure, with about twenty more serious injuries, including one case of permanent paralysis.” (p. 602) The reasons he gives, “using my tendency list as a checklist”: “Reward-Super Response Tendency drove McDonnell Douglas to act fast. It couldn't sell its airliner until it passed the test.” “Also pushing the company was Doubt-Avoidance Tendency with its natural drive to arrive at a decision and run with it.” (p. 602) “By now the course of action had been decided, so Inconsistency Avoidance Tendency helped preserve the near idiotic plan.” (p. 603) “Social Proof Tendency, therefore, swamped the queasiness.” (p. 603) “McDonnell Douglas ignored the strong disconfirming evidence from the failure of the first test because confirmation bias, aided by the triggering of strong Deprival- Super Reaction Tendency, favored maintaining the original plan.” (p. 603; space after “Deprival-” as printed) “McDonnell Douglas' Deprival-Super Reaction Tendency was now like that which causes a gambler, bent on getting even after a huge loss, to make his final big bet.” (p. 603) His closing remark: “More psychology-based explanation can probably be made, but the foregoing discussion is complete enough to demonstrate the utility of my system when used in a checklist mode.” (pp. 603–604) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 601–604)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven, Questions and Answers)** — *Changed his mind about the Harvard Business School's decision trees.* Item “Seven” on his list of examples of the utility of elementary psychological knowledge: “The Harvard Business School's emphasis on decision trees.” (pp. 605–606) What he used to do: “When I was young and foolish I used to laugh at the Harvard Business School. I said, "They're teaching twenty-eight year- old people that high school algebra works in real life"” (p. 606; space after “year-” as printed) What changed and why: “But later, I wised up and realized that it was very important that they do that to counter some bad effects from psychological tendencies. Better late than never.” (p. 606) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 605–606)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven Revisited, 2006)** — *Wrote to Harvard urging a Ph.D. for Judith Rich Harris, and failed.* Situation: “Long before the book was published, Ms. Harris, now 67, was kicked out of Harvard's PhD. program in psychology because Harvard believed that she lacked qualities ideal in psychological research.” (p. 610) She later won an American Psychological Association medal “named after the man who signed her dismissal notice from Harvard,” (p. 611) What he did: “When I learned from her impressive book that this ironic result had occurred, I wrote to Harvard, my alma mater, urging it to award Ms. Harris, whom I did not know, an honorary PhD., or, better yet, a real Ph. D. I cited the example of Oxford.” (p. 611) Outcome, his words: “I failed utterly in my effort to convince Harvard to imitate Oxford in this way. But Harvard did later recruit from MIT one of the most famous living psychology professors, Steven Pinker, and Pinker is a big admirer of Ms. Harris.” (p. 611) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 610–611)
- **REPORTED (Buffett, 1999 Chairman's letter, quoted in the book's introduction)** — *Scott Fetzer, the banker's book.* Situation, as Buffett tells it: an investment bank failed to sell Scott Fetzer; Buffett wrote to its CEO, Ralph Schey, and "within a week we had a deal"; the bank's engagement letter nonetheless gave it a $2.5 million fee on any sale, and the banker offered Berkshire a copy of the book his firm had prepared on Scott Fetzer. / Did: Munger declined it, with this answer. Buffett's introduction to it: “With his customary tact, Charlie responded:” The answer: “I'll give you 2.5 million ‘not’ to read it.” / Reason: none given beyond the answer. (Buffett's words; Munger's sentence is reported, not recorded.) (The Complete Investor, p. 6)
- **AUTHOR + HIM** — *Bank stocks, first quarter 2009.* AUTHOR: “during the first quarter of 2009, when most everyone was still panicking from the financial crisis, Munger took the excess cash available in the accounts of the Daily Journal and plowed it into bank stocks.” Reason, HIM: “We just put the money in. It didn't take any novel thought. It was a once-in-40-year opportunity.” (The Complete Investor, p. 80; book's label: Jason Zweig Interview, 2014 (the HIM quote))
- **REPORTED (Jason Zweig, email to the author, October 2014)** — *Wells Fargo, March 2009.* Zweig lists it as an example of imperturbability, with no reasoning from Munger: “when Charlie buys Wells Fargo the day before the bottom tick in March 2009.” (The Complete Investor, p. 9)
- **AUTHOR** — *Early mistakes.* “One of his early mistakes was to own a company that made electrical transformers.” “He has also said that he has found himself in real estate ventures that would only be enjoyed by a masochist.” No quote from him in this book on either. Also: “He seems to have more tolerance for mistakes in real estate than other areas of business.” (The Complete Investor, p. 45)
- **AUTHOR** — *Dexter Shoes.* The author ties both men to the error: “Buying Dexter Shoes was also a multi-billion dollar mistake for Berkshire.” “In doing their due-diligence analysis for Dexter shoes, Buffett and Munger made the mistakes of not making sure the business had a moat and being too focused on what they thought was an attractive purchase price.” Buffett's own words, as the book quotes them (not Munger's): “What I had assessed as durable competitive advantage vanished within a few years.” (The Complete Investor, p. 45)
- **AUTHOR** — *Wal-Mart, not bought.* “Munger and Buffett's decision to not invest in Wal-Mart is just one example of a mistake of omission.” “Buffett has said that just this one mistake with Wal-Mart cost them $10 billion.” (The $10 billion figure is Buffett's, as the author reports it. Munger gives no reason in this passage.) (The Complete Investor, p. 46)
- **HIM** — *Admitted error: what he did not do.* “Our biggest mistakes were things we didn't do, companies we didn't buy.” “We saw it, but didn't act on it.” (The Complete Investor, p. 46; book's label: Fortune Magazine, 1998; Berkshire Annual Meeting, 2001)
- **HIM** — *Admitted error: See's Candies.* “We were pretty damn stupid when we bought See's—just a little less stupid enough to buy it.” (The Complete Investor, pp. 86–87; book's label: Berkshire Annual Meeting, 2014)
- **HIM** — *Admitted error: early fretting.* “We left a lot of money on the table through early fretting.” (The Complete Investor, p. 79; book's label: Wesco Annual Meeting, 2001)
- **AUTHOR** — *Who sets pay.* “It's precisely because of the dangers of misaligned incentives that Munger and Buffett chose to make compensation decisions themselves, whereas they delegate almost all management responsibilities.” (The Complete Investor, p. 52)
- **AUTHOR** — *From cigar butts to quality.* “As a result of the new post-Great Depression environment, Munger and many other Graham value investors began to apply the same Graham value investing principles to businesses that were of high quality instead of businesses trading below liquidation value—and the margin of safety process worked just as well.” (No quote from Munger in this passage; his own words on cigar butts are in RULES, p. 31–32.) (The Complete Investor, p. 32)
- **AUTHOR** — *The year 2002.* “The year 2002 was an excellent time to buy stocks, but the memory of the crash of the stock market after the collapse of the Internet bubble was so vivid that only people who, like Munger, had trained themselves to overcome this tendency were able to use that opportunity to reap big investing rewards.” (No purchase is named in the passage.) (The Complete Investor, p. 67)
- **AUTHOR** — *Valuation in his head.* “In an ideal situation, the process of determining the intrinsic valuation of a business is easy enough that Munger can do that valuation in his head.” (The Complete Investor, p. 94)
- **AUTHOR (joint: Munger and Buffett; the text ties both)** — *Owner's earnings as the starting point.* “When Munger and Buffett value a business, they use what they call owner's earnings as the starting point.” (The Complete Investor, p. 95)

## 2. RULES IN HIS WORDS

- **HIM** — On physics at Michigan. “To me, it was a total eye-opener,” / “The tradition of always looking for the answer in the most fundamental way available-that is a great tradition, and it saves a lot of time in this world. And, of course, the problems are hard enough that you have to learn to have what some people call assiduity. Well, I've always liked that word-because to me it means that you sit down on your ass until you've solved your problem.” (Damn Right!, p. 60)
- **HIM** — On science. “I am in no sense a working scientist or a working amateur scientist,” “I have a very deep appreciation of science and I find the methods used are useful outside of science.” (AUTHOR adds that he would require physics of everyone who qualified, “simply because it teaches a person how to think.”) (Damn Right!, p. 60)
- **HIM** — Poker. “Playing poker in the Army and as a young lawyer honed my business skills,” “What you have to learn is to fold early when the odds are against you, or if you have a big edge, back it heavily because you don't get a big edge often. Opportunity comes, but it doesn't come often, so seize it when it does come.” (Damn Right!, p. 63)
- **HIM** — After Teddy's death. “You should never, when facing some unbelievable tragedy, let one tragedy increase to two or three through your failure of will.” (Damn Right!, p. 73)
- **HIM** — On regret. “I don't spend much time regretting the past, once I've taken my lesson from it. I don't dwell on it. Certainly I had more sense when I was 32 than I did when I was 22. But I don't have any feeling of terrible regret.” (Damn Right!, p. 73)
- **HIM** — Opportunity cost. “Life is a whole series of opportunity costs,” “You've got to marry the best person who is convenient to find that will have you. An investment is much the same sort of process.” (AUTHOR: “perhaps he wasn't speaking specifically of his own experience.”) (Damn Right!, p. 73)
- **HIM** — Why he got rich. “I liked the independence of a capitalist. And I always had sort of a gambling personality. I liked figuring things out and making bets. So I simply did what came naturally.” (Damn Right!, p. 76) Also in, HIM (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 263): “Plus, I like the independence of a capitalist. And I'd always had sort of a gambling personality. I like figuring things out and making bets. So I simply did what came naturally.”
- **HIM** — Good and bad businesses. “The difference between a good business and a bad business is that good businesses throw up one easy decision after another. The bad businesses throw up painful decisions time after time.” (The .txt prints “had business” for “bad business”, an OCR error; read as “bad”.) (Damn Right!, p. 91)
- **HIM** — Buying to liquidate. “It's not that much fun to buy a company that you hope liquidates at a profit just before it is destined to go broke.” (Damn Right!, p. 90)
- **HIM** — After the transformer business. “I never went back to the high-tech mode. I tried it once and found it to have many problems. I was like Mark Twain's cat that, after a bad experience, never again sat on a hot stove or on a cold stove either.” (Damn Right!, p. 90)
- **HIM** — The McFayden lesson. “It is a lesson I have never forgotten and it has helped my business career, even though I find, like other businessmen, that it is harder to starve the lawyers now than it used to be.” (See DECISIONS, pp. 88–89.) (Damn Right!, p. 89)
- **HIM** — Landscaping. “Lush landscaping,” “That's what sells. You spend money on trees, and you get it back triple. Stinting on landscaping is building malpractice.” (Damn Right!, p. 99)
- **HIM** — Colleagues. “You know the cliche that opposites attract?” “Well, opposites don't attract. Everybody engaged in complicated work needs colleagues. Just the discipline of having to put your thoughts in order with somebody else is a very useful thing.” (Damn Right!, p. 24)
- **HIM** — His own life, in his words. “not a big story-long dull stretches are possible. To finish first you have to first finish. Don't get in a position where you go back to go.” and “The reason it was possible? Self-education-developing mental discipline, big ideas that really work.” (Damn Right!, p. 25)
- **HIM** — Behaviour. “How you behave in one place,” he says, “will help in surprising ways later.” (Damn Right!, p. 26)
- **HIM** — Upbringing. “While no real money came down, my family gave me a good education and a marvelous example of how people should behave, and in the end that was more valuable than money,” “Being surrounded by right values from the beginning is an immense treasure.” (Damn Right!, p. 21)
- **HIM** — Chapter epigraph. “There are a lot of things in life way more important than money. All that said, some people do get confused. I play golf with a man who says, “What good is health? You can't buy money with it.”” (The inner sentence is the golf partner's.) (Damn Right!, p. 28)
- **HIM** — Learning. “I learn better sort of plowing through written material by myself. I've done a lot of that in my life. I frequently like the eminent dead better than the live teachers.” (Damn Right!, p. 65)
- **HIM** — The store job. “The Buffett family store provided a very desirable introduction to business. It required hard, accurate work over long hours, which caused many of the young workers, including me (and later Ernest's grandson Warren), to look for an easier career and to be cheerful upon finding disadvantages therein.” (Damn Right!, p. 57)
- **REPORTED (Hal Borthwick, stepson, on what Charlie drummed in)** — “Do the best that you can do. Never tell a lie. If you say you're going to do it, get it done. Nobody gives a shit about an excuse. Leave for the meeting early. Don't be late, but if you are late, don't bother giving people excuses. Just apologize. They're due the apology, but they're not interested in an excuse.” (.txt OCR prints “clone” for “done”.) “Return your calls quickly. The other thing is the five-second no. You've got to make your mind up. You don't leave people hanging.” REPORTED (Emilie, p. 85): when they came back with a reason they couldn't, he sent them out “to solve the problem and keep our word. Fine tune your judgment.” (Damn Right!, pp. 84–85)
- **AUTHOR (only the word is his)** — “As Munger often says, you can get “lollapalooza” results when you bring together the right combination of factors.” No verbatim sentence of his on this page. (Damn Right!, p. 24)
- **HIM** — Irrationality, on starting out. “just out of our respective graduate schools, my friend Warren Buffett and I entered the business world to find huge, predictable patterns of extreme irrationality. These irrationalities were obviously important to what we wanted to do, but our professors had never mentioned them. [Understanding the problem of irrationalities] was not easy ... I came to [study] the psychology of human misjudgment almost against my will: I rejected it until I realized that my attitude was costing me a lot of money and reduced my ability to help everything I loved.” (The brackets and ellipsis are in the book.) (Damn Right!, p. 101)
- **HIM** — Hands-on work. “I don't believe in doing everything with lily white hands, nor would I care to be like [early financier] Russell Sage, remembered only for skill in buying and selling little pieces of paper.” (Damn Right!, p. 104)
- **AUTHOR (his view, not his words)** — Reversing experts. “Munger agrees that humor helps-as does being plainly right-when reversing expert's ideas in their own territory, but he thinks that he sometimes causes resentment. It requires constant vigilance to keep from going too far.” (Damn Right!, p. 104)
- **HIM** — Partners. “A partner ideally is capable of working alone,” “You can be a dominant partner, subordinate partner, or an always collaborative equal partner. I've done all three. People couldn't believe that I suddenly made myself a subordinate partner to Warren. But there are some people that it's okay to be subordinate partner to. I didn't have the kind of ego that prevented it. There always are people who will be better at something than you are. You have to learn to be a follower before you become a leader. People should learn to play all roles. You can divide up in different ways with different people.” (Damn Right!, p. 112)
- **HIM** — Two like minds. “If a mistake goes through one filter, it is likely to go through both,” (about himself and Buffett thinking alike; AUTHOR: “The tendency for him and Buffett to think alike hasn't always been an advantage.”) (Damn Right!, p. 113)
- **AUTHOR (attributing the idea to him)** — Envy. “One of Munger's favorite ideas is from Aristotle, ‘The best way to avoid envy is to deserve the success you get.' ” (The words are Aristotle's as the author gives them; the sentence runs across pp. 113–114.) Also in, HIM (p. 363, Appendix D): “The best way to avoid envy, recognized by Aristotle, is to plainly deserve the success we get.” (Damn Right!, pp. 113–114)
- **HIM** — Independent thought. “If, in your thinking, you rely entirely on others, often through purchase of professional advice, whenever outside a small territory of your own, you will suffer much calamity.” AUTHOR on how he applies it: “He considers what they say, continues his research, seeks other opinions, and in the end, reaches his own conclusion.” Also in, HIM (p. 356, Appendix D): the same sentence, “If, in your thinking, you rely entirely on others…” (see the new entry under pp. 356–357). (Damn Right!, p. 114)
- **HIM** — On Graham. “I thought a lot of them were just madness,” “They ignored relevant facts.” “Ben Graham had blind spots. He had too low an appreciation of the fact that some businesses were worth paying big premiums for.” (Damn Right!, pp. 114–115)
- **HIM** — What of Graham he kept. “The basic concept of value to a private owner and being motivated when you're buying and selling securities by reference to intrinsic value instead of price momentum-I don't think that will ever be outdated,” “I never want to pay above intrinsic value for stock-with very rare exceptions where someone like Warren Buffett is in charge,” “There are people-very few-worth paying up a bit to get in with for a long-term advantage. The investment game always involves considering both quality and price, and the trick is to get more quality than you pay for in price. It's just that simple.” (Damn Right!, p. 115) Also in: HIM, The Complete Investor, p. 23 (book's label: Damn Right, 2000).
- **HIM** — Freedom to be useful. Munger “hoped to use his wealth, when he felt secure with it, to emulate his childhood idol, Benjamin Franklin” (AUTHOR). HIM: “Franklin was able to make the contribution he did because he had (financial) freedom.” AUTHOR: he “came to understand that in order to be truly wealthy, a person needed to build ownership in a business.” (Damn Right!, p. 130)
- **HIM (paraphrasing Parkinson)** — Chapter epigraph. “The opulence at the head office is often inversely related to the financial substance of the firm.” (Damn Right!, p. 134)
- **HIM** — The cheap money-manager record. “Over the course of Wheeler, Munger's first 13 years of life, ending with 1974, an investment mimicking performance of the Dow Jones Industrial Average, after counting all dividends received, would have produced a nominal return just above zero,” (Damn Right!, p. 149)
- **HIM** — Partners. “All my life, I had high grade partners, some of the very best that could ever be. Even now that he is so famous, people underrate how fine a partner Buffett is. Jack Wheeler was great, even though he drank too much.” “Marshall was wonderful-worked hard on projects, made a big partnership contribution in pushing so hard for the purchase of See's Candy. I never had any flannel-mouthed baloney in the operation. I dealt with quality people.” (The .txt prints “A]” for “Al” before “Marshall”.) (Damn Right!, p. 152)
- **HIM** — Complexity as a warning. “because so many people create complications to hide fraud,” “People assume if what you're doing is enormously complicated, you're probably doing something wrong.” (Context: the SEC and Blue Chip's criss-crossed ownerships; see DECISIONS, pp. 161–162.) (Damn Right!, pp. 161–162)
- **HIM + Buffett (joint letter)** — Simplicity. “Also, simplicity has a way of improving performance through enabling us better to understand what we are doing.” (Buffett and Munger together wrote this to shareholders at the merger; not Munger alone.) (Damn Right!, p. 164)
- **HIM** — Patience and decisiveness. AUTHOR: Charlie finds it interesting “how few big business decisions were involved in creating billions of dollars out of less than $40 million, fewer than one every three years.” HIM (the .txt prints “1” for “I” before “think”): “think the record shows the advantage of a peculiar mind-set-not seeking action for its own sake, but instead combining extreme patience with extreme decisiveness.” (Damn Right!, pp. 164–165)
- **REPORTED (Wendy Munger)** — Trust over economics. “the lesson of his business life is that you don't want to do business with people you can't trust. The economics are irrelevant if you don't have trust. Most people are just thinking about the economics, thinking that the contract will save you when entering into a transaction with someone you can't trust. You must do business with high-grade people-that's all he will deal with.” (AUTHOR, p. 168: “There never had been a written contract covering the terms of their work together. Rather, Munger and Buffett simply went forward on trust.”) (Damn Right!, pp. 168–169)
- **HIM** — Pigs. “Never wrestle with a pig because if you do you'll both get dirty, but the pig will enjoy it.” (AUTHOR: “Charlie also puts it this way:” after the Wendy quote.) (Damn Right!, p. 169)
- **HIM** — Epigraph, on rewards. “Generally, I'm not in favor of a social system that throws huge rewards to people who don't improve the factories, don't invent better systems, and so forth. Of course, you can argue that I'm condemning myself. All I can say is it's almost intentional.” (Damn Right!, p. 171)
- **HIM** — Business traps (the mouse). Speaking at See's seventy-fifth anniversary: “my favorite business analogy-the mouse who says ‘let me out of the trap, I've decided I don't want the cheese.' There are a million business traps. You can get sloppy, you can get alcoholic, you can get megalomania, you can not understand your own limitations. There are a million ways to gum it up.” (Damn Right!, p. 173)
- **HIM** — Why See's has stayed out of traps. “The ordinary candy company puts in too many stores,” “You have this huge overhead you're carrying through July and August, and you just can't get well at Christmas. But See's has always had the discipline of knowing their own business.” “And of course the fanaticism about the quality of the product and service is the heart and soul of the business.” (Damn Right!, p. 173)
- **HIM** — Deserved trust. “You get suppliers who are good and who are trusted because they deserve trust, and you behave the same way toward your own customer, then you are a little part of a civilization that is a seamless web of deserved trust. This is the way the world ought to work. It is a better example for everyone else. It is the right way to build up a state or a civilization.” “It is Ben Franklin (or his business philosophy) all over again, alive and well at See's after all these years.” (Damn Right!, p. 173) Also in: HIM (Talk Ten): “in your own life what you want to maximize is a seamless web of deserved trust.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 480)
- **HIM** — Winner-take-all. “In some businesses, the very nature of things is a sort of cascade toward the overwhelming dominance of one firm,” “It tends to cascade to a winner-take-all result. And these advantages of scale are so great, for example, that when Jack Welch came into General Electric, he just said, ‘To hell with it. We're either going to be number one or number two in every field we're in or we're going to be out.' That was a very tough-minded thing to do, but I think it was a correct decision if you're thinking about maximizing shareholder wealth.” (Damn Right!, p. 181)
- **HIM** — Paying for quality. “I don't think it's necessary to be as dumb as we were.” (The .txt prints “1” for “I”; AUTHOR: “Munger says he and Buffett should have seen the advantages of paying for quality much earlier.”) (Damn Right!, p. 183) Also in: HIM, The Complete Investor, p. 45 (book's label: Berkshire Annual Meeting, 2011).
- **HIM** — Cash-throwing businesses. “By the way, we really shouldn't complain about this because we've carefully selected a bunch of businesses that just drown in money every year.” AUTHOR: he told shareholders there are many businesses that throw off cash but cannot be expanded, and “To try to expand would be throwing money down a rat hole, he said.” (AUTHOR's paraphrase.) (Damn Right!, p. 183)
- **HIM** — Biography. “I am a biography nut myself,” (the .txt prints “'Jam”; read as “I am”) “and I think when you're trying to teach the great concepts that work, it helps to tie them into the lives and personalities of the people who developed them. I think that you learn economics better if you make Adam Smith your friend. That sounds funny, making friends among the eminent dead, but if you go through life making friends with the eminent dead who had the right ideas, I think it will work better in life and work better in education. It's way better than just giving the basic concepts.” (Damn Right!, p. 187) Also in: HIM (See's Candy 75th anniversary, as Broggie gives it): “I am a biography nut myself. And I think when you're trying to teach the great concepts that work, it helps to tie them into the lives and personalities of the people who developed them. I think you learn economics better if you make Adam Smith your friend.” and “making friends with the eminent dead who had the right ideas, I think it will work better for you in life and work better in education. It's way better than just giving the basic concepts.” (this version has ‘for you’ after ‘better’) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 28–29) Also in: Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 141–142 (HIM, Tilson's notes: the same passage, ending “you make Adam Smith your friend.”).
- **HIM** — Useful, not rich. “I always cared more about being useful than dying rich,” “but sometimes I drift too far away from this idea.” (Damn Right!, p. 187)
- **HIM** — Population, in a letter to Fortune (1990). “In a finite world system, subject to the laws of physics, two variables (population and per capita welfare) can't both be maximized forever.” “No one contemplating the prospective environmental burden described by the Ehrlichs can be confident that such a ‘benign demographic transition' will occur or that the population-growth-driven conditions won't be ghastly in 100 years or less. For one thing, the technological development that is necessary to permit a peacetime population increase will also make weapons both more effective and more generally available in a more crowded world.” (Damn Right!, p. 192)
- **HIM** — Epigraph, Wesco and the S&L war. “If you mix raisins with turds, they are still turds.” (Berkshire Hathaway annual meeting, May 2000, as printed under the chapter title.) (Damn Right!, p. 205)
- **HIM** — On deposit insurance and bolder conduct (1983 Wesco letter). “A sort of Gresham's law (bad loan practice drives out good) may take effect at fully competitive but deposit-insured institutions.” (The .txt prints “had” for “bad”.) AUTHOR frames it: Munger “is no champion of government regulation” but thought the timing and coordination of deregulation dangerous. (Damn Right!, pp. 208–209)
- **HIM** — Deposit insurance, kept. “I want banking insured. Bank panics are for the birds.” AUTHOR: his fury at the S&L industry “did not mean he opposed the concept of deposit insurance”. (Damn Right!, p. 213)
- **HIM** — The few big chances. “A few major opportunities clearly recognizable as such, will usually come to one who continuously searches and waits, with a curious mind, loving diagnosis involving multiple variables. And then all that is required is a willingness to bet heavily when the odds are extremely favorable, using resources available as a result of prudence and patience in the past.” (See also DECISIONS, Freddie Mac.) (Damn Right!, p. 210) Also in: HIM, The Complete Investor, p. 55 (book's label: Wesco Annual Meeting, 1996).
- **HIM** — Remembering the obvious; not stupid versus intelligent. “Wesco continues to try more to profit from always remembering the obvious than from grasping the esoteric,” “It is remarkable how much long-term advantage people like us have gotten by trying to be consistently not stupid, instead of trying to be very intelligent. There must be some wisdom in the folk saying, ‘It's the strong swimmers who drown.’” (Damn Right!, p. 217) Also in: HIM, The Complete Investor, p. 15 (book's label: Wesco Annual Report, 1989).
- **HIM** — Wesco is not small Berkshire. “Wesco is not an equally-good-but-smaller version of Berkshire Hathaway, better because its small size makes growth easier. Instead, each dollar of book value at Wesco continues to provide much less intrinsic value than a similar dollar of book value at Berkshire Hathaway.” (Damn Right!, p. 220)
- **HIM** — A cult. “what we have created at Berkshire and Wesco is, to some extent, a cult. And you can say it's a nice cult and you like the people who join-and we do feel that way. But to some extent, we have followers who are unusually interested in what we do and feel comfortable about investing with us. I think it's had effects on the stock prices of Wesco and Berkshire.” (Damn Right!, p. 220)
- **HIM** — Epigraph, Berkshire: several things to know, scale among them. “If you're going to learn to drive a car, it doesn't do any good just to know how to use the accelerator. There are four or five things you have to know before you understand the system correctly. I do think some things are way more important than others, and in the game we're in, understanding the advantages of scale, scale of experience, efficiency in the plant, scale of experience in leasing, other advantages of scale. [Take] Adam Smith's pin factory, I think that's a very important basic concept, but it's just one.” (Damn Right!, p. 223)
- **HIM** — A few great investments. “There are huge advantages for an individual to get into a position where you make a few great investments and just sit back,” “You're paying less to brokers. You're listening to less nonsense....” “If it works, the governmental tax system gives you an extra one, two, or three percentage points per annum with compound effects.” (Damn Right!, p. 228)
- **HIM** — Berkshire is not a closed-end fund. “No, it never was,” “We always preferred operating companies to marketable securities. We used float to buy other stocks. Berkshire has a lot of marketable securities-and big operating companies. We like that system. We generate all this cash. We started out that way, buying companies that throw off cash. Why should we change?” (Damn Right!, p. 228)
- **HIM** — What Berkshire contributes, and what managers need. “Our chief contribution to the businesses we acquire,” “is what we don't do.” “There's integrity, intelligence, experience, and dedication,” “That's what human enterprises need to run well.” (Damn Right!, p. 231)
- **HIM** — Overhead, headquarters, decentralization. “I don't know of anybody our size who has lower overhead than we do,” “And we like it that way. Once a company starts getting fancy,” “it's difficult to stop.” On a subpoena for “staff papers”: “There were no papers. There was no staff.” “We've decentralized power in our operating businesses to a point just short of total abdication....” “Our model's not right for everybody, but it's suited us and the kind of people who've joined us. But we don't have criticism for others-such as General Electric-who operate with plans, compare performance against plans, and all that sort of thing. That's just not our style.” “Berkshire's assets have been lovingly put together so as not to require continuing intelligence at headquarters.” (Damn Right!, pp. 231–232)
- **HIM** — Why it should not be easy. To a shareholder who complained that no great franchises like Coca Cola were left: “Why should it be easy to do something that, if done well two or three times, will make your family rich for life?” (Damn Right!, p. 234)
- **HIM** — A few simple big ideas. “People underrate the importance of a few simple big ideas. And I think to the extent Berkshire Hathaway is a didactic enterprise teaching the right systems of thought, the chief lesson is that a few big ideas really work. I think these filters of ours have worked pretty well-because they are so simple,” Also: “I knew it would do well, but not this well.” (Damn Right!, p. 236)
- **AUTHOR** — Staying in the game. “Munger tells investors to conduct their financial affairs so that no matter what crazy things happen in the markets, they can stay in the game. He cautioned that if you can't afford for your Berkshire Hathaway stock (or any stock, for that matter) to drop 50 percent, you probably shouldn't own it.” (Damn Right!, p. 251)
- **HIM** — Epigraph, Berkshire in the 1990s: competence. “The game of investing is one of making better predictions about the future than other people. How are you going to do that? One way is to limit your tries to areas of competence. If you try to predict the future of everything, you attempt too much. You're going to fail through lack of specialization.” (Damn Right!, p. 238)
- **REPORTED (Buffett) — Munger's influence on earning power.** “Charlie made me focus on the merits of a great business with tremendously growing earning power,” “but only when you can be sure of it-not like Texas Instruments or Polaroid, where the earning power was hypothetical.” (Damn Right!, p. 238)
- **HIM** — Size. “Size, at a certain point, gets to be an anchor, which drags you down,” “We always knew that it would. You get $10 billion in marketable securities. Show me unbelievable compound rates when people get $10 billion.” (Damn Right!, p. 242)
- **HIM** — Supercat risk. Wesco: “supercat reinsurance is not for the faint of heart. AUTHOR (p. 241): a big California earthquake loss as high as $600 million “would be ‘irritating,’ Munger said.” (Damn Right!, pp. 218, 241–242)
- **HIM** — Berkshire is not the model for everyone. “It would be a huge mistake to assume that Berkshire Hathaway is the right model for all America. - **HIM** — Being out of fashion. “I'm 76 years of age,” “I've been through a number of down periods. If you live a long time, you're going to be out of investment fashion some of the time.” (Damn Right!, p. 221)
- **HIM** — Berkshire's remaining strength. “Basically, we have a wonderful bunch of businesses. We have float that keeps increasing and a pretty good record of doing well in marketable securities. None of that has gone away.” (The .txt prints “hunch” for “bunch”.) (Damn Right!, p. 252)
- **HIM** — The Salomon lesson: respond fast. “People will be smart enough to realize this is the response we want” (the .txt prints “super prompteven”: a dash is missing) “even if it means cashiering some people who may not deserve it.” “It was a huge mistake for John Gutfruend not to go to the New York Fed when he saw that Mozer was in trouble,” “The Fed would not have called for Gutfreund's head. - **HIM** — Overconfidence in the smart (on LTCM). “The hedge fund known as Long Term Capital Management recently collapsed through overconfidence in its highly leveraged methods, despite IQs of its principals that must have averaged 160,” “Smart, hard-working people aren't exempted from professional disasters of overconfidence. - **HIM** — Epigraph, Daily Journal chapter: on humility. “In my whole life nobody has ever accused me of being humble. Although humility is a trait I much admire, I don't think I quite got my full share.” (The .txt prints “In mj, whole life”.) (Damn Right!, p. 271)
- **HIM** — Epigraph, Good Samaritan chapter. “The early Charlie Munger is a horrible career model for the young, because not enough was delivered to civilization in return for what was” (the .txt ends the line “u'rested.j'rom capitalism.”, garbled, and it is not quoted here). (Damn Right!, p. 287) Also in: HIM, Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 360 (Talk Six), where the .txt is clean: “Early Charlie Munger is a horrible career model for the young because not enough was delivered to civilization in return for what was wrested from capitalism.” (the editors quote it on p. 347 as “Early Charlie Munger is a horrible career model for the young.”).
- **HIM** — Atoning. On Keynes, who “atoned for his portfolio management ‘sins’ by making money for his college and serving his nation,” “I do my outside activities to atone, and Warren uses his investment success to be a great teacher.” (The .txt prints “1 do”.) AUTHOR (p. 289): he gets involved in community causes “to ease his guilt over having accumulated so much money, to clear his own conscience for having more wealth than he thinks he deserves.” (Damn Right!, p. 289)
- **HIM** — Public schools and vouchers. “I'm a product of the Omaha public schools.” “You don't just keep repeating what isn't working.” On vouchers: he would favor them “if the vouchers were given only to poor people. The better off people don't need them, since they already can afford choice and are exercising it.” “I think it's insane to keep going the way we are.” (Damn Right!, p. 297)
- **HIM** — The worst frame of mind, and the iron prescription. “You could argue that the very worst of all academic inanity is in the liberal arts departments of the great universities.” “The answer, of course, would be some sort of paranoid self-pity. I can't imagine a more destructive frame of mind.” “It's amazing how these pockets of irrationality creep into eminent places.” And: “the iron prescription: every time you think some person, or some unfairness is ruining your life, it is you who are ruining your life.” (Damn Right!, pp. 297–298)
- **HIM** — Uni-disciplinary education; big ideas; pilots. “Our education was far too uni-disciplinary,” “Many problems, by nature, cross many academic disciplines. Accordingly, using a uni-disciplinary attack on such problems is like playing a bridge hand by counting trumps and ignoring all else. This is bonkers, sort of like the Mad Hatter's tea party.” “You don't have to know everything,” “A few really big ideas carry most of the freight.” On training professionals like pilots: “They learn everything that is useful in piloting, and then, must retrain continuously so that they can cope promptly with practically any eventuality,” “Like any good algebraist,” “the pilot is made to think sometimes in a forward fashion and sometimes in reverse: and so he learns when to concentrate mostly on what he wants to happen and also when to concentrate mostly on avoiding what he does not want to happen.” AUTHOR (p. 298): he says mastery of psychology and accounting should be required of lawyers. Also in, HIM, Appendix D (“Multidisciplinary Skills: Educational Implications”): p. 346 “Our education was far too unidisciplinary.” (the .txt prints “honkers” for “bonkers” in the bridge-hand sentence, p. 346); pp. 347–348 the pilot system, including item 3 on forward and reverse thinking (the .txt prints “foreword”); p. 349 “the truly big ideas in each discipline, learned only in essence, carry most of the freight.” (Damn Right!, pp. 298–299)
- **HIM** — His own education. “I had taken one silly course in biology in high school” and “To this day, I have never taken any course, anywhere, in chemistry, economics, psychology, or business. But I early took elementary physics and math and paid enough attention to somehow assimilate the fundamental organizing ethos of hard science, which I thereafter pushed further and further into softer and softer subjects, using it as my organizing guide and filing system in a search for whatever multidisciplinary worldly wisdom it would be easy to get.” “I was productively led into psychology, where I had no plans to go, creating large advantages.” Also in, HIM (p. 354, Appendix D): the full passage, including “I came to Harvard Law School very poorly educated, with desultory work habits and no college degree.” and “I was productively led into psychology, where I had no plans to go, creating large advantages that deserve a story on another day.” (Damn Right!, p. 299) Also in: HIM, Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 341–342 (Talk Five): the same passage (“I came to Harvard Law School very poorly educated, with desultory work habits and no college degree.” p. 341; “To this day, I have never taken any course, anywhere, in chemistry, economics, psychology, or business.” p. 341), with this addition on p. 342: “What I found, in my extended attempts to complete by informal means my stunted education, was that, plugging along with only ordinary will but with the fundamental organizing ethos as my guide, my ability to serve everything I loved was enhanced far beyond my deserts.” and “sometimes making me like the only one without the blindfold in a high-stake game of ‘pin the tail on the donkey.’” Of the whole: “Thus, my life became a sort of accidental educational experiment” (p. 341).
- **AUTHOR** — A liberal arts college with no majors. At the Cass Lake breakfast, “Charlie, Sr. is saying that he'd like to create a true liberal arts college in which students have no major and few elective courses.” Set curriculum; “No specialization would be allowed until graduate school.” “The problem with many of today's young people, Munger argues to his unconvinced offspring, is that they specialize too early and never learn some subjects they can't live well without.” (Damn Right!, p. 295)
- **AUTHOR (what he often quotes)** — Not fooling yourself. “Charlie Munger often quotes the late Nobel laureate physicist Richard Feynman, who said the first rule is to not fool yourself, and you are the easiest person to fool. Munger can be merciless if he believes he has caught someone in an act of silly self-deception.” The words are the author's. (The .txt's drop-cap garbles the first word, “nARLIE MUNGER OFTEN QUOTES THE late…”.) (Damn Right!, p. 302) Also in: HIM, Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 352–353 (Talk Six): Munger himself quotes Feynman, “The first principle is that you must not fool yourself, and you're the easiest person to fool.” (see the Talk Six entry in RULES).
- **HIM** — Berkshire as a classroom. Asked by a student whether he and Buffett were fulfilling their responsibility to share their wisdom: “Sure: Look at Berkshire Hathaway. I call it the ultimate didactic exercise. Warren's never going to spend any money. He's going to give it back to society. He's just building a platform so people will listen to his notions. Needless to say, they are very good notions. And the platform's not so bad either. But you could argue that Warren and I are academics in our own way.” (The .txt prints “had” for “bad”.) (Damn Right!, p. 305)
- **AUTHOR (his lessons and method, not verbatim)** — “Some of his lessons are quite practical and apply to life in general and to financial matters in particular: To those whom much is given, much is expected. Always live below your financial means so that you will have money to invest. Invest in such a way so as to avoid the possibility of falling into a negative position-primarily, by limiting the amount of debt you use.” And: “His method is to present major themes, such as advising listeners to identify the few big ideas that make a difference and try to live by them. Although he talks about what those ideas might be, he does not offer a succinct formula, or a simple list of directions”. (Damn Right!, pp. 305–306)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — Why, why, why. HIM: “If you want to get smart,” “the question you have to keep asking is ‘why, why, why?’ And you have to relate the answers to a structure of deep theory. You've got to know the main theories. And it's mildly laborious, but its also a lot of fun.” (The .txt prints “its also”.) AUTHOR: “From physicists, Munger has learned to solve a problem by seeking the simplest, most direct answer. The easiest way invariably is the best way. From mathematicians Munger learned to turn problems upside down or to look at them backward-invert, always invert.” (Damn Right!, p. 306)
- **AUTHOR + HIM** — Inversion at a commencement. AUTHOR (p. 306): at the Harvard School commencement in 1986, his youngest son Philip's graduating class, Munger “told the students that his prescriptions for life were based on a Johnny Carson speech explaining the things a person should do to ensure a miserable life,” which included “ingesting chemicals in an effort to alter mood or perception, and allowing oneself to indulge in envy and to wallow in resentment.” AUTHOR: “Then Munger added four practices that he believes also will help guarantee failure. Be unreliable; learn everything from your own experience rather than learning from others; give up trying after your first, second, or third reverse of fortune; and finally, give in to fuzzy thinking.” HIM, the story that goes with the last (the .txt lead-in is garbled): “a story they told me when I was very young about a rustic who said, ‘I wish I knew where I was going to die, and then I'd never go there.’” (Same rustic, HIM, p. 356.) (Damn Right!, p. 306) Also in: Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 150–161 (HIM: the speech itself, entries in RULES, SIGNALS and TEMPERAMENT).
- **HIM** — Know your own nature. AUTHOR: “First of all, you have to understand your own nature, said Munger.” HIM: “Each person has to play the game given his own marginal utility considerations and in a way that takes into account his own psychology. If losses are going to make you miserable-and some losses are inevitable-you might be wise to utilize a very conservative pattern of investment and saving all your life. So you have to adapt your strategy to your own nature and your own talents. I don't think there's a one-size-fits-all investment strategy that I can give you.” (Damn Right!, pp. 306–307)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — Reading. HIM: “I think both Warren and I learn more from the great business magazines than we do anywhere else,” “It's such an easy, shorthand way of getting a vast variety of business experience just to riffle through issue after issue covering a great variety of businesses. And if you get into the mental habit of relating what you're reading to the basic structure of the underlying ideas being demonstrated, you gradually accumulate some wisdom about investing. I don't think you can get to be a really good investor over a broad range without doing a massive amount of reading. I don't think any one book will do it for you.” AUTHOR: “Each year at the Berkshire annual meeting Munger recommends a wide range of reading material. These include Value Line charts, Robert B. Cialdini's book Influence on how people are persuaded to buy products and take other actions, and recently, Robert Hagstrom's book, The Warren Buffett Portfolio: Mastering the Power of the Focus Investment Strategy.” (Damn Right!, p. 307)
- **HIM** — Reading with a question. “you have to have some idea of why you're looking for the information. Don't read annual reports the way Francis Bacon said you do science-which, by the way, is not the way you do science-where you just collect endless data and then only later do you try to make sense of it. You have to start with some ideas about reality. And then you have to look to see whether what you're seeing fits in with proven basic concepts.” (The .txt prints “sciencewhich”, hyphen missing.) (Damn Right!, p. 307)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — A business with fabulous results. HIM: “Frequently, you'll look at a business having fabulous results. And the question is, ‘How long can this continue?’ Well, there's only one way I know to answer that. And that's to think about why the results are occurring now-and then to figure out the forces that could cause those results to stop occurring.” AUTHOR: “This is the method of thinking that helps Munger and Buffett spot a company that has a franchise on a certain product, a so-called ‘moat’ around its business.” (Damn Right!, p. 307)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — Wrigley's, and the price of an obvious franchise. HIM: “It's such a huge advantage to be by far the best-known gum company in the world. Just think of how hard it would be to replace that image. If you know you like Wrigley's Gum and you see it there for two bits, are you really going to reach for Glotz's Gum because it's 20 cents and put something you don't know in your mouth? It's not worth it for you to think about buying an alternative gum. So it's easy to understand why Wrigley's Gum has such a huge advantage.” On price, HIM: “The trouble with the Wrigley Gum-type investments is that everybody can see that they're wonderful businesses. So you look at it and you think, ‘My God! The thing's at eight times book value or something. And everything else is at three times book value.’ So you think, ‘I know it's wonderful, but is it wonderful enough to justify that big a premium?’” AUTHOR: “The ability to answer such questions explains why some people are successful investors and others are not.” HIM: “On the other hand, if it weren't a little difficult, everybody would be rich,” (Damn Right!, p. 308) Also in: HIM, The Complete Investor, p. 17 (book's label: Damn Right, 2000).
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — Corporate finance, modern portfolio theory and luck. AUTHOR: he is “perpetually miffed at investors and academics who promote the harsh form of the efficient market theory of investing”. HIM: “If you think psychology is badly taught in America, you should look at corporate finance. Modern portfolio theory? It's demented!” On the theorist who explained Buffett as luck: “this theorist finally got all the way up to six sigmas-six standard deviations-of luck. But then, people started laughing at him because six sigmas of luck is a lot. So what did he do? He changed his theory. Now, he explains, Warren has six or seven sigmas of skill.” On Michael Lewis: “He's got the idea that Warren's success for 40 years is because he flipped coins for 40 years and it has come up heads 40 times. All I can say is, if he believes that, I've got a bridge I'd like to sell him.” Also in (HIM, Appendix D, p. 350): “importation into many law and business schools of hard-form efficient-market theory by misguided would-be experts in corporate finance, one of whom kept explaining Berkshire Hathaway's investing success by adding standard deviations of luck until, at six standard deviations, he encountered enough derision to force a change in explanation.” (Damn Right!, pp. 309, 350) Also in: HIM, Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 334–335 (Talk Five): the same passage on “hard-form, efficient-market theory by misguided would-be experts in corporate finance, one of whom kept explaining Berkshire Hathaway's investing success by adding standard deviations of luck until, at six standard deviations, he encountered enough derision to force a change in explanation.”
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — Volatility and Beta. AUTHOR: “they ignore the popular financial indicator called Beta, which measures a stock's volatility in relation to the overall market.” HIM: “This great emphasis on volatility in corporate finance we regard as nonsense ... ” “Let me put it this way: as long as the odds are in our favor and we're not risking the whole company on one throw of the dice or anything close to it, we don't mind volatility in results. What we want are the favorable odds. We figure the volatility over time will take care of itself at Berkshire.” (Damn Right!, pp. 309–310)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — Accounting for stock options. AUTHOR: “Both Munger and Buffett are indignant over the way the regulators allow stock options to be counted on the books so that they don't show up as an expense to the company. They mention the problem at nearly every annual meeting.” HIM: “It's fundamentally wrong not to have rational, honest accounting in big American corporations,” “And it's very important not to let little corruptions start because they become big corruptions-and then you have vested interests that fight to perpetuate them. Accounting for stock options in America is corrupt, and it's not a good idea to have corrupt accounting.” (Damn Right!, p. 310) Also in: Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 388 (the editors' box, with the words attributed to Charlie, “Charlie remains skeptical”): “The right way to behave is to never let improper accounting start.” and p. 400 (HIM, Talk Eight Revisited, 2006); see the Talk Eight entries in RULES.
- **AUTHOR + HIM** — Unsolicited tender offers (where he differs from Buffett). AUTHOR: “Buffett and Munger agree on most things, but they have a different opinion about who should be the decision maker when an unsolicited tender offer is made to a corporation. Buffett says his heart is with the shareholders, but Munger says there is a social interest in some cases, making it okay to make laws to govern such transactions.” HIM: “I totally agree that for the ordinary little family business that owns a theater, the shareholders ought to decide whether the theater is sold. But once you get into great big social institutions, that given certain circumstances, will go together in waves of acquisitions into huge agglomerations, that bothers me. So, I think that it's appropriate to have laws that prevent it,” (Damn Right!, p. 310)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — Finance taught from easy cases (National Cash Register). AUTHOR (p. 310): “If he were teaching finance, Munger said he would use the histories of 100 or so companies that did something right or something wrong.” HIM: “Finance properly taught should be taught from cases where the investment decisions are easy,” “And the one that I always cite is the early history of the National Cash Register Company. It was created by a very intelligent man who bought all the patents, had the best sales force, and the best production plants. He was a very intelligent man and a fanatic, all of whose passions were dedicated to the cash register business. And of course, the invention of the cash register was a godsend to retailing. You might even say that cash registers were the pharmaceuticals industry of a former age. If you read an early annual report when Patterson was the CEO of National Cash Register, an idiot could tell that here was a talented fanatic-very favorably located. Therefore, the investment decision was easy.” The material on Patterson that follows (p. 311: the Dayton flood, T.J. Watson, “Shrouds don't have pockets”) is the author's, not Munger's. (Damn Right!, pp. 310–311)
- **AUTHOR + HIM** — Building companies to last. AUTHOR (p. 311): “Though few companies last forever, all of them should be built to last a long time, says Munger. The approach to corporate control should be thought of as ‘financial engineering.’ Just as bridges and airplanes are constructed with a series of back-up systems and redundancies to meet extreme stresses, so too should corporations be built to withstand the pressures from competition, recessions, oil shocks, or other calamities. Excess leverage, or debt, makes the corporation especially vulnerable to such storms.” HIM (p. 312): “It is a crime in America,” “to build a weak bridge. How much nobler is it to build a weak company?” (Damn Right!, pp. 311–312)
- **HIM + AUTHOR + REPORTED (Bogle)** — Mutual funds and foundations. HIM: “It is, to me, just amazing what's happened in the mutual fund business,” “It just grows and grows and grows. And they get these fees just for maintaining shares in place-12-B-1 fees or whatever they call them. I am not charmed with the mechanics of that business.” HIM, 1998, to foundation executives in Santa Monica, on Yale investing its endowment in the equivalent of a fund of mutual funds: “This is an amazing development. Few would have predicted that, long after Cornfeld's fall into disgrace, major universities would be leading foundations into Cornfeld's system.” AUTHOR: he was “attacking the practice of hiring consultants to hire money managers who in turn select mutual funds operated by other money managers. At each step, there is a commission to be paid, which skims off the money that can be used in the work of the charity.” REPORTED (John Bogle, on Munger's point): “Mr. Munger goes on to point out,” “the devastating impact of the cost of all this complexity on the return of foundations and endowments in a stock market with lower returns. Market returns-5 percent; total cost, 3 percent; net return, 2 percent.” (Bogle's wording, not Munger's.) (Damn Right!, p. 312) Also in: HIM, Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 348 (Talk Six): “Few would have predicted that long after Cornfeld's fall into disgrace, major universities would be leading foundations into Cornfeld's system.” (the Almanack's wording has no comma after “that”); and “Some foundations, following the lead of institutions like Yale, have tried to become much better versions of Bernie Cornfeld ‘fund of funds.’”
- **AUTHOR + HIM** — What foundations should do instead. AUTHOR (pp. 312–313): his recommendation “was simple: Save yourself a lot of time, money, and worry. Just put your endowments into index funds. Alternately, the foundations could follow Berkshire's lead and simply buy high quality stocks (if they are not highly overvalued in price) and hold on for the long term.” HIM (p. 313): “In the United States, a person or institution with almost all wealth invested, long term, in just three fine domestic corporations, is securely rich,” “And why should such an owner care if at any time most other investors are faring somewhat better or worse. And particularly so when he rationally believes, like Berkshire, that his long-term results will be superior by reason of his lower costs, required emphasis on long-term effects, and concentration in his most preferred choices.” AUTHOR: he “went so far as to suggest that investors could have 90 percent of their wealth in a single company, if it was the right company.” HIM: “Indeed, I hope the Mungers follow roughly this course. And I note that the Woodruff Foundations have, so far, proven extremely wise to retain an approximately 90 percent concentration in the founder's Coca Cola stock. It would be interesting to calculate just how all American foundations would have fared if they had never sold a share of founder's stock. Very many, I think, would now be much better off.” (The .txt prints “Mongers” for “Mungers”.) (Damn Right!, pp. 312–313) Also in: HIM, Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 356–358 (Talk Six): “I go even further. I think it can be a rational choice, in some situations, for a family or a foundation to remain ninety percent concentrated in one equity. Indeed, I hope the Mungers follow roughly this course.” (p. 357; the Almanack's .txt prints “Mungers” correctly) and the Woodruff and “never sold a share” passage (pp. 357–358); see the Talk Six entries in RULES.
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — Index funds, and when not. AUTHOR: “Munger asserted that the vast majority of professionally managed money, after taking into account the impact of fees and transaction costs would be better off in index funds,” HIM: “Does that mean you should be in an index fund? Well, that depends on whether or not you can invest money way better than average or you can find someone who almost surely will invest money way better than average. And those are the questions that make life interesting.” AUTHOR: “If everyone put their money in index funds, Munger conceded, the prices of indexed stocks would be forced beyond intrinsic value, and the process would become meaningless.” (Damn Right!, p. 313) Also in: HIM, Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 354–355 (Talk Six): his own words on indexing, recorded in RULES.
- **HIM** — Examining blunders. “If I were ordaining rules for running boards of directors, I'd require that three hours be spent examining stupid blunders including quantification of effects considering opportunity costs.” (AUTHOR's lead-in: “Munger admits that he and Buffett have made many investment errors.”) (Damn Right!, p. 316)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — American business, on balance. AUTHOR: “Despite his criticism and his insistence that there are corrupt practices afoot in the business world, Munger feels that overall, America and its businesses usually are honorable.” HIM: “I would not agree that things are generally going to hell,” “Sure we've got some pockets of social pathology in our big cities, and we've got pockets of social pathology in the high reaches of business, but averaged out, I would say it's pretty good. If you take engineering integrity in products, when was the last time your automatic transmission went out? We've learned to do a lot of things with enormous reliability. You take a company like Boeing and all the hours that the airplanes stay in the air and the three back up systems behind every system that navigates that airplane. I would argue that there is a lot to admire in American business and a lot that's done right, And that these oldfashioned values, averaged out, are winning, not losing. It's too bad we have all that social pathology-but we have it in the high reaches of politics, why shouldn't we have it in the high reaches of business.” (The .txt prints “hut” for “but”.) (Damn Right!, pp. 316–317)
- **HIM** — Failure and headwinds. To the employees at See's Candy on its 75th anniversary: “It's ... necessary to accommodate a lot of failure, and because no matter how able you are, you're going to have headwinds and troubles,” “The Sees who created this business had failed at least once, and had seriously failed. But if a person just keeps going on the theory that life is full of vicissitudes and just does the right thinking and follows the right values it should work out well in the end. So I would say, don't be discouraged by a few reverses.” (Damn Right!, p. 321)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — Ideas and opportunity. AUTHOR: “By heeding basic principles and being alert for opportunities, Munger matte the leap from a respectably successful lawyer to an individual investor who is known internationally for his expertise.” (The .txt prints “matte” for “made”.) HIM: “It was a number of ideas, not just one. A lot of ideas. In the nature of things, really wonderful ideas are virtually sure to win. You can be sure that if you master the wonderful concepts you're going to get opportunities if you look for them.... But you won't get an unlimited number of good ideas-so when they come along, seize them.” (Compare “seize it when it does come”, p. 63.) (Damn Right!, p. 321)
- **AUTHOR (what he has said, not his words)** — Building wealth. “Munger has said that accumulating the first $100,000 from a standing start, with no seed money, is the most difficult part of building wealth. Making the first million was the next big hurdle. To do that a person must consistently underspend his income. Getting wealthy, he explains, is like rolling a snowball. It helps to start on the top of a long hill-start early and try to roll that snowball for a very long time. It helps to live a long life.” (Damn Right!, pp. 321–322)
- **HIM** — Incentive-caused bias and “man with a hammer” (Appendix D, “Multidisciplinary Skills: Educational Implications”; the notes, p. 370, date this address to the fiftieth reunion of the Harvard Law School Class of 1948 as April 4, 1999). Quoting a Shaw character: “In the last analysis, every profession is a conspiracy against the laity.” Then: “But Shaw plainly understates the problem in implying that a conscious, self-interested malevolence is the main culprit. More important, there are frequent terrible effects in professionals from intertwined subconscious mental tendencies, two of which are exceptionally prone to cause trouble:” “1. Incentive-caused bias, a natural cognitive drift toward the conclusion that what is good for the professional is good for the client and the wider civilization; and” “2. ‘Man with a hammer’ tendency, with the name taken from the proverb: ‘to a man with only a hammer, every problem tends to look pretty much like a nail.’” (Damn Right!, pp. 345–346) Also in: HIM, Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 324–325 (Talk Five). The Almanack gives the occasion as “Fiftieth Reunion of Harvard Law School Class of 1948 / April 24,1998” (p. 323); the notes in Damn Right! (p. 370) date it April 4, 1999.
- **HIM** — Multiple tools; A plus B. “One partial cure for man-with-a-hammer tendency is obvious: if a man has a vast set of skills over multiple disciplines, he, by definition, carries multiple tools and therefore will limit bad cognitive effects from ‘man with a hammer’ tendency. Moreover, when he is multidisciplinary enough to absorb from practical psychology the idea that all his life he must fight bad effects from both the tendencies I mentioned, both within himself and from others, he has taken a constructive step on the road to worldly wisdom.” “If ‘A’ is narrow professional doctrine and ‘B’ consists of the big, extra-useful concepts from other disciplines, then, clearly, the professional possessing ‘A’ plus ‘B’ will usually be better off than the poor possessor of ‘A’ alone. How could it be otherwise?” (The .txt prints “had” for “bad”.) (Damn Right!, p. 346) Also in: HIM, Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 325–326 (Talk Five). The Almanack adds on p. 326: “And thus, the only rational excuse for not acquiring more ‘B’ is that it is not practical to do so, given the man's need for ‘A’ and the other urgent demands in his life.”
- **HIM** — Balkanized academia. “Even in our youth, some of the best professors were horrified by bad effects from balkanization of academia into insular, turf-protecting enclaves wherein notions were maintained by leaps of faith plus exclusion of non-believers. Alfred North Whitehead, for one, long ago sounded an alarm in strong language when he spoke of ‘the fatal unconnectedness of academic disciplines.’” (The .txt prints “had” for “bad”.) (Damn Right!, pp. 346–347) Also in: HIM, Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 326–327 (Talk Five), with Whitehead's “fatal un connectedness” (the .txt splits the word) and, on p. 327: “Modern academia now gives more multidisciplinarity than we received and is plainly right to do so.”
- **HIM** — The pilot's training as the model. “To find the best educational narrowscale model, we have to look not at unthreatened schools of education and the like, too much driven by our two counterproductive psychological tendencies and other bad influences, but, instead, where incentives for effective education are strongest and results are most closely measured. This leads us to a logical place: the hugely successful education now mandatory for airline pilots. (Yes, I am suggesting today that mighty Harvard would do better if it thought more about pilot training.)” The six elements, in part: “1. His formal education is wide enough to cover practically everything useful in piloting.” “2. His knowledge of practically everything needed by pilots is not taught just well enough to enable him to pass one test or two; instead, all his knowledge is raised to practice-based fluency, even in handling two or three intertwined hazards at once.” “4. His training time is allocated among subjects so as to minimize damage from his later malfunctions; and so what is most important in his performance gets the most training coverage and is raised to the highest fluency levels.” “5. ‘Check list’ routines are always mandatory for him.” “6. Even after original training, he is forced into a special knowledge-maintenance routine: regular use of the aircraft simulator to prevent atrophy through long disuse of skills needed to cope with rare and important problems.” (Item 3, forward and reverse thinking, is quoted in the earlier entry on pp. 298–299.) “The need for this clearly correct six-element system, with its large demands in a narrowscale field where stakes are high, is rooted in the deep structure of the human mind.” (The .txt prints “had” for “bad”.) (Damn Right!, pp. 347–348) Also in: HIM, Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 328–330 (Talk Five); see also the entry on what best-form education must contain in RULES.
- **HIM** — What makes broad skill feasible. “First, the concept of ‘all needed skills’ lets us recognize that we don't have to raise everyone's skill in celestial mechanics to that of Laplace and also ask everyone to achieve a similar skill level in all other knowledge. Instead, it turns out that the truly big ideas in each discipline, learned only in essence, carry most of the freight. And they are not so numerous, nor are their interactions so complex, that a large and multidisciplinary understanding is impossible for many, given large amounts of talent and time.” “Third, thinking by inversion and through use of ‘check lists’ is easily learned-in broadscale life as in piloting.” And: “we can believe in the attainability of broad multidisciplinary skill for the same reason the fellow from Arkansas gave for his belief in baptism: ‘I've seen it done.’” (Damn Right!, pp. 348–349) Also in: HIM, Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 331–332 (Talk Five).
- **HIM** — “Take what you wish” and its defects. “However, an extremely permissive practice like ‘take what you wish’ was not destined to have 100 percent-admirable results in soft science.” Among its worst outcomes: “(1) assimilation of Freudianism in some literature departments, (2) importation into many places of extremist political ideologies of the left or right that had, for their possessors, made regain of objectivity almost as unlikely as regain of virginity”. (Item 3 is the efficient-market passage quoted on p. 350 above.) Defects (p. 351): “takings from more fundamental disciplines were often done without attribution, sometimes under new names, with little attention given to rank in a fundamentalness order for absorbed concepts. Such practices (1) act like a lousy filing system that must impair successful use and synthesis of absorbed knowledge and (2) do not maximize in soft-science the equivalent of Linus Pauling's systematic mining of physics to improve chemistry. There must be a better way.” (Damn Right!, pp. 350–351) Also in: HIM, Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 332–335 (Talk Five). The Almanack names the practice before the defects: “a different sort of correction has usually worked best, namely augmentation, or ‘take what you wish’ practice that encourages any discipline to simply assimilate whatever it chooses from other disciplines.” (pp. 332–333)
