This blessing came to both Buffett and myself in large measure, sometimes from the same persons and ideas.” (p. 519) “For instance, it is obviously desirable to attract a lot of lovable, admirable people into the teaching profession.” (p. 519) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 518–519) Also in: HIM, The Complete Investor, p. 52 (book's label: Harvard University, 1995).
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Disliking/Hating: the same distortion, reversed. “Disliking/Hating Tendency also acts as a conditioning device that makes the disliker hater tend to (1) ignore virtues in the object of dislike, (2) dislike people, products, and actions merely associated with the object of his dislike, and (3) distort other facts to facilitate hatred.” (p. 521) “Distortion of that kind is often so extreme that miscognition is shockingly large.” “When the World Trade Center was destroyed, many Pakistanis immediately concluded that the Hindus did it, while many Muslims concluded that the Jews did it.” (p. 521) “clever political arrangements of man “channel” the hatreds and dislikings of individuals and groups into nonlethal patterns including elections.” (p. 520) “Politics is the art of marshalling hatreds.” (p. 520, the English maxim as he gives it) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 520–521)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Buffett on suing relatives. “At the family level, we often see one sibling hate his other siblings and litigate with them endlessly if he can afford it. Indeed, a wag named Buffett has repeatedly explained to me that “a major difference between rich and poor people is that the rich people can spend their lives suing their relatives.”” (p. 521) (Munger quotes Buffett; the line is Buffett's, retold by Munger.) “My father's law practice in Omaha was full of such intrafamily hatreds.” (p. 521) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 521)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Doubt-Avoidance, and the “mask” of objectivity. “The brain of man is programmed with a tendency to quickly remove doubt by reaching some decision.” (p. 522) “So pronounced is the tendency in man to quickly remove doubt by reaching some decision that behavior to counter the tendency is required from judges and jurors. Here, delay before decision making is forced.” (p. 522) “what usually triggers Doubt-Avoidance Tendency is some combination of (l) puzzlement and (Z) stress.” (p. 523; “(l)” and “(Z)” as printed) “Thus, the natural state of most men is in some form of religion. And this is what we observe.” (p. 523) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 522–523)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Habit is destiny; prevent it rather than change it. “Few people can list a lot of bad habits that they have eliminated, and some people cannot identify even one of these. Instead, practically everyone has a great many bad habits he has long maintained despite their being known as bad.” (p. 524) “it is not too much in many cases to appraise early-formed habits as destiny.” (p. 524) Marley: “I wear the chains I forged in life,” “chains of habit that were too light to be felt before they became too strong to be broken.” (p. 524) “The rare life that is wisely lived has in it many good habits maintained and many bad habits avoided or cured.” (p. 524) Franklin: “An ounce of prevention is worth a pound of cure.” “Inconsistency-Avoidance Tendency makes it much easier to prevent a habit than to change it.” (p. 524) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 524)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — First-conclusion bias, and how courts guard against it. “It is easy to see that a quickly reached conclusion, triggered by Doubt-Avoidance Tendency, when combined with a tendency to resist any change in that conclusion, will natUrally cause a lot of errors in cognition for modern man.” (p. 525; “natUrally” as printed) “before making decisions, judges and juries are required to hear long and skillful presentations of evidence and argument from the side they will not naturally favor, given their ideas in place.” (p. 526) “Similarly, other modern decision makers will often force groups to consider skillful counter arguments before making decisions.” (p. 526) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 525–526)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Keynes on old ideas, and the egg. “it was not the intrinsic difficulty of new ideas that prevented their acceptance. Instead, the new ideas were not accepted because they were inconsistent with old ideas in place.” (p. 526) “the human mind works a lot like the human egg. When one sperm gets into a human egg, there's an automatic shut-off device that bars any other sperm from getting in. The human mind tends strongly toward the same sort of result.” (pp. 526–527) “And so, people tend to accumulate large mental holdings of fixed conclusions and attitudes that are not often reexamined or changed, even though there is plenty of good evidence that they are wrong.” (p. 527) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 526–527)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Planck and Einstein on holding old errors. “Planck is famous not only for his science but also for saying that even in physics the radically new ideas are seldom really accepted by the old guard. Instead, said Planck, the progress is made by a new generation that comes along, less brain-blocked by its previous conclusions.” (p. 527) “At his peak, Einstein was a great destroyer of his own ideas, but an older Einstein never accepted the full implications of quantum mechanics.” (pp. 527–528) (Planck also: Talk Nine, pp. 432–433; Talk Ten, Planck knowledge, pp. 475–476.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 527–528)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Darwin's antidote to first-conclusion bias. “He trained himself, early, t<t intensively consider any evidence tending to disconfirm any hypothesis of his, more so if he thought his hypothesis was a particularly good one.” (p. 528; “t<t” as printed) “The opposite of what Darwin did is now called confirmation bias, a term of opprobrium.” (p. 528) “Darwin's practice came from his acute recognition of man's natural cognitive faults arising from Inconsistency-Avoidance Tendency.” (p. 528) “He provides a great example of psychological insight correctly used to advance some of the finest mental work ever done.” (p. 528) (Earlier on Darwin: Harvard School commencement, 1986, pp. 158–159; Talk Ten, p. 473.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 528)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Sacrifice deepens commitment: initiation, Franklin's borrowed book, brainwashing. “a person making big sacrifices in the course of assuming a new identity will intensify his devotion to the new identity. After all, it would be quite inconsistent behavior to make a large sacrifice for something that was no good.” (pp. 528–529) Franklin: “Franklin would often maneuver that man into doing Franklin some unimportant favor like lending Franklin a book. Thereafter the man would admire and trust Franklin more because a non admitted and non trusted Franklin would be inconsistent with the appraisal implicit in lending Franklin the book.” (p. 529) “Small step by small step, the technique often worked better than torture in altering prisoner cognition in favor of Chinese captors.” (p. 530) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 528–530)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Hurting someone makes you hate him; stop prisoner abuse at once. “When one is maneuvered into deliberately hurting some other person, one will tend to disapprove or even hate that person.” “A man never forgets where he has buried the hatchet.” (p. 530) “an intense, continuous effort should be made (1) to prevent prisoner abuse from starting and (2) to stop it instantly when it starts because it will grow by feeding on itself, like a cluster of infectious disease.” (pp. 530–531) “More psychological acuity on this subject, aided by more insightful teaching, would probably improve the overall effectiveness of the U.S. Army.” (p. 531) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 530–531)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Pretending changes you; teaching changes you; indoctrinating your own students harms them. “And many a hypocrite is improved by his pretensions of virtue. And many a judge and juror, while pretending objectivity, is gaining objectivity.” (p. 531) “see one, do one, and then teach one” (p. 531) “modern education often does much damage when young students are taught dubious political notions and then enthusiastically push these notions on the rest of us.” (p. 532) “It is important not to thus put one's brain in chains before one has come anywhere near his full potentiality as a rational person.” (p. 532) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 531–532)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Curiosity: Athens against Rome. “Athens (including its colony, Alexandria) developed much math and science our of pure curiosity while the Romans made almost no contribution to either math or science.” (pp. 532–533; “our” as printed) “They instead concentrated their attention on the “practical” engineering of mines, roads, aqueducts, etc.” (p. 533) “The curious are also provided with much fun and wisdom long after formal education has ended.” (p. 533) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 532–533)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Kantian Fairness, and slavery. “it is not too much to say that modern acculturated man displays, and expects from others, a lot of fairness as thus defined by Kant.” (p. 533) “Also, strangers often voluntarily share equally in unexpected, unearned good and bad fortune. And, as an obverse consequence of such “fair-sharing” conduct, much reactive hostility occurs when fair sharing is expected yet not provided.” (p. 534) “My guess is that Kantian Fairness Tendency was a major contributor to this result.” (p. 534, on the abolition of slavery) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 533–534)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Envy/Jealousy, and Buffett's line. “Envy/jealousy is extreme in myth, religion, and literature wherein, in account after account, it triggers hatred and injury.” (p. 535) “And, because this is roughly right, one would expect a vast coverage of envy/jealousy in psychology textbooks. But no such vast coverage existed when I read my three textbooks. Indeed, the very words “envy” and “jealousy” were often absent from indexes.” (p. 536) Buffett: “As I have shared the observation of life with Warren Buffett over decades, I have heard him wisely say on several occasions: “It is not greed that drives the world, but envy.”” (p. 536; Munger's report of Buffett's words, which he endorses as “roughly right”) Compensation: “And in modern investment banks, law firms, etc., the envy/jealousy effects are usually more extreme than they are in university faculties.” (p. 535) “Many big law firms, fearing disorder from envy/jealousy, have long treated all senior partners alike in compensation, no matter how different their contributions to firm welfare.” (pp. 535–536) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 535–536)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — The taboo on saying “envy.” “When did any of you last engage in any large group discussion of some issue wherein adult envy/jealousy was identified as the cause of someone's argument? There seems to be a general taboo against any such claim.” (p. 536) “My guess is that people widely and generally sense that labeling some position as driven by envy/ jealousy will be regarded as extremely insulting to the position taker, possibly more so when the diagnosis is correct than when it is wrong.” (pp. 536–537) “But should this general taboo extend to psychology texts when it creates such a large gap in the correct, psychological explanation of what is widespread and important? My answer is no.” (p. 537) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 536–537)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Ants and Genghis Khan: nature does not reward turning the other cheek. “Indeed, E. O. Wilson once waggishly suggested that if ants were suddenly to get atom bombs, all ants would be dead within eighteen hours.” (p. 538) “What both human and ant history suggest is (1) that nature has no general algorithm making intraspecies, turn-the other-cheek behavior a booster of species survival, (2) that it is not clear that a country would have good prospects were it to abandon all reciprocate disfavor tendency directed at outsiders, and (3) if turn-the-other-cheek behavior is a good idea for a country as it deals with outsiders, man's culture is going to have to do a lot of heavy lifting because his genes won't be of much help.” (p. 538) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 538)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Defer your reaction; Tom Murphy. “The standard antidote to one's overactive hostility is to train oneself to defer reaction.” (p. 539) “As my smart friend Tom Murphy so frequently says, “You can always tell the man off tomorrow if it is such a good idea.”” (p. 539; the line is Murphy's, which Munger adopts as the antidote) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 539)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Reciprocation in commerce and marriage. “It is obvious that commercial trade, a fundamental cause of modern prosperity. is enormously facilitated by man's innate tendency to reciprocate favors.” (pp. 539–540) “Daily interchange in marriage is also assisted by Reciprocation Tendency, without which marriage would lose much of its allure.” (p. 540) “Like other psychological tendencies, and also man's ability to turn somersaults, reciprocate-favor tendency operates to a very considerable degree at a subconscious level.” (p. 540) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 539–540)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — The car salesman's coffee, and the purchasing agent. “when an automobile salesman graciously steers you into a comfortable place to sit and gives you a cup of coffee, you are very likely being tricked, by this small courtesy alone, into parting with an extra five hundred dollars.” (pp. 540–541) “But suppose you are the purchasing agent of someone else-a rich employer, for instance. Now the minor favor you receive from the salesman is less opposed by the threat of extra cost to you because someone else is paying the extra cost. Under such circumstances, the salesman is often able to maximize his advantage, particularly when government is the purchaser.” (p. 541) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 540–541)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Sam Walton's rule on favors from vendors. “Wise employers, therefore, try to oppose reciprocate-favor tendencies of employees engaged in purchasing. The simplest antidote works best: Don't let them accept any favors from vendors.” (p. 541) “Sam Walton agreed with this idea of absolute prohibition. He wouldn't let purchasing agents accept so much as a hot dog from a vendor.” (p. 541) “Given the subconscious level at which much Reciprocation Tendency operates, this policy of Walton's was profoundly correct. If I controlled the Defense Department, its policies would mimic Walton's.” (pp. 541–542) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 541–542)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Cialdini's zoo experiment, and Watergate. “one person in six out of a large sample actually agreed to do this.” (the zoo trip, p. 542) “This ridiculous request got him a one hundred percent rejection rate.” “This raised Cialdini's former acceptance rate of l/6 to 1/2, a tripling.” (p. 542; “l/6” as printed) “What Cialdini's “compliance practitioners” had done was make a small concession, which was reciprocated by a small concession from the other side.” (pp. 542–543) Watergate: “Cialdini believes that subconscious Reciprocation Tendency thus became one important cause of the resignation of a United States president in the Watergate debacle, and so do I.” (p. 544) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 542–544)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Reciprocation, on balance. “Overall, both inside and outside religions, it seems clear to me that Reciprocation Tendency's constructive contributions to man far outweigh its destructive effects.” (pp. 544–545) “And the very best part of human life probably lies in relationships of affection wherein parties are more interested in pleasing than being pleased-a not uncommon outcome in display of reciprocate favor tendency.” (p. 545) On guilt: “And if you, like me and this priest, believe that, averaged out, feelings of guilt do more good than harm, you may join in my special gratitude for reciprocate-favor tendency, no matter how unpleasant you find feelings of guilt.” (p. 546) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 544–546)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Mere association: price as quality, and who is selling it. “some seller of an ordinary industrial product will often change his product's trade dress and raise its price significantly hoping that quality-seeking buyers will be tricked into becoming purchasers by mere association of his product and its high price.” (p. 547) “This industrial practice frequently is effective in driving up sales and even more so in driving up profits.” (p. 547) “You won't see Coke advertised alongside some account of the death of a child. Instead. Coke ads picture life as happier than reality.” (p. 548) “However, the most damaging miscalculations from mere association do not ordinarily come from advertisers and music providers.” (p. 548) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 547–548)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Do not be fooled by what was merely associated with past success. “To avoid being misled by the mere association of some fact with past success, use this memory clue. Think of Napoleon and Hitler when they invaded Russia after using their armies with much success elsewhere.” (pp. 548–549) “The proper antidotes to being made such a patsy by past success are (1) to carefully examine each past success, looking for accidental, non causative factors associated with such success that will tend to mislead as one appraises odds implicit in a proposed new undertaking and (2) to look for dangerous aspects of the new undertaking that were not present when past success occurred.” (p. 549) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 548–549)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Love blinds: Franklin's “half shut” and his own version. The jury case: the woman's testimony; counsel's closing, “What are we to make of such testimony?” (pp. 549–550); “The jury disbelieved the woman's testimony. They easily recognized the strong mis influence of love on her cognition.” (p. 550) Franklin: “Keep your eyes wide open before marriage and half shut thereafter.” (p. 550) His own: “Perhaps this “eyes-half-shut” solution is about right, but I favor a tougher prescription: “See it like it is and love anyway.”” (pp. 550–551) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 549–551)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Hating makes you under-appraise competitors; the Persian Messenger. “Hating and disliking also cause miscalculation triggered by mere association. In business, I commonly see people under appraise both the competency and morals of competitors they dislike. This is a dangerous practice, usually disguised because it occurs on a subconscious basis.” (p. 551) Persian Messenger Syndrome: “It was actually safer for the messenger to run away and hide, instead of doing his job as a wiser boss would have wanted it done.” (p. 551) “It is actually dangerous in many careers co be a carrier of unwelcome news.” (p. 551; “co” as printed) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 551)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Welcome bad news: Paley again, and the Berkshire injunction. “Sometimes lawyers, knowing their clients will hate them if they recommend an unwelcome but wise settlement, will carry on to disaster.” (p. 552) “My guess is that the cause of the trial was some general counsel's unwillingness to carry bad news to a strong-minded CEO.” (p. 552, on the two oil companies that litigated in a Texas trial court) “CBS, in its late heyday, was famous for occurrence of Persian Messenger Syndrome because Chairman Paley was hostile to people who brought him bad news. The result was that Paley lived in a cocoon of unreality, from which he made one bad deal after another, even exchanging a large share of CBS for a company that had to be liquidated shortly thereafter.” (p. 552) “The proper antidote to creating Persian Messenger Syndrome and its bad effects, like those at CBS, is to develop, through exercise of will, a habit of welcoming bad news. At Berkshire, there is a common injunction: “Always tell us the bad news promptly. It is only the good news that can wait.”” (pp. 552–553) “It also helps to be so wise and informed that people fear not telling you bad news because you are so likely to get it elsewhere.” (p. 553) (Same Paley story: USC talk, p. 181.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 552–553)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — A favor can breed hatred; classification stereotypes. “Sometimes, when one receives a favor, his condition is unpleasant, due to poverty, sickness, subjugation, or something else. In addition, the favor may trigger an envy-driven dislike for the person who was in so favorable a state that he could easily be a favor giver.” (p. 553) Henry Ford line, “dubiously attributed”: “Why does that man hate me so? I never did anything for him.” (p. 553) Stereotypes: “just as he must learn that trend does not always correctly predict destiny, he must learn that the average dimension in some group will not reliably guide him to the dimension of some specific item.” (p. 554) “like that of the fellow who drowned in a river that averaged out only eighteen inches deep.” (pp. 554–555) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 553–555)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Denial, and chemical dependency. “The reality is too painful to bear, so one distorts the facts until they become bearable.” (p. 555) “His mother, who was a very sane woman, then refused to believe he was dead. That's Simple, Pain-Avoiding Psychological Denial.” (p. 555, of a family friend's son lost over the Atlantic in World War II) “In my youth, Freudian remedies failed utterly in reversing chemical dependency, but nowadays Alcoholics Anonymous routinely achieves a fifty percent cure rate by causing several psychological tendencies to act together to counter addiction.” (p. 556) “One should stay far away from any conduct at all likely to drift into chemical dependency. Even a small chance of suffering so great a damage should be avoided.” (p. 556) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 555–556) Also in: HIM, The Complete Investor, pp. 60–61 (book's label: Harvard University, 1995).
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Excessive Self-Regard: Swedish drivers, the endowment effect, people like oneself. “He mostly misappraises himself on the high side, like the ninety percent of Swedish drivers that judge themselves to be above average.” (p. 556) “Once owned, they suddenly become worth more to him than he would pay if they were offered for sale to him and he didn't already own them.” (pp. 556–557) “Man's excess of self-regard typically makes him strongly prefer people like himself.” (p. 557) “Some of the worst consequences in modern life come when dysfunctional groups of cliquish persons, dominated by Excessive Self-Regard Tendency, select as new members of their organizations persons who are very much like themselves.” (p. 557) “Therefore, some of the most useful members of our civilization are those who are willing to “clean house” when they find a mess under their ambit of control.” (p. 558) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 556–558)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Self-regard in gambling and in betting against better players. “In lotteries, the play is much lower when numbers are distributed randomly than it is when the player picks his own number. This is quite irrational.” (p. 558) “Also extremely counterproductive is man's tendency to bet, time after time, in games of skill, like golf or poker, against people who are obviously much better players.” (p. 559) “More counterproductive yet are man's appraisals, typically excessive, of the quality of the future service he is to provide to his business. His over appraisal of these prospective contributions will frequently cause disaster.” (p. 559) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 558–559)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Hiring: underweigh the interview, overweigh the record; Hewlett-Packard and Fiorina. “Excesses of self-regard often cause bad hiring decisions because employers grossly over appraise the worth of their own conclusions that rely on impressions in face-to-face contact. The correct antidote to this sort of folly is to underweigh face to-face impressions and overweigh the applicant's past record.” (pp. 559–560) “Because man is likely to be over influenced by face-to-face impressions that by definition involve his active participation, a job candidate who is a marvelous “presenter” often causes great danger under modern executive-search practice.” (p. 560) “In my opinion, Hewlett-Packard faced just such a danger when it interviewed the articulate, dynamic Carly Fiorina in its search for a new CEO.” (p. 560) “And I believe (1) that Hewlett-Packard made a bad decision when it chose Ms. Fiorina and Q) that this bad decision would not have been made if Hewlett-Packard had taken the methodological precautions it would have taken if it knew more psychology.” (pp. 560–561; “Q)” as printed) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 559–561)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — The Tolstoy effect: excuses for fixable poor performance. “The second half of the “Tolstoy effect”, where the man makes excuses for his fixable poor performance, instead of providing the fix, is enormously important.” (p. 561) “(1) fixable but unfixed bad performance is bad character and tends to create more of itself, causing more damage to the excuse giver with each tolerated instance, and (2) in demanding places, like athletic teams and General Electric, you are almost sure to be discarded in due course if you keep giving excuses instead of behaving as you should.” (pp. 561–562) “The main institutional antidotes to this part of the “Tolstoy effect” are (1) a fair, meritocratic, demanding culture plus personnel handling methods that build up morale and (2) severance of the worst offenders.” (p. 562) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 561–562)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — The father who told his son to call himself a thief. “I once heard of a child-teaching method so effective that the child remembered the learning experience over fifty years later. The child later became Dean of the USC School of Music and then related to me what his father said when he saw his child taking candy from the stock of his employer with the excuse that he intended to replace it later. The father said, ‘Son, it would be better for you to simply take all you want and call yourself a thief every time you do it’” (p. 562) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 562)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Be objective about yourself; pride in being trustworthy. “The best antidote to folly from an excess of self-regard is to force yourself to be more objective when you are thinking about yourself, your family and friends, your property, and the value of your past and future activity. This isn't easy to do well and won't work perfectly, but it will work much better than simply letting psychological nature take its normal course.” (p. 562) “Never underestimate the man who overestimates himself.” (p. 563) “Of all forms of useful pride, perhaps the most desirable is a justified pride in being trustworthy.” (p. 563) “Without such justified pride, many more airplanes would crash.” (p. 563) “Moreover, the trustworthy man, even after allowing for the inconveniences of his chosen course, ordinarily has a life that averages our better than he would have if he provided less reliability.” (p. 563; “our” as printed) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 562–563)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Overoptimism: Demosthenes, and the math of Fermat and Pascal. “What a man wishes, that also will he believe.” (p. 564, Demosthenes as he gives it) “One standard antidote to foolish optimism is trained, habitual use of the simple probability math of Fermat and Pascal, taught in my youth to high school sophomores.” (p. 564) “The mental rules of thumb that evolution gives you to deal with risk are not adequate. They resemble the dysfunctional golf grip you would have if you relied on a grip driven by evolution instead of golf lessons.” (p. 564) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 564)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Deprival-Super Reaction: loss hurts more, and he misframes what matters. “The quantity of man's pleasure from a ten dollar gain does not exactly match the quantity of his displeasure from a ten-dollar loss. That is, the loss seems to hurt much more than the gain seems to help.” (p. 565) “if a man almost gets something he greatly wants and has it jerked away from him at the last moment, he will react much as if he had long owned the reward and had it jerked away.” (p. 565) “He will often compare what is near instead of what really matters. For instance, a man with $10 million in his brokerage account will often be extremely irritated by the accidental loss of $100 out of the $300 in his wallet.” (p. 565) “A man ordinarily reacts with irrational intensity to even a small loss, or threatened loss, of property, love, friendship, dominated territory, opportunity, status, or any other valued thing.” (p. 566) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 565–566)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Bureaucratic territory, and Jack Welch. “As a natural result, bureaucratic infighting over the threatened loss of dominated territory often causes immense damage to an institution as a whole. This factor, among others, accounts for much of the wisdom of Jack Welch's long fight against bureaucratic ills at General Electric. Few business leaders have ever conducted wiser campaigns.” (p. 566) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 566)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Ideology, heresy, and two antidotes. “University Liberal arts departments, law schools, and business organizations all display plenty of such ideology-based group think that rejects almost all conflicting inputs.” (pp. 566–567) “It is almost everywhere the case that extremes of ideology are maintained with great intensity and with great antipathy to non-believers, causing extremes of cognitive dysfunction.” (p. 567) “One antidote to intense, deliberate maintenance of group think is an extreme culture of courtesy, kept in place despite ideological differences, like the behavior of the justices now serving on the U.S. Supreme Court. Another antidote is to deliberately bring in able and articulate disbelievers of incumbent group think.” (pp. 567–568) “Successful corrective measures to evil examples of group think maintenance have included actions like that of Derek Bok when, as president of Harvard, he started disapproving tenure appointments proposed by ideologues at Harvard Law School.” (p. 568) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 566–568)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Neighbors, zoning hearings, labor relations. “Even a one-degree loss from a 1SO-degree view will sometime create enough Deprival-Super reaction Tendency to turn a neighbor into an enemy” (p. 568; “1SO” as printed) “the clamor of almost any group of neighbors displaying irrational, extreme deprival-super reaction over some trifle in a zoning hearing is not a pretty thing to watch.” (p. 568) “Deprival-Super Reaction Tendency has ghastly effects in labor relations.” (p. 568) “Many tragedies, therefore, occur that would have been avoided had there been more rationality and less subconscious heed of the imperative from Deprival-Super Reaction Tendency.” (p. 569) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 568–569)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Gambling, auctions, throwing good assets after a bad venture. “First, it causes the gambler to have a passion to get even once he has suffered loss, and the passion grows with the loss. Second, the most addictive forms of gambling provide a lot of near misses and each one triggers Deprival-Super Reaction Tendency.” (p. 569) “The best antidote to being thus triggered into paying foolish prices at open-outcry auctions is the simple Buffett practice: Don't go to such auctions.” (p. 570; the practice is Buffett's, named by Munger) “In this form of ruin, a man gradually uses up all his good assets in a fruitless attempt to rescue a big venture going bad. One of the best antidotes to this folly is good poker skill learned young.” (p. 570) “The teaching value of poker demonstrates that not all effective teaching occurs on a standard academic path.” (p. 570) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 569–570)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Why Berkshire holders never sell. “However, I believe that I haven't defined the tendency as broadly as I should. My reason for suggesting an even broader definition is that many Berkshire Hathaway shareholders I know never sell or give away a single share after immense gains in market value have occurred.” (p. 571) “But I believe the single strongest irrational explanation is a form of Deprival-Super reaction Tendency.” (p. 572) “Partly they dislike facing what they consider an impairment of identity, but mostly they fear missing out on future gains from stock sold or given away.” (p. 572) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 571–572)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Social Proof defined, and the football crowd. “The otherwise complex behavior of man is much simplified when he automatically thinks and does what he observes to be thought and done around him. And such followership often works fine.” (p. 572) “an automatic tendency to think and act as he sees others around him thinking and acting.” (p. 572) Judith Rich Harris: “This makes it wise for parents to rely more on manipulating the quality of the peers than on exhortations to their own offspring.” (p. 573) “A person like Ms. Harris, who can provide an insight of this quality and utility, backed by new reasons, has not lived in vain.” (p. 573) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 572–573)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Oil companies copying each other; when social proof bites. “If one oil company foolishly buys a mine, other oil companies often quickly join in buying mines. So also if the purchased company makes fertilizer. Both of these oil company buying fads actually bloomed, with bad results.” (p. 574) “So oil company executives, like everyone else, have made many bad decisions that were quickly triggered by discomfort from doubt. Going along with social proof provided by the action of other oil companies ends this discomfort in a natural way.” (p. 574) “Triggering most readily occurs in the presence of puzzlement or stress, and particularly when both exist.” (p. 574) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 574)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Isolation plus stress: swampland sales and cults. “disreputable sales organizations, engaged, for instance, in such action as selling swampland to schoolteachers, manipulate targets into situations combining isolation and stress.” (pp. 574–575) “One cult even used rattlesnakes to heighten the stress felt by conversion targets.” (p. 575) “it is highly important that human societies (1) stop any bad behavior before it spreads and (2) foster and display all good behavior.” (p. 575) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 574–575)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Serpico Syndrome, Kitty Genovese, and directors. “Such corruption was being driven by social proof plus incentives, the combination that creates Serpico Syndrome. The Serpico story should be taught more than it now is” (p. 576) “In the presence of doubt, inaction by others becomes social proof that inaction is the right course.” (p. 576) “They fail to object to anything much short of an axe murder until some public embarrassment of the board finally causes their intervention.” (p. 577, of outside directors on a corporate board) Joe Rosenfield: “They asked me if I wanted to become a director of Northwest Bell, and it was the last thing they ever asked me.” (p. 577) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 576–577)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Advertising and the soup can; the Middle East. “an advertiser will pay a lot to have its soup can, instead of someone else's, in a movie scene involving soup consumption only in a peripheral way.” (p. 577) “By now the resources spent by Jews, Arabs and all others over a small amount of disputed land if divided arbitrarily among land claimants, would have made everyone better off, even before taking into account any benefit from reduced threat of war, possibly nuclear.” (p. 578) “I often wonder if some day, in some way, more use of psychological insight will eventually improve outcomes.” (p. 578) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 577–578)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — The one Social-Proof lesson he would keep. “If only one lesson is to be chosen from a package of lessons involving Social-Proof Tendency, and used in self improvement, my favorite would be: Learn how to ignore the examples from others when they are wrong, because few skills are more worth having.” (p. 579) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 579)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Contrast-Misreaction: the $1,000 leather dashboard, the second wife. “Few psychological tendencies do more damage to correct thinking.” (p. 579) “Small-scale damages involve instances such as man's buying an overpriced $1,000 leather dashboard merely because the price is so low compared to his concurrent purchase of a $65,000 car.” (p. 579) “Large- scale damages often ruin lives, as when a wonderful woman having terrible parents marries a man who would be judged satisfactory only in comparison to her parents. Or as when a man takes wife number two who would be appraised as all right only in comparison to wife number one.” (pp. 579–580) “To make an ordinary price seem low, the vendor will very frequently create a highly artificial price that is much higher than the price always sought, then advertise his standard price as a big reduction from his phony price.” (p. 580) “Even when people know that this sort of customer manipulation is being attempted, it will often work to trigger buying.” (p. 580) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 579–580) Also in: HIM, The Complete Investor, pp. 65–66 (book's label: Harvard University, 1995).
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Small steps toward disaster; the frog; the small leak. “When a man's steps are consecutively taken toward disaster, with each step being very small, the brain's Contrast-Misreaction Tendency will often let the man go too far toward disaster to be able to avoid it.” (p. 581) “But no matter because many businesses die in just the manner claimed by - friend for the frog.” (p. 581; “by - friend” as printed) “Cognition, misled by tiny changes involving low contrast, will often miss a trend that is destiny.” (p. 581) Franklin: “A small leak will sink a great ship.” (p. 581) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 581)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Stress: light helps, heavy breaks; Churchill and Johnson. “light stress can slightly improve performance-say, in examinations-whereas heavy stress causes dysfunction.” (p. 582) “Even before modern drugs were available, many people afflicted by depression, such as Winston Churchill and Samuel Johnson, gained great achievement in life.” (p. 582) “Fortunately, as most people also know, such a depression is one of mankind's more reversible ailments.” (p. 582) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 582)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Pavlov's last work, in his own account. “During the great Leningrad Flood of the 1920s, Pavlov had many dogs in cages.” (p. 583) “The dog that formerly had liked his trainer now disliked him, for example.” (p. 583) “That was indeed Pavlov's reaction. But not many scientists would have done what Pavlov next did.” (p. 584) Pavlov's four findings: “(1) that he could classify dogs so as to predict how easily a particular dog would breakdown (2) that the dogs hardest to break down were also the hardest to return to their pre breakdown state; (3) that any dog could be broken down; and (4) that he couldn't reverse a breakdown except by reimposing stress.” (p. 584) “Obviously, Pavlov is now a sort of forgotten hero in medical science.” (p. 585) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 583–585) Also in: HIM, The Complete Investor, p. 66 (book's label: Harvard University, 1995).
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Why he put Pavlov in the talk. “I never wanted to get into the legal controversy that existed about this subject. But I did conclude that the controversy couldn't be handled with maximized rationality without considering whether, as Pavlov's last work suggests, the heavy-handed imposition of stress might be the only reversal method that would work to remedy one of the worst evils imaginable: a stolen mind.” (p. 585) “I have included this discussion of Pavlov (1) partly out of general antagonism toward taboos, (2) partly to make my talk reasonably complete as it considers stress and (3) partly because I hope some listener may continue my inquiry with more success.” (p. 585) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 585)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Availability-Mis Weighing: the girl I'm near, and the antidotes. “When I'm not near the girl I love, I love the girl I'm near.” (p. 586) “Man's imperfect, limited-capacity brain easily drifts into working with what's easily available to it.” (p. 586) “The main antidote to miscues from Availability Misweighing Tendency often involve procedures, including use of checklists, which are almost always helpful.” (p. 586) “What should be done is to especially emphasize factors that don't produce reams of easily available numbers, instead of drifting mostly or entirely into considering factors that do produce such numbers.” (p. 586) “Still another antidote is to find and hire some skeptical, articulate people with far reaching minds to act as advocates for notions that are opposite to the incumbent notions.” (p. 586) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 586)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Vivid evidence, and the great algorithm. “One consequence of this tendency is that extra vivid evidence, being so memorable and thus more available in cognition, should often consciously be underweighted while less vivid evidence should be overweighed.” (p. 587) “The great algorithm to remember in dealing with this tendency is simple: An idea or a fact is not worth more merely because it is easily available to you.” (p. 587) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 587)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Use-It-or-Lose-It: the simulator, the checklist, Paderewski, fluency. “All skills attenuate with disuse.” (p. 587) “The right antidote to such a loss is to make use of the functional equivalent of the aircraft simulator employed in pilot training.” (pp. 587–588) “Throughout his life, a wise man engages in practice of all his useful, rarely used skills, many of them outside his discipline, as a sort of duty to his better self.” (p. 588) “It is also essential for a thinking man to assemble his skills into a checklist that he routinely uses. Any other mode of operation will cause him to miss much that is important.” (p. 588) “Skills of a very high order can be maintained only with daily practice.” (p. 588) “a wise man engaged in learning some important skill will not stop until he is really fluent in it.” (p. 589) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 587–589)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Drugs, and old age. “This tendency's destructive power is so widely known to be intense, with frequent tragic consequences for cognition and the outcome of life, that it needs no discussion here to supplement that previously given under “Simple, Pain-Avoiding Psychological Denial.”” (p. 589) “Practically no one is good at learning complex new skills when very old. But some people remain pretty good in maintaining intensely practiced old skills until late in life.” (p. 589) “Continuous thinking and learning, done with joy can somewhat help delay what is inevitable.” (p. 590) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 589–590) Also in: HIM, The Complete Investor, p. 69 (book's label: Harvard University, 1995).
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Authority-Misinfluence: the pilot, the copilot, and Buffett. “man was born mostly to follow leaders, with only a few people doing the leading.” (p. 590) “man is often destined to suffer greatly when the leader is wrong or when his leader's ideas don't get through properly in the bustle of life and are misunderstood.” (p. 590) The pilot: “a new pilot for a general, who sat beside him in the copilot's seat, was so anxious to please his boss that he misinterpreted some minor shift in the general's position as a direction to do some foolish thing. The pilot crashed the plane and became a paraplegic.” (p. 591) “Well, naturally, cases like this one get the attention of careful thinkers like Boss Buffett, who always acts like an over quiet mouse around his pilots.” (p. 591) “copilots in simulator exercises will too often allow the simulated plane to crash because of some extreme and perfectly obvious simulated error of the chief pilot.” (pp. 591–592) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 590–592)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Milgram, the checklist, and the professors. “Almost any intelligent person with my checklist of psychological tendencies in his hand would, by simply going down the checklist, have seen that Milgram's experiment involved about six powerful psychological tendencies acting in confluence to bring about his extreme experimental result.” (pp. 592–593) “For instance, the person pushing Milgram' s shock lever was given much social proof from presence of inactive bystanders whose silence communicated that his behavior was okay.” (p. 593) “Yet it took over a thousand psychological papers, published before I got to Milgram, for the professoriate to get his experiment only about ninety percent as well understood as it would have immediately been by any intelligent person who used (1) any sensible organization of psychology along the lines of this talk, plus (2) a checklist procedure.” (p. 593) (Same subject: Talk Three, pp. 241–244.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 592–593)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — The tarpon angler and the psychology Ph.D. CEO. The guide's order, “Give him the rod, give him the rod.” (p. 594) “This example shows how powerful is the tendency to go along with an authority figure and how it can turn one's brain into mush.” (p. 594) The CEO: “A psychology Ph. D. once became a CEO of a major company and went wild, creating an expensive new headquarters, with a great wine cellar, at an isolated site.” “Take the money out of the depreciation reserves,” (p. 594) “Not too easy because a depreciation reserve is a liability account.” (p. 594) “The obvious implication: Be careful who you appoint to power because a dominant authority figure will often be hard to remove, aided as he will be by Authority Misinfluence Tendency.” (p. 595) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 594–595)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Twaddle, the honeybee, and the Caltech professor. “Man, as a social animal who has the gift of language, is born to prattle and to pour out twaddle that does much damage when serious work is being attempted.” (p. 595) The bee, told to report nectar straight up: “She comes into the hive and does an incoherent dance. Well, all my life I've been dealing with the human equivalent of that honeybee. And it's a very important part of wise administration to keep prattling people, pouring our twaddle, far away from the serious work.” (p. 596; “our” as printed) The professor: “The principal job of an academic administration is to keep the people who don't matter from interfering with the work of the people that do.” (p. 596) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 595–596)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Reason-Respecting, and Carl Braun's rule. “Few practices, therefore, are wiser than not only thinking through reasons before giving orders but also communicating these reasons to the recipient of the order.” (p. 597) “No one knew this better than Carl Braun, who designed oil refineries with spectacular skill and integrity. He had a very simple rule, one of many in his large, Teutonic company: You had to tell Who was to do What, Where, When, and Why. And if you wrote a communication leaving out your explanation of why the addressee was to do what was ordered, Braun was likely to fire you because Braun well knew that ideas got through best when reasons for the ideas were meticulously laid out.” (p. 597) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 597)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Theory answering “Why?”, and the meaningless reason. “In general, learning is most easily assimilated and used when, life long, people consistently hang their experience, actual and vicarious, on a lattice work of theory answering the question: Why?” (p. 597) “Indeed, the question “Why?” is a sort of Rosetta stone opening up the major potentiality of mental life.” (p. 598) “Unfortunately, Reason- Respecting Tendency is so strong that even a person's giving of meaningless or incorrect reasons will increase compliance with his orders and requests.” (p. 598) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 597–598)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Lollapalooza Tendency, and what the old textbooks did to it. “This tendency was not in any of the psychology texts I once examined, at least in any coherent fashion, yet it dominates life. It accounts for the extreme result in the Milgram experiment and the extreme success of some cults that have stumbled through practice evolution into bringing pressure from many psychological tendencies to bear at the same time on conversion targets.” (pp. 598–599) “How could anyone who had taken a freshman course in physics or chemistry not be driven to consider, above all, how psychological tendencies combine and with what effects?” (p. 599) “What could be more ironic than professors using oversimplified notions while studying bad cognitive effects grounded in the mind's tendency to use oversimplified algorithms?” (p. 599) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 598–599)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Why the old psychology professors went wrong, as he guesses it. “It would be like physics ignoring (1) astrophysics because it couldn't happen in a physics lab, plus (2) all compound effects.” (p. 600) “One candidate would be Avail ability-Mis Weighing Tendency grounded in a preference for easy-to-control data. And then the restrictions would eventually create an extreme case of man with a hammer tendency. Another candidate might be envy/jealousy Tendency through which early psychology professors displayed some weird form of envy of a physics that was misunderstood. And this possibility tends to demonstrate that leaving envy/jealousy out of academic psychology was never a good idea.” (p. 600) “Well, that ends my brief description of psycho logical tendencies.” (p. 600) The talk continues past p. 600. (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 599–600)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven, Questions and Answers)** — The limits of his own list of tendencies. “Isn't this list of psychological tendencies tautological to some extent compared to the system of Euclid? That is, aren't there overlaps in the tendencies? And couldn't the system be laid out just as plausibly in a somewhat different way? The answers are yes, yes, and yes, but this matters only moderately.” (p. 601) “Further refinement of these tendencies, while desirable, has a limited practical potential because a significant amount of messiness is unfixable in a soft science like psychology.” (p. 601) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 601)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven, Questions and Answers)** — The tendencies are mostly good; tendency is not destiny. “Well, the answer is that the tendencies are probably much more good than bad. Otherwise, they wouldn't be there, working pretty well for man, given his condition and his limited brain capacity.” (p. 604) “So the tendencies can't be simply washed out automatically, and shouldn't be.” (p. 604) “Nevertheless, the psychological thought system described, when properly understood and used, enables the spread of wisdom and good conduct and facilitates the avoidance of disaster. Tendency is not always destiny, and knowing the tendencies and their antidotes can often help prevent trouble that would otherwise occur.” (p. 604) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 604) Also in: HIM, The Complete Investor, p. 48 (book's label: Harvard University, 1995).
- **HIM (Talk Eleven, Questions and Answers)** — His ten examples of “the great utility of elementary psychological knowledge”: “One: Carl Braun's communication practices.” “Two: The use of simulators in pilot training.” “Three: The system of Alcoholics Anonymous.” “Four: Clinical training methods in medical schools.” (pp. 604–605) “Six: The use of Granny's incentive-driven rule to manipulate oneself toward better performance of one's duties.” (p. 605) “Seven: The Harvard Business School's emphasis on decision trees.” (pp. 605–606) “Eight: The use of autopsy equivalents at Johnson & Johnson.” (p. 606) “Nine: The great example of Charles Darwin as he avoided confirmation bias, which has morphed into the extreme anti- confirmation-bias method of the "double blind" studies wisely required in drug research by the FDA” (p. 606; space after “anti-” as printed) “Ten: The Warren Buffett rule for open-outcry auctions: Don't go.” (p. 606) (Number Five is the Constitutional Convention entry below; Seven is the decision-trees entry in DECISIONS; Eight is in SIGNALS; Ten is in REFUSALS. Also in: Braun, p. 597 and Granny's Rule, p. 516 above; the pilot simulator, pp. 587–588 above.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 604–606) Also in: HIM, The Complete Investor, p. 74 (book's label: Harvard University, 1995).
- **HIM (Talk Eleven, Questions and Answers)** — The Constitutional Convention's rules as psychology-respecting. “Five: The rules of the U.S. Constitutional Convention: totally secret meetings, no recorded vote by name until the final vote, votes reversible at any time before the end of the convention, then just one vote on the whole Constitution. These are very clever psychology- respecting rules.” (p. 605; space after “psychology-” as printed) “If the founders had used a different procedure, many people would have been pushed by various psychological tendencies into inconsistent, hardened positions.” (p. 605) “The elite founders got our Constitution through by a whisker only because they were psychologically acute.” (p. 605) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 605)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven, Questions and Answers)** — The paradox in the system. “Well, one answer is paradox. In social psychology, the more people learn about the system the less it is true, and this is what gives the system its great value as a preventer of bad outcomes and a driver of good outcomes.” (p. 607) “This result is paradoxical, and doesn't remind one of elementary physics, but so what. One can't get all the paradox out of pure math, so why should psychology be shocked by some paradox?” (p. 607) “There is also some paradox in cognition change that works even when the manipulated person knows he is being manipulated.” (p. 607) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 607)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven, Questions and Answers)** — Using psychology to manipulate: Captain Cook allowed; the trusted person, no. “psychological knowledge improves persuasive power and, like other power, it can be used for good or ill.” (p. 609) “Captain Cook once played a psychology-based trick on his seamen to cause them to eat sauerkraut and avoid scurvy. In my opinion. this action was both ethical and wise under the circumstances, despite the deliberate manipulation involved.” (p. 609; the period after “opinion” as printed) “But ordinarily, when you try to use your knowledge of psychological tendencies in the artful manipulation of someone whose trust you need, you will be making both a moral and prudential error.” (p. 609) “The moral error is obvious. The prudential error comes because many intelligent people, targeted for conscious manipulation, are likely to figure out what you are trying to do and resent your action.” (p. 609) (Cook's sauerkraut is also told in Talk Three, above.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 609)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven, Questions and Answers)** — What he hopes psychology professors will do. “I even hope that more psychology professors will join me in: (1) making heavy use of inversion; (2) driving for a complete description of the psychological system so that it works better as a checklist; and (3) especially emphasizing effects from combinations of psychological tendencies.” (p. 610) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 610)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven Revisited, 2006)** — What the talk claims, and why he assembled it. The .txt of pp. 612–613 prints this passage without punctuation; the quotes are as printed. “In this talk I displayed some impertinency of my own by delivering an extreme sounding message it claims nothing less than (1) that academic psychology is hugely important, (2) that even so it is usually ill thought out and ill presented by its PHD denizens and (3) that my way of presenting psychology often has a large superiority in practical utility compared to most textbooks.” (p. 613) “Naturally I believe these extreme claims are correct after all I assemble the material contained in this talk to help me succeed in practical thinking and not to gain advantage by making public any would be clever notions.” (p. 613) “If anyone partly right the world will eventually see more Psychology in roughly the form of this talk if so I confidently predict that the change in practice will improve general competency.” (p. 613) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 613)
- **HIM** — On teaching. “The best thing a human being can do is help another human being know more.” (The Complete Investor, p. 7; book's label: Berkshire Annual Meeting, 2010)
- **HIM** — What he does. “I observe what works and what doesn't and why.” (AUTHOR, p. 7: "Much of what is interesting about Munger is explained by this simple sentence." No source is printed for the sentence.) (The Complete Investor, p. 7)
- **HIM** — Simple idea. “Take a simple idea and take it seriously.” (AUTHOR calls this "Munger's version" of Buffett's "investing is simple, but not easy.") (The Complete Investor, p. 9)
- **HIM** — Checklists. “I'm a great believer in solving hard problems by using a checklist. You need to get all the likely and unlikely answers before you; otherwise it's easy to miss something important.” (The Complete Investor, p. 10; book's label: Wesco Annual Meeting, 2007)
- **HIM** — On Graham. “[Ben Graham] was trying to invent a system anybody could use.” (The Complete Investor, p. 12; book's label: USC Business School, 1994)
- **HIM** — Simplicity. “We have a passion for keeping things simple.” (The Complete Investor, p. 13; book's label: Wesco Annual Meeting, 2002)
- **HIM** — Clearing the clutter. AUTHOR quotes it with "said Munger", no source printed: “so your mind isn't cluttered with them … you're better able to pick up a few sensible things to do,” (The Complete Investor, p. 13)
- **HIM** — Too hard. “If something is too hard, we move on to something else. What could be simpler than that?” (The Complete Investor, p. 13; book's label: Berkshire Annual Meeting, 2006)
- **HIM** — Three baskets. “We have three baskets: in, out, and too tough. … We have to have a special insight, or we'll put it in the ‘too tough’ basket.” (The Complete Investor, p. 13; book's label: Wesco Annual Meeting, 2002)
- **HIM** — Knowing the extent of one's ignorance. “Confucius said that real knowledge is knowing the extent of one's ignorance. Aristotle and Socrates said the same thing. Is it a skill that can be taught or learned? It probably can, if you have enough of a stake riding on the outcome. Some people are extraordinarily good at knowing the limits of their knowledge, because they have to be. Think of somebody who's been a professional tightrope walker for 20 years—and has survived. He couldn't survive as a tightrope walker for 20 years unless he knows exactly what he knows and what he doesn't know. He's worked so hard at it, because he knows if he gets it wrong he won't survive. The survivors know.” (The Complete Investor, p. 14; book's label: Jason Zweig Interview, 2014)
- **HIM** — The flip side. “What's the flip side, what can go wrong that I haven't seen?” (The Complete Investor, p. 15; book's label: Forbes, 1969)
- **HIM** — Knowing what you don't know. “Knowing what you don't know is more useful than being brilliant.” (AUTHOR: "Once, in an interview with Jason Zweig, Munger said it simply:") (The Complete Investor, p. 15; book's label: Jason Zweig interview, as introduced by the author)
- **HIM** — Poker. “The idea that everyone can have wonderful results from stocks is inherently crazy. Nobody expects everyone to succeed at poker.” (The Complete Investor, p. 16; book's label: Daily Journal Meeting, 2013)
- **HIM** — Mispricing. “For a security to be mispriced, someone else must be a damn fool. It may be bad for the world, but not bad for Berkshire.” (The Complete Investor, p. 17; book's label: Wesco Annual Meeting, 2008)
- **HIM** — The 10 percent. “Most people who try [investing] don't do well at it. But the trouble is that if even 90 percent are no good, everyone looks around and says, ‘I'm the 10 percent.’” (The Complete Investor, p. 17; book's label: Wesco Annual Meeting, 2004)
- **HIM** — Why the ideas have not spread. “It's a very simple set of ideas and the reason that our ideas have not spread faster is they're too simple. The professional classes can't justify their existence if that's all they have to say.” (The Complete Investor, p. 18; book's label: BBC Interview, 2009)
- **HIM** — The know-nothing investor. “Our standard prescription for the know-nothing investor with a long-term time horizon is a no-load index fund.” (The Complete Investor, p. 19; book's label: Kiplinger Interview, 2005)
- **HIM** — Not hiring out the thinking. “I think you'll at least make fewer mistakes than people who think they can do anything, no matter how complex, by just hiring somebody with a credible label. You don't have to hire out your thinking if you keep it simple.” (The Complete Investor, p. 21; book's label: Berkshire Annual Meeting, 1994)
- **HIM** — Investing and managing. “Understanding how to be a good investor makes you a better business manager and vice versa.” (The Complete Investor, p. 22; book's label: Kiplinger, 2005)
- **HIM** — The herd. “Mimicking the herd invites regression to the mean (merely average performance).” (The Complete Investor, p. 24; book's label: Poor Charlie's Almanack, 2005)
- **HIM** — Projections. “[Projections] are put together by people who have an interest in a particular outcome, have a subconscious bias, and its apparent precision makes it fallacious. They remind me of Mark Twain's saying, ‘A mine is a hole in the ground owned by a liar.’ Projections in America are often a lie, although not an intentional one, but the worst kind because the forecaster often believes them himself.” (The Complete Investor, p. 27; book's label: Buffett Speaks, 2007)
- **HIM** — Projections, the other way of saying it. “I don't let others do projections for me, because I don't like throwing up on the desk.” (The Complete Investor, p. 27; book's label: University of California, Santa Barbara, 2003)
- **HIM** — Projections from interested people. “A projection prepared by anybody who stands to earn a commission or an executive trying to justify a particular course of action will frequently be a lie—although it's not a deliberate lie in most cases. The man has come to believe it himself. And that's the worst kind. Projections should be handled with care, particularly when they're being provided by someone who has an interest in misleading you.” (The Complete Investor, p. 60; book's label: Berkshire Annual Meeting, 1995)
- **HIM** — Margin of safety. “The idea of a margin of safety, a Graham precept, will never be obsolete.” (The Complete Investor, p. 29; book's label: Wesco Annual Meeting, 2003)
- **HIM** — Not worth an infinite price. “No matter how wonderful [a business] is, it's not worth an infinite price. We have to have a price that makes sense and gives a margin of safety considering the normal vicissitudes of life.” (The Complete Investor, p. 29; book's label: BBC Interview, 2009)
- **HIM** — Graham's one-third rule, as he tells it. “If you could take the stock price and multiply it by the number of shares and get something that was one third or less of sellout value, [Ben Graham] would say that you've got a lot of edge going for you. Even with an elderly alcoholic running a stodgy business, this significant excess of real value per share working for you means that all kinds of good things can happen to you. You had a huge margin of safety—as he put it—by having this big excess value going for you.” (The Complete Investor, pp. 30, 97; book's label: USC Business School, 1994)
- **HIM** — On Graham as an investor. “Ben Graham had a lot to learn as an investor. His ideas of how to value companies were all shaped by how the Great Crash and the Depression almost destroyed him, and he was always a little afraid of what the market can do. It left him with an aftermath of fear for the rest of his life, and all his methods were designed to keep that at bay. I think Ben Graham wasn't nearly as good an investor as Warren Buffett is or even as good as I am. Buying those cheap, cigar-butt stocks” [the author's bracketed gloss in the book: companies with limited potential growth selling at a fraction of what they would be worth in a takeover or liquidation] “was a snare and a delusion, and it would never work with the kinds of sums of money we have. You can't do it with billions of dollars or even many millions of dollars. But he was a very good writer and a very good teacher and a brilliant man, one of the only intellectuals—probably the only intellectual—in the investing business at the time.” (The Complete Investor, pp. 31–32; book's label: Jason Zweig Interview, 2014)
- **HIM** — How efficient the market is. “I think it's roughly right that the market is efficient, which makes it very hard to beat merely by being an intelligent investor. But I don't think it's totally efficient at all. And the difference between being totally efficient and somewhat efficient leaves an enormous opportunity for people like us to get these unusual records. It's efficient enough, so it's hard to have a great investment record. But it's by no means impossible. Nor is it something that only a very few people can do. The top 3 or 4 percent of the investment management world will do fine.” (The Complete Investor, p. 34; book's label: Kiplinger, 2009)
- **HIM** — Buying more when it goes down, and getting out when you are wrong. “Over many decades, our usual practice is that if [the stock of] something we like goes down, we buy more and more. Sometimes something happens, you realize you're wrong, and you get out. But if you develop correct confidence in your judgment, buy more and take advantage of stock prices.” (The Complete Investor, p. 35; book's label: Wesco Annual Meeting, 2002)
- **HIM** — Rationality as a binding principle. “Rationality is not just something you do so that you can make more money; it's a binding principle. Rationality is a really good idea. You must avoid the nonsense that is conventional in one's own time. It requires developing systems of thought that improve your batting average over time.” (The Complete Investor, p. 36; book's label: Wesco Annual Meeting, 2006)
- **HIM** — Rationality as a moral duty. “[An] increase in rationality is not just something you choose or don't choose; it's a moral duty to keep up as much as you reasonably can. It worked so well at Berkshire, not because we were so darned smart to start with—we were massively ignorant. Any of the great successes of Berkshire started with stupidity and failure.” (The Complete Investor, p. 36; book's label: Wesco Annual Meeting, 2011)
- **HIM** — Objective and dispassionate. “The idea of being objective and dispassionate will never be obsolete.” (The Complete Investor, p. 36; book's label: Berkshire Annual Meeting, 2003)
- **HIM** — Poetry professors. “All the wisdom of the world is not to be found in one little academic department. That's why poetry professors, by and large, are so unwise in a worldly sense. They don't have enough models in their heads.” (The Complete Investor, p. 38; book's label: USC Business School, 1994)
- **HIM** — General education before stock picking. “The theory of modern education is that you need a general education before you specialize. And I think to some extent, before you're going to be a great stock picker, you need some general education.” (The Complete Investor, p. 41; book's label: USC Business School, 1994)
- **HIM** — Eighty or ninety models. “You may say, ‘My God, this is already getting way too tough.’ But, fortunately, it isn't that tough—because eighty or ninety important models will carry about 90 percent of the freight in making you a worldly wise person. And, of those, only a mere handful really carry very heavy freight.” (The Complete Investor, p. 43; book's label: USC Business School, 1994)
- **HIM** — Smart people. “Terribly smart people make totally bonkers mistakes.” (The Complete Investor, p. 44; book's label: USC Business School, 1994)
- **HIM** — No method that prevents mistakes. “I don't want you to think we have any way of learning or behaving so you won't make mistakes.” (The Complete Investor, p. 44; book's label: USC Business School, 1994)
- **HIM** — Rubbing his nose in his mistakes. “I like people admitting they were complete stupid horses' asses. I know I'll perform better if I rub my nose in my mistakes. This is a wonderful trick to learn.” (The Complete Investor, p. 45; book's label: Berkshire Annual Meeting, 2011)
- **HIM** — Complexity, fraud and mistakes. “Where you have complexity, by nature you can have fraud and mistakes.” (The Complete Investor, p. 45)
- **HIM** — Forgetting mistakes. “Forgetting your mistakes is a terrible error if you're trying to improve your cognition. Reality doesn't remind you. Why not celebrate stupidities in both categories?” (The Complete Investor, p. 46; book's label: Wesco Annual Meeting, 2006)
- **HIM** — Mistakes of omission. “The most extreme mistakes in Berkshire's history have been mistakes of omission. We saw it, but didn't act on it. They're huge mistakes—we've lost billions. And we keep doing it. We're getting better at it. We never get over it. There are two types of mistakes: 1) doing nothing—what Warren calls ‘sucking my thumb’ and 2) buying with an eyedropper things we should be buying a lot of.” (The Complete Investor, p. 46; book's label: Berkshire Annual Meeting, 2001)
- **HIM** — Biggest mistakes. “Our biggest mistakes were things we didn't do, companies we didn't buy.” (The Complete Investor, p. 46; book's label: Fortune Magazine, 1998)
- **HIM** — Probabilities and crude heuristics. “Bias [arises] from the nonmathematical nature of the human brain in its natural state as it deals with probabilities employing crude heuristics, and is often misled.” (The Complete Investor, p. 47; book's label: Harvard University, 1995)
- **HIM** — Incentives, he underestimated them. “Almost everyone thinks he fully recognizes how important incentives and disincentives are in changing cognition and behavior. But this is not often so. For instance, I think I've been in the top 5 percent of my age cohort almost all my adult life in understanding the power of incentives, and yet I've always underestimated that power. Never a year passes but I get some surprise that pushes a little further my appreciation of incentive superpower.” (The Complete Investor, p. 50; book's label: Harvard University, 1995)
- **HIM** — Upton Sinclair on belief and livelihood. “Upton Sinclair said it best of all. He said, ‘It's very hard to get a man to believe non-X when his way of making a living requires him to believe X.’ On a subconscious level, your brain plays tricks on you and you think [that] what is good for the true little me is what you should believe.” (The Complete Investor, p. 50; book's label: Harvard-Westlake School, 2010)
- **HIM** — Ants and sugar. “The iron rule of nature is that you get what you reward for. If you want ants to come, put sugar on the floor.” (The Complete Investor, p. 50; book's label: Wesco Annual Meeting, 2001)
- **HIM** — The investment-management business. “Everyone wants to be an investment manager, raise the maximum amount of money, trade like mad with one another, and then just scrape the fees off the top. I know one guy; he's extremely smart and a very capable investor. I asked him, ‘What returns do you tell your institutional clients you will earn for them?’ He said, ‘20 percent.’ I couldn't believe it, because he knows that's impossible. But he said, ‘Charlie, if I gave them a lower number, they wouldn't give me any money to invest!’ The investment-management business is insane.” (The Complete Investor, pp. 50–51; book's label: Jason Zweig Interview, 2014)
- **HIM** — The Roman arch. “An example of a really responsible system is the system the Romans used when they built an arch. The guy who created the arch stood under it as the scaffolding was removed. It's like packing your own parachute.” (The Complete Investor, p. 51; book's label: Berkshire Annual Meeting, 1993)
- **HIM** — Rich people and their relatives (Munger quoting Buffett, per the AUTHOR). “A major difference between rich and poor people is that the rich people can spend more of their time suing their relatives.” (The Complete Investor, p. 53)
- **HIM** — The prey animal. “[It's] counterproductive for a prey animal that is threatened by a predator to take a long time in deciding what to do.” (The Complete Investor, p. 54; book's label: Poor Charlie's Almanack, 2005)
- **HIM** — Reluctance to change. “The brain of man conserves programming spaces by being reluctant to change.” (The Complete Investor, p. 54; book's label: Harvard University, 1995)
- **HIM** — Curiosity. “Curiosity can provide both fun and wisdom, and occasionally trouble.” (The Complete Investor, p. 55; book's label: Poor Charlie's Almanack, 2005)
- **HIM** — Born curious. “I was born innately curious. If that doesn't work for you, figure out your own damn system.” (The Complete Investor, p. 55; book's label: Wesco Annual Meeting, 2010)
- **HIM** — Fairness. “Modern acculturated man displays and expects from others a lot of fairness.” (The Complete Investor, p. 56; book's label: Harvard University, 2005)
- **HIM** — Origin of envy. “A member of a species designed through evolutionary process to want often scarce food is going to be driven strongly toward getting food when it first sees food. And this is going to occur often and tend to create some conflict when the food is seen in the possession of another member of the same species. This is probably the evolutionary origin of the envy/jealousy tendency that lies so deep in human nature.” (The Complete Investor, p. 57; book's label: USC, 1994)
- **HIM** — Missing out. “Missing out on some opportunity never bothers us. What's wrong with someone getting a little richer than you? It's crazy to worry about this.” (The Complete Investor, p. 57; book's label: Wesco Annual Meeting, 2005)
- **HIM** — Someone is always getting richer faster. “Here's one truth that perhaps your typical investment counselor would disagree with: if you're comfortably rich and someone else is getting richer faster than you by, for example, investing in risky stocks, so what?! Someone will always be getting richer faster than you. This is not a tragedy.” (The Complete Investor, p. 57; book's label: Wesco Annual Meeting, 2000)
- **HIM** — Envy and the Ten Commandments. “Well, envy/jealousy made, what, two out of the Ten Commandments? Those of you who have raised siblings … or tried to run a law firm or investment bank or even a faculty, you know about envy. I've heard Warren say a half a dozen times, ‘It's not greed that drives the world, but envy.’” (The Complete Investor, p. 58; book's label: Harvard University, 1995)
- **HIM** — Coveting the donkey. “Remember, [Moses] said you couldn't even covet your neighbor's donkey.” (The Complete Investor, p. 58; book's label: CNBC Interview, 2014)
- **HIM** — Reciprocation. “The automatic tendency of humans to reciprocate favors and disfavors has long been noticed as extreme.” (The Complete Investor, p. 58; book's label: Harvard University, 1995)
- **HIM** — Association and Coca-Cola. “Think how association, pure association, works. Take Coca-Cola Company (we're the biggest shareholder). They want to be associated with every wonderful image: heroics in the Olympics, wonderful music, you name it. They don't want to be associated with presidents' funerals and so forth.” (The Complete Investor, p. 59; book's label: Harvard University, 1995)
- **HIM** — Work on something that is false. “We don't like complexity and we distrust other systems and think it many times leads to false confidence. The harder you work, the more confidence you get. But you may be working hard on something that is false.” (The book prints the label "Seeking Wisdom, 2003"; that book is not a source here.) (The Complete Investor, p. 61; book's label: Seeking Wisdom, 2003)
- **HIM** — Deprival super-reaction. “The deprival super-reaction syndrome of man helps cause much ruin as people's cognition is distorted as a result of their suffering losses and near misses.” (The Complete Investor, p. 63; book's label: Stanford Law School, 1998)
- **HIM** — Loss and gain, and Zeckhauser. “Your brain doesn't naturally know how to think the way Zeckhauser knows how to play bridge. For example, people do not react symmetrically to loss and gain. Well, maybe a great bridge player like Zeckhauser does, but that's a trained response.” “I mean people are really crazy about minor decrements down. … Huge insanities can come from just subconsciously overweighing the importance of what you're losing or almost getting and not getting.” (The Complete Investor, pp. 63–64; book's label: Harvard University, 1995)
- **HIM** — Oil companies and fertilizer companies. “Big-shot businessmen get into these waves of social proof. Do you remember some years ago when an oil company bought a fertilizer company, and every other major oil company practically ran out and bought a fertilizer company? And there was no more damned reason for all these oil companies to buy fertilizer companies, but they didn't know exactly what to do, and if Exxon was doing it, it was good enough for Mobil, and vice versa. I think they're all gone now, but it was a total disaster.” (The Complete Investor, pp. 64–65; book's label: Harvard University, 1995)
- **HIM** — Good ideas and mischief. “This brings to mind Ben Graham's paradoxical observation that good ideas cause more investment mischief than bad ideas. He had it right. It's so easy for us all to push a really good idea to wretched excess, as in the case of the Florida land bubble or the ‘nifty fifty’ corporate stocks. Then mix in a little ‘social-proof’ (from other experts), and brains (including ours) often turn to mush.” (The Complete Investor, p. 65; book's label: Harvard University, 1995)
- **HIM** — The availability algorithm. “The great algorithm to remember in dealing with this tendency is simple: an idea or a fact is not worth more merely because it's easily available to you.” (The Complete Investor, p. 67; book's label: Harvard University, 1995)
- **HIM** — Calculus. “All skills attenuate with disuse. I was a whiz at calculus until age twenty, after which the skill was soon obliterated by total nonuse.” (The Complete Investor, p. 67; book's label: Harvard University, 1995)
- **HIM** — Alcohol and drugs. “We all know talented people who have ruined their lives abusing either alcohol or drugs—and often both.” (The Complete Investor, p. 68; book's label: Harvard-Westlake School, 1986)
- **HIM** — Three things. “Three things ruin people: drugs, liquor, and leverage.” (The Complete Investor, p. 68; book's label: Wesco Annual Meeting, 2009)
- **HIM** — The pilot and the co-pilot. “You get a pilot and a co-pilot. The pilot is the authority figure. They don't do this in airplanes, but they've done it in simulators.
