- **HIM + AUTHOR — suspicion (a business).** The sign: a small New England textile enterprise. Quote: Munger described Berkshire as “a small, doomed New England textile enterprise”; AUTHOR: “and he was correct.” (Damn Right!, p. 225)
- **REPORTED (Buffett) — suspicion (earning power).** The sign: earning power that is “hypothetical” (“Texas Instruments or Polaroid”). What it meant: Buffett credits Munger with making him look for earning power “only when you can be sure of it”. (Damn Right!, p. 238)
- **HIM + AUTHOR — suspicion (an industry).** The sign: an airline. Quote: “marginal cost with wings.” AUTHOR: Munger said that once an airline files for bankruptcy, “it is then operating debt free, thus it can be more competitive than a solvent one.” HIM on the unions and competitors: “all those unions that could only think about reality from their own point of view. And the idiot competition-including bankrupt competitors who stiff-armed their creditors while they lost money ruining our business.” (Damn Right!, pp. 244–246)
- **HIM — trust (a franchise).** The sign: customers hooked on a store. Quote: “If you get hooked on going to Costco with your family, you'll go for the rest of your life,” AUTHOR: “One of the reasons Munger likes Costco is because of Jim Sinegal, the company's president, whose office has no walls separating him from passing employees.” AUTHOR: “Charlie absolutely crows at the story of Costco's paper towels, a story that to Munger represents an admirable example of business ethics”; when the towels didn't match the guarantee, “they were withdrawn until a suitable towel could be sold.” (Damn Right!, pp. 249–250)
- **AUTHOR — suspicion (Salomon's talking points).** The sign: passive voice in a press statement (“one part of the problem has been known since late April”). What it meant: “Munger objected to the use of the passive voice, and demanded to be told who exactly knew.” REPORTED (Carol Loomis, Fortune, quoted by the author): “Munger didn't like it, finding this behavior neither candid nor smart. But not considering himself an expert on ‘funding,’ he subsided.” HIM: “Warren and I didn't see John's downfall” that first evening. (Damn Right!, p. 255)
- **AUTHOR — suspicion (a board's excuse).** The sign: a press release saying management had waited nearly four months because of the “press of other business.” What it meant: “That lame excuse, the board felt, would not fly. The wording was changed”. (The text says “the board”, not Munger by name.) (Damn Right!, p. 256)
- **AUTHOR — trust, then distrust (Gutfreund).** Trust: “Gutfruend impressed Warren and Charlie when in 1987 Salomon took a large trading loss, and then in a restructuring laid off 800 employees. That year Gutfreund declined a bonus worth about $2 million.” AUTHOR (Lowenstein, quoted): “To Charlie Munger, Gutfreund evoked all that was noble in Salomon's tribal culture, particularly its willingness to lay its capital on the line.” Distrust: “they were disturbed that at board meetings, directors were given out-of-date balance sheets.” (Damn Right!, pp. 259–260)
- **HIM — suspicion (a manager who hands the problem up).** The sign: Meriwether reported Mozer's trading to Gutfreund and added “I hope you can figure out a way to save this fine young man”; Gutfreund was “indecisive-‘How can I save my profit center, this employee, etc., etc.’ He procrastinated and didn't level with the board until the Fed threatened him-and then he still didn't fully disclose.” What he said the general counsel should have told him: “Tell the truth, tell it fully, tell it fast.” “He would have had to cashier the trader, but he got cashiered anyway.” AUTHOR: Munger called Gutfreund's reaction a “thumb-sucking response.” (Damn Right!, pp. 259–260)
- **HIM — suspicion (information withheld from directors).** The sign: a letter from the New York Fed's president went to outside lawyers and not to directors. Quote: “He gave it to outside lawyers but didn't send a copy to the directors,” “Warren and I went into that crisis without knowing all the facts. We got mildly nebulous responses. If we'd known, we would have worked it differently.” AUTHOR: “his credibility with the board of directors was destroyed.” (Damn Right!, pp. 260–261)
- **AUTHOR — suspicion (pay against results).** The sign: bonuses unrelated to the unit's results, 106 people at $1 million or more in a below-average year (Buffett's figure, in the company's quarterly report); “some issues, such as the granting of excessive bonuses, were never resolved to Munger's and Buffett's full satisfaction.” (Damn Right!, pp. 265–266)
- **REPORTED (Simpson) — what Munger looked for on the audit committee.** “He seized on tough issues and came back to them.” (The .txt prints “hack”.) “He spent the whole time reading and re-reading audit material. He added a lot of value in just questioning practices and the way to think about these practices.” (Damn Right!, p. 264)
- **HIM — suspicion (overconfidence).** The sign: high IQs, high leverage, and self-appraisals of superior methods (LTCM). Quote: “relying on their self-appraisals that they have superior talents and methods.” (Damn Right!, p. 269) Also in: HIM, Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 352 (Talk Six).
- **REPORTED (Marshall) + AUTHOR — suspicion (newspapers).** REPORTED (Marshall): “Marshall pointed out that neither Munger nor Buffett like investing in newspapers as much as they once did.” AUTHOR (p. 282): “newspapers aren't as lucrative as they once were”; legal advertising “has always been cyclical” and is “a slowly shrinking revenue source”. HIM on the Daily Journal's litigation (p. 273): “Berkshire has practically no litigation,” “But if you take our legal newspapers, I don't think a year passes without litigation. Discrimination, sex, old age, race. It is very litigious. The Metropolitan News is suing us now regarding the foreclosure business. It gets to be a bit of a sewer.” (Damn Right!, pp. 273, 277, 282)
- **HIM + AUTHOR — trust (a manager).** The sign: Salzman, “a known, tested, and trusted person”; HIM: “talented and so honest”. (See DECISIONS, p. 280.) (Damn Right!, p. 280)
- **AUTHOR — suspicion (a hospital).** The sign: a board that always backed the medical staff, whose decisions “frequently protected the economic interests of certain doctors”; a cardiovascular department “rife with political intrigue, turf protection, and not enough attention to medical issues.” (Damn Right!, pp. 291–292)
- **AUTHOR — suspicion (inflated claims).** The sign: other hospitals claiming earthquake damage for “every old crack in their cement”; Good Sam found no significant damage and Munger would not file. (Damn Right!, p. 293)
- **HIM — trust (a company run by a fanatic).** The sign: an early annual report in which “an idiot could tell that here was a talented fanatic-very favorably located.” (National Cash Register under Patterson.) What it meant: “Therefore, the investment decision was easy.” (Damn Right!, p. 311)
- **HIM — suspicion (an undefined number in a model).** The sign: a model that uses the cost of capital without saying what it is. Bratton: “In the model? It will just be stated.” Munger: “Where? Out of the forehead of job or something?” and “if you explain things in terms of unexplainable subconcepts, what kind of an explanation is that?” (The .txt prints “job”.) (Damn Right!, pp. 303–304)
- **HIM — what to ask of a business with fabulous results.** The sign: results that look fabulous. What it meant: ask how long they can last. “that's to think about why the results are occurring now-and then to figure out the forces that could cause those results to stop occurring.” (Damn Right!, p. 307)
- **HIM — a franchise, and its price.** The sign: a brand people reach for without thinking (Wrigley's). What it meant: “It's not worth it for you to think about buying an alternative gum.” The caution: when “everybody can see that they're wonderful businesses,” it trades at “eight times book value or something. And everything else is at three times book value.” (Damn Right!, p. 308)
- **AUTHOR (what he said he remembers) — suspicion (a business that looks invincible).** The sign: downtown department stores that “seemed invincible”: “They offered enormous selections, had large purchasing power, and owned the highest priced real estate in town, the corners where the streetcar lines crossed.” What it meant: “as time passed, private cars became the prevalent mode of transportation. The streetcar lines were taken out, customers moved to the suburbs and shopping centers became the dominant shopping venues.” AUTHOR: “Some simple changes in the way we live can completely alter the long-term value of a business.” (Damn Right!, p. 308)
- **AUTHOR + HIM + REPORTED (Bogle) — suspicion (layers of fees).** The sign: consultants hired to hire money managers who pick funds run by other money managers; AUTHOR: “At each step, there is a commission to be paid, which skims off the money that can be used in the work of the charity.” HIM: “And they get these fees just for maintaining shares in place”. REPORTED (Bogle, on Munger's point): “Market returns-5 percent; total cost, 3 percent; net return, 2 percent.” (Damn Right!, p. 312)
- **HIM — suspicion (the adviser's incentives).** The sign: a narrow professional adviser outside your own territory. What it meant: “It is not usually the conscious malfeasance of your narrow professional adviser that does you in. Instead, your troubles come from his subconscious bias. His cognition will often be impaired, for your purposes, by financial incentives different from yours.” (The .txt prints “cone” and “he impaired”.) (Damn Right!, pp. 356–357) Also in: HIM, Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 301 (Talk Four) and p. 325 (Talk Five).
- **HIM — suspicion (little corruptions).** The sign: accounting that hides a cost (stock options). What it meant: “it's very important not to let little corruptions start because they become big corruptions-and then you have vested interests that fight to perpetuate them.” (Damn Right!, p. 310) Also in: Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 388 (the editors' box, words attributed to Charlie): “The right way to behave is to never let improper accounting start.”
- **HIM — suspicion (passion in a thinker).** The sign: a professor or student of “super strong, passionate political ideology”. What it meant: “Best-form multidisciplinarity requires an objectivity such passionate people have lost, and a difficult synthesis is not likely to be achieved by minds in ideological fetters.” (Damn Right!, p. 352) Also in: HIM, Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 337–338 (Talk Five).
- **REPORTED (Molly) — trust.** “After something is checked out and you can feel safe, he gets very devoted to people,” (The opening quotation mark is lost in the .txt, so the attribution rests on the closing “said Molly”.) (Damn Right!, pp. 330–331)
- **HIM (as the author gives it) — suspicion (a shipyard).** The sign: trouble three months into the build at the original Florida yard. What he called it: “rogues, scallywags and pirates in Florida.” REPORTED (Williams): “I went down, things weren't going well,” (See DECISIONS, p. 326.) (Damn Right!, p. 326)
- **HIM — suspicion (a company's own choices, Coca-Cola).** The sign: the real company “did lose half its trademark and did grant perpetual bottling franchises at fixed syrup prices.” What it meant: it “did lose much pricing control that would have improved results, had it been retained.” (Damn Right!, p. 364) Also in: HIM, Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 313 (Talk Four).
- **HIM — suspicion (educated people who cannot explain a success).** The sign: educators “who can't satisfactorily explain Coca-Cola, even in retrospect, and even after watching it closely all their lives,” and executives who “did not understand elementary psychology well enough to predict and avoid the ‘New Coke’ fiasco”. (Damn Right!, p. 364) Also in: HIM, Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 314–315 (Talk Four).
- **AUTHOR — trust (a handshake).** Of Munger and Buffett: “There was no formal partnership or contractual relationship-the bond was created by a handshake and backed by two Midwesterners who understood and respected the value of one's word.” No quote from him. (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 23–24)
- **AUTHOR — trust (a likeness to Munger).** Dr. Davis, Munger's father's best friend, met Buffett and “agreed to place a large part of their life savings-$100,000-with him. Why? The doctor explained that Warren reminded him of Charlie Munger.” (The signal is Davis's, as the author tells it; no quote from Munger.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 19)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — trust (passion in a manager).** The sign: “Berkshire is full of people who have a peculiar passion for their own business. I would argue passion is more important than brain power.” (The .txt runs the first sentence into the question “What matters most passion or competence that was inborn?”.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 99) Also in: HIM — trust, The Complete Investor, p. 86 (book's label: Berkshire Annual Meeting, 2003).
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — trust (a manager who admits earlier error).** The sign: people he admires with power under him, “And they create the culture for whatever invention and reality recognition is going on in their businesses. And included in that reality recognition is the recognition that previous conclusions were incorrect.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 100)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — trust (old-fashioned businesses).** “A lot of the businesses we buy are kind of cranky and old-fashioned like us.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 97)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — suspicion (a CEO's duty is to make the stock go up).** The sign: “Today, it seems to be regarded as the duty of CEOs to make the stock go up This leads to all sorts of foolish behavior.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 98)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — suspicion (a seat waiting for deals).** The sign: sitting and waiting for the acquisition to come by. “Anytime you sit there waiting for a deal to come by, you're in a very dangerous seat.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 99)
- **AUTHOR (compilers' description of his screens) — suspicion (promoted deals).** “many investor favourites such as pharmaceuticals and Technology for example go straight to the too tough to understand basket”; “heavily promoted Deals And IPOs earn an immediate no.” (The .txt for this page is run together in places; quoted as printed. The compilers' wording, not his.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 71–72)
- **AUTHOR (compilers' description of his screens) — what he checks in management.** “He especially assesses a company's management well beyond conventional number crunching-in particular, the degree to which they are ‘able, trustworthy, and owner-oriented.’ For example, how do they deploy cash? Do they allocate it intelligently on behalf of the owners, or do they overcompensate themselves, or pursue ego-oriented growth for growth's sake?” (The compilers' words; the inner quote is as printed.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 74)
- **AUTHOR — what he asks of a cheap stock.** “Okay, it's a good company. But is the price low enough? Is the management made up of people Munger and Buffett Are comfortable with? If it is cheap enough to buy, is it cheap for the wrong reason or the right reason? What's the flip side? What can go wrong that I haven't seen?” (The compilers' words.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 78)
- **REPORTED (Gates) — suspicion (stock options, cost-plus contracts).** Gates on Munger's phrases: “When discussing venture capitalists who defend stock options, he deems them ‘no better than the piano player in a whorehouse.’ When discussing the deleterious effects on efficiency of cost-plus contracts, he likes to say even the mule knew to slow down.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 59)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — suspicion (a claim of synergy).** The sign: a promise of synergy. What it meant: “people generally claim more synergistic benefits than will come.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 101)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — trust (a manager).** The sign: Paul Arnold, CORT's CEO, of whom he says: “Paul's been running the business since he was in law school and loves it.” Munger had Buffett's word first: “You're going to love Paul Arnold [CORT's CEO].” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 101)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — suspicion (a CEO's or an activist's behavior).** The sign: an activist who says “having Warren on the board of Coke is contrary to the interests of Coke.” What it meant: “The cause of reform is hurt not helped when an activist makes an idiotic suggestion like saying that having Warren on the board of Coke is contrary to the interests of Coke. Nutty behavior undermines their cause.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 119)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — suspicion (an adviser's incentive: brokers).** The sign: brokers who switch clients between funds. What it meant: “costing another three to four percentage points.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 119)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — suspicion (managers who are crooks; the hard-to-see ones).** “Bernie Ebbers and Ken Lay were caricatures-they were easy to spot. They were almost psychopaths. But it's much harder to spot problems at companies like Royal Dutch [Shell].” “But we don't learn because I'd still expect that Exxon's figures are fair.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 118)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — suspicion (a business whose numbers keep surprising).** The sign: the same “extraordinary” charge every year. Quote: “If it happens every year like clockwork, what's so extraordinary about it?” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 119)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — suspicion (an investment bank).** The sign: a bank screening its clients less over time. The contrast he draws: “We did a $6 million deal years ago for Diversified Retailing, and we were rigorously and intelligently screened. The bankers cared and wanted to protect their clients.” Now: “The culture now is that anything that can be sold for a profit will be.” “An investment banker earning would be increased in a significant way if he wrote a few more tickets to Maxwell you have to control this if guys can make money by bringing dubious things in the door” (p. 122; the .txt is garbled and unpunctuated). (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 121–122)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — suspicion (an auditing firm).** The sign: selling tax shelters. What it meant: “tax shelters, with their contingency fees and secrecy, are a total abomination.” “You'll better understand the evil of top audit firms starting to sell fraudulent tax shelters when I tell you that one told me that they're better [than the others] because they only sold the schemes] to their top twenty clients, so no one would notice.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 123)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — suspicion (pension return assumptions).** The sign: expected pension returns of nine or ten percent. What it meant: “[Company leaders] may believe it-they're honest people-but subconsciously they believe it because they want to believe it. It makes earnings good so they can promote the stock.” Why accountants say nothing: “Whose bread I eAt, his song I sing.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 125)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — suspicion (EBITDA).** The sign: the term EBITDA. What it meant: “bullshit earnings.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 124)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — suspicion (a financial institution).** The sign: talk of how good its risk management is. Quote: “If they start talking about how good risk management is, it makes us nervous.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 128)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — suspicion (a derivatives book).** The test: “I think a good litmus test of the mental and moral quality at any large institution [with significant derivatives exposure] would be to ask them, "Do you really understand your derivatives book?" Anyone who says yes is either crazy or lying.” And: “You can't believe how big the incentives are to have wishful thinking about values and wishful thinking about ability to clear.” (p. 128) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 128–129)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — suspicion (earnings “blessed by accountants”).** “all kinds of earnings, blessed by accountants, are not really being earned.” (pp. 130–131) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 130–131)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — trust (a capital allocator).** The sign: Henry Singleton's record. AUTHOR (the compilers): “Charlie wonders, "Given the many talent and record, have we learned enough from him?"” HIM: “Henry Singleton has the best operating and capital deployment record in American business...if one took the 100 top business school graduates and made a composite of their triumphs, their record would not be as good as Singleton.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 138)
- **HIM (on Lou Simpson) — trust (an investor).** The sign: Simpson's record and what he did under pressure. “It's not unheard of to beat the market averages for a couple of years, maybe even five or ten years. But, imagine beating the S&P 500 by an average annual gain of 6.8 percent over twenty-five years!” “You can't believe the pressure that he was under, year after year, as the world seemed to be reaping enormous gains while he, correctly, avoided the bubble altogether, staying true to fundamentals. Lou was a wonderful example in that period-intelligent, honorable, and true to his fundamentals.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 147)
- **HIM (on Jim Sinegal) — trust (an operator).** “You just wouldn't believe how efficient and sophisticated Costco's warehouse operation is. Jim Sinegal is a fabulous business operator-like a Carnegie, Rockefeller, or James J. Hill.” “He works eighty hours a week and sets a terrific example for his entire organization in terms of work ethic, integrity, loyalty, and selflessness. He's a moral leader as well as a practical leader. These are not minor virtues.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 148)
- **HIM (Harvard School commencement, 1986) — trust (a person).** The sign: reliability. A college roommate, severely dyslexic: “perhaps the most reliable man I have ever known.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 154)
- **HIM (USC talk, 1994) — trust (an early-stage business).** The sign: John Patterson of National Cash Register. “Patterson was a small retail merchant who didn't make any money. One day, somebody sold him a crude cash register, which he put into his retail operation. And it instantly changed from losing money to earning a profit because it made it so much harder for the employees to steal.” He “got the best distribution system, the biggest collection of patents, and the best of everything. He was a fanatic about everything important as the technology developed.” “a well-educated orangutan could see that buying into partnership with Patterson in those early days, given his notions about the cash register business, was a total one hundred percent cinch.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 190–191) Also in: HIM (Talk Nine): “The cash register did more for human morality than the Congregational Church.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 444)
- **HIM (USC talk, 1994) — suspicion (a manager who does not want bad news).** The sign: Bill Paley at CBS. “But he didn't like to hear what he didn't like to hear, and people soon learned that. So they told Paley only what he liked to hear. Therefore, he was soon living in a little cocoon of unreality and everything else was corrupt although it was a great business.” “If people tell you what you really don't want to hear-what's unpleasant-there is an almost automatic reaction of antipathy. You have to train yourself out of it.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 181) Also in: AUTHOR — suspicion (Munger cited as the source of the example), The Complete Investor, p. 60.
- **HIM (USC talk, 1994) — suspicion (a big organization).** The signs: layers and “established way of thinking.” On Sears: “Sears had layers and layers of people it didn't need. It was very bureaucratic. It was slow, to think. And there was an established way of thinking. If you poked your head up with a new thought, the system kind of turned against you.” On departments sharing power: “If you won't bother me, I won't bother you, and we're both happy.” (pp. 179–180; the quote is on p. 180) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 180, 184)
- **HIM (USC talk, 1994) — suspicion (a capital-spending projection).** The sign: a salesman's or internal bureaucrat's projection of savings. What it meant: “In all cases, the people who sell the machinery-and, by and large, even the internal bureaucrats urging you to buy the equipment-show you projections with the amount you'll save at current prices with the new technology. However, they don't do the second step of the analysis-which is to determine how much is going to stay home and how much is just going to flow through to the customer.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 189)
- **HIM (USC talk, 1994) — suspicion (an industry).** The sign: a commodity with a rival like “a demented Kellogg.” What it meant: “In some businesses, the participants behave like a demented Kellogg. In other businesses, they don't. Unfortunately, I do not have a perfect model for predicting how that's going to happen.” “If it's a pure commodity like airline seats, you can understand why no one makes any money.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 185–186)
- **HIM (USC talk, 1994) — suspicion (investment advisers).** The sign: an adviser's incentive. What it meant: “So what makes sense for the investor is different from what makes sense for the manager. And, as usual in human affairs, what determines the behavior are incentives for the decision maker.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 200)
- **HIM — suspicion.** The sign: anything sold with a tax shelter, or “a big commission and a 200 page prospectus.” What it meant: don't buy it. Quote: “Anytime somebody offers you a tax shelter from here on in life, my advice would be don't buy it.” (p. 212) “anytime anybody offers you anything with a big commission and a 200 page prospectus, don't buy it.” (p. 213) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 212–213)
- **HIM — suspicion (experts whose advice pays them).** The sign: two sets of engineers, each recommending a course “very advantageous to itself.” What it meant: distrust the recommendation; look for a third opinion. Quote: “I saw huge reasons in the circumstances for bias in each set of engineers as each recommended a course of action very advantageous to itself. And what each was saying was so consonant with a natural bias that it made me distrust it.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 270–271)
- **HIM — suspicion (the confident non-expert).** The sign: people who always answer questions confidently without knowing. What it meant: he gets rid of them. Quote: “I try to get rid of people who always confidently answer questions about which they don't have any real knowledge. To me, they're like the bee dancing its incoherent dance. They're just screwing up the hive.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 272) Also in: HIM — suspicion, The Complete Investor, p. 71 (book's label: Wesco Meeting Notes, 1998).
- **HIM — suspicion (a system that rewards cheating).** The sign: easy-to-claim injuries and referral schemes in workers' compensation and auto accidents. What it meant: the cheating spreads and is very hard to stop. Quote: “And he got two chiropractors cards and one lawyer's card before he'd even left the intersection. They're in the business of manufacturing claims that necks hurt.” (p. 283) The plant, as printed (p. 251): “massive fraud in the workers compensation system to the point that is premium reached double digit percentages of payroll” The result of moving: “So my friend closed his plant and moved the work to Utah among a community of believing Mormons.” “It's two percent of payroll” (p. 252) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 251–252, 283)
- **HIM — suspicion (a flood of imitators).** The sign: other institutions rushing to copy Harvard and Yale, “driven by envy and salesmen,” and the earlier venture-capital rush. What it meant: “a lot of future adversity.” Quote: “foreseeing a lot of future adversity for other worthy institutions, driven by envy and salesmen into enthusiastic imitation of Harvard and Yale” (p. 222) “caused about $90 billion to rush into low-quality, imitative early stage ventures that by now may have created as much as $45 billion in net losses for late-coming investors.” (p. 222) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 222)
- **HIM — suspicion (an executive who postpones the unpleasant disclosure).** The sign: Gutfreund, told by his general counsel that he should tell the federal authorities about Salomon's trading improprieties. What it meant: it did not get done. Quote: “The task was easy to put off-because it was unpleasant. So that's what Gutfreund did-he put it off.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 278)
- **HIM — suspicion (success that makes a company think it knows everything).** The sign: GEICO “making a lot of money.” What it meant: foolish things and huge losses; the sound business underneath could be saved by cutting the folly. Quote: “They got to thinking that, because they were making a lot of money, they knew everything. And they suffered huge losses.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 217) Also in: HIM — suspicion, The Complete Investor, p. 62 (book's label: USC Business School, 1994).
- **HIM — suspicion (an industry's record with shareholders).** The sign: the airline business. What it meant: he did not buy the common; he lent against a preferred. Quote: “the history of the airline business in terms of taking care of shareholders has been terrible.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 258–259)
- **HIM — suspicion (being the only deep pocket).** The sign: a product that can injure a customer, from a rich corporation. What it meant: “bad business.” Quote: “There are businesses-given the way the civilization has developed-where being the only deep pocket around is bad business.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 282)
- **HIM — suspicion (heavy ideology in a thinker).** The sign: a mind “absolutely sure.” What it meant: a distorted cognition, even in a genius. Quote: “If ideology can screw up the head of Chomsky, imagine what it does to people like you and me.” (p. 237) “But a heavy ideology that makes you absolutely sure that the minimum wage should be raised or that it shouldn't-and it's kind of a holy construct where you know you're right-makes you a bit nuts.” (p. 238) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 237–238)
- **HIM — suspicion (advisers' interest in the problem).** The sign: law firms that cluck at litigation while it pays them. What it meant: they are “somewhat schizophrenic on the subject.” Quote: “But, really, they're somewhat schizophrenic on the subject because it's been very good for [them].” “Part of the defense bar lobbied quietly against certain propositions and, effectively, against their clients because they didn't want their clients to catch 'em in the process.” “And the reason that they did so was because it became harder for plaintiffs to bring cases.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 280)
- **HIM — what he noticed (an adviser whose career rides on one person).** The sign: Salomon's general counsel, with “very little constituency within Salomon except for the CEO.” What it meant: his career depended on the CEO. Quote: “And the general counsel had very little constituency within Salomon except for the CEO. If the CEO went down, the general counsel was going down with him.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 278)
- **HIM — trust (untapped pricing power).** The sign: a business where “any manager could raise the return enormously just by raising prices-and here they haven't done it.” What it meant: “the ultimate no-brainer”; Disney, See's Candy, Coca-Cola. Quote: “So they have huge untapped pricing power that they're not using. That is the ultimate no-brainer.” (p. 214) “we invested in Coca-Cola-which had some untapped pricing power. And it also had brilliant management.” (p. 215) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 214–215)
- **HIM — trust (owners of integrity, one winner left).** The sign: the Washington Post: top hand in a game with one winner, and “management with a lot of integrity and intelligence.” What it meant: “That one was a real dream.” Quote: “They're very high class people-the Katharine Graham family. That's why it was a dream-an absolute. damn dream.” (the .txt prints a period after “absolute”.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 216)
- **HIM — trust (a stable, hard-to-copy product).** The sign: Hershey, which “knew enough when they wanted to expand into Canada to know they shouldn't change their winning flavor”; International Flavors and Fragrances, which gets royalties on what can't be patented. What it meant: “One of my favorite business stories comes from Hershey.” (p. 230) Quote: “Well, it took them five years to duplicate their own flavor.” (p. 231) “It's the only company I know that does something on which you can't get a copyright or a patent, but which nevertheless receives a permanent royalty.” (p. 231) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 230–231)
- **HIM — trust (managers he admires).** The sign: managers at Berkshire he and Buffett “love and admire.” What it meant: the culture is theirs, and Berkshire does little. Quote: “By and large, we've chosen people we admire enormously to have the power beneath us. It's easy for us to get along with them on average because we love and admire them. And they create the culture for whatever invention and reality recognition is going on in their businesses.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 256–257)
- **HIM — trust (a tail wind he did not have to create).** The sign: Disney's old movies already “in the can,” with the copyright, when the videocassette arrived. What it meant: “that's a marvelous model if you can find it.” (p. 262) Quote: “They had all those movies in the can. They owned the copyright.” “All you have to do is to sit there while the world carries you forward.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 261–262)
- **HIM — suspicion (layers of consultants and analysts).** The sign: foundations with “many investment counselors, chosen by an additional layer of consultants,” who rely in turn on investment-bank analysts. What it meant: a cost that “can easily reach three percent of foundation net worth per annum.” Quote: “Now, in some foundations, there are not few but many investment counselors, chosen by an additional layer of consultants who are hired to decide which investment counselors are best, help in allocating funds to various categories, make sure that foreign securities are not neglected in favor of domestic securities, check validity of claimed investment records, ensure that claimed investment styles are scrupulously followed, and help augment an already large diversification in a way that conforms to the latest notions of corporate finance professors about volatility and ‘beta.’” (p. 348) “These security analysts receive enormous salaries, sometimes set in seven figures after bidding wars. The hiring investment banks recoup these salaries from two sources: (1) commissions and trading spreads borne by security buyers (some of which are rebated as ‘soft dollars’ to money managers) plus (2) investment banking charges paid by corporations that appreciate the enthusiastic way their securities are being recommended by the security analysts.” (p. 349) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 348–349)
- **HIM — suspicion (an expert who rates himself above average).** The sign: a seller of investment advice judging his own prospects. What it meant: “people who are successfully selling something, as investment counselors do, make Swedish drivers sound like depressives.” Quote: “Virtually every investment expert's public assessment is that he is above average, no matter what is the evidence to the contrary.” (p. 351) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 351) Also in: HIM — suspicion, The Complete Investor, p. 62 (book's label: Speech to Foundation Officers, 1998).
- **HIM — suspicion (an excess of professionalism: General Motors' truck).** The sign: a company that trusted “fancy consumer surveys” over watching people use the product. What it meant: “Using fancy consumer surveys, its excess of professionalism, it concluded not to put a fourth floor in a truck designed to serve also as the equivalent of a comfortable five-passenger car.” (pp. 351–352; the .txt prints “fourth floor”) Its competitors “had actually seen five people enter and exit cars. Moreover, they had noticed that people were used to four doors in a comfortable five-passenger car and that biological creatures ordinarily prefer effort minimization in routine activities and don't like removals of long-enjoyed benefits.” (p. 352) Quote: “There are only two words that come instantly to mind in reviewing General Motors' horrible decision, which has blown many hundreds of millions of dollars. And one of those words is ‘oops.’” (p. 352) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 351–352)
- **HIM — suspicion (the financial scene that reminds you of Sodom and Gomorrah).** The sign: widespread corrupt accounting. What it meant: it “will eventually create bad long-term consequences.” Quote: “I suggest that when the financial scene starts reminding you of Sodom and Gomorrah, you should fear practical consequences even if you like to participate in what is going on.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 376)
- **HIM (Talk Eight, fiction; the editors say the founder is loosely based on Carl Braun, whom Charlie greatly admired) — trust (a founder's compensation and balance-sheet habits).** The sign, as he writes it: no stock options, pay tied to precise performance, no dividends, a fortress of cash. What it meant: “a golden age of sound values.” (p. 381) Quote: “There were no stock options because the old man had considered the accounting treatment required for stock options to be ‘weak, corrupt, and contemptible,’ and he no more wanted bad accounting in his business than he wanted bad engineering.” “Moreover, the old man believed in tailoring his huge incentive bonuses to precise performance standards established for individuals or small groups, instead of allowing what he considered undesirable compensation outcomes, both high and low, such as he believed occurred under other companies' stock option plans.” (p. 382) “Moreover, he loved his business and his coworkers and always wanted to have on hand large amounts of cash equivalents so as to be able to maximize workout or work-up chances if an unexpected adversity or opportunity came along.” (p. 383) “The old man's dividend policy for Quant Tech was simplicity itself He never paid a dividend. Instead, all earnings simply piled up in cash equivalents.” (p. 384; the .txt has no period after “itself”) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 381–384)
- **HIM (Talk Eight, fiction) — suspicion (a board that wants a higher stock price, and the officers it hires).** The sign: new officers recruited from a company with a conventional option plan and a higher multiple. What it meant: “modern financial engineering.” Quote: “Incident to the recruitment of the new executives, it was made plain that Quant Tech's directors wanted a higher market capitalization as soon as feasible.” (p. 385) “Therefore, the new officers were attracted to employing what they called ‘modern financial engineering,’ which required prompt use of any and all arguably lawful methods for driving up reported earnings, with big, simple changes to be made first” (p. 385; the .txt has no period) The alternative he offers, as printed: “A wiser board could then have bought in Quant Tech's stock very aggressively” (p. 384; the .txt continues in garbled form). (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 384–385)
- **HIM (Talk Eight, fiction) — suspicion (earnings that grow by the same percentage every year; cash that cannot square with the profit).** The sign: smoothness, a falling tax rate, a pile of cash. What it meant: manufactured earnings. Quote: the officers wanted “Quant Tech's reported earnings to go up by roughly the same percentage each year throughout the whole twenty years because they believed that financial analysts, representing institutional investors, would value Quant Tech's stock higher if reported annual earnings growth never significantly varied.” (p. 390) “They saw that as Quant tech's reported earnings, containing an increasing phony element, kept rising at twenty-eight percent, Quant Tech's income taxes as a percentage of reported pretax earnings kept going lower and lower. This plainly increased chances for causing undesired questions and criticism.” (p. 392) “Cash equivalents on hand now amounted to an astounding $85 billion, and somehow it didn't seem impossible to most investors that a company virtually drowning in so much cash could be earning the $16 billion it was reporting.” (p. 392) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 390–392)
- **HIM (Talk Eight, fiction) — suspicion (accountants who have already signed; critics dismissed as cranks).** The sign: auditors who bless a few statements. What it meant: they cannot turn back. Quote: “Third, the outside accountants, once they had blessed a few financial statements containing earnings increases, only a minority share of which were phony, would probably find it unendurably embarrassing not to bless new financial statements containing only the same phony proportion of reported earnings increase.” (p. 389) “No one criticized Quant techs financial reporting except a few people widely regarded as impractical, overly theoretical, misanthropic cranks.” (p. 391; the .txt prints “techs”) “The new CEO privately called the desirable, cautious approach ‘wisely restrained falsehood.’” (p. 387) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 387–391)
- **HIM — suspicion (a conclusion that follows the speaker's incentive).** The sign: a Nobel Prize-winning economist who explained Berkshire first as luck, then as skill. Quote: “when he finally got to six sigmas of luck, people were laughing so hard he stopped doing it.” “Then, he reversed the explanation 180 degrees. He said, ‘No, it was still six sigmas, but it was six sigmas of skill.’” (p. 403) What it meant, to him: “The man changed his silly view when his incentives made him change it and not before.” (p. 404) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 403–404)
- **HIM — suspicion (a reason given for keeping the old way).** The sign: the answer to his question why the Jules Stein Eye Institute still used an obsolete cataract operation: “Charlie, it's such a wonderful operation to teach.” What it meant: the operation served the teacher; it changed only after “almost all the patients had voted with their feet.” (p. 404) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 404)
- **HIM — suspicion (a consultant who reasons from a wrong theory).** The sign: the McKinsey partner who told the Washington Post not to repurchase. What it meant: “Because the stock price, by definition, is totally efficient, there could never be any advantage. QED.” “But he so believed what he'd been taught in graduate school that he told the Washington Post It shouldn't buy its own stock.” (p. 415; the .txt prints a capital “It”) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 415)
- **HIM — suspicion (a projection).** The sign: a precise forecast put on the desk. What it meant: it sells, it does not inform. Quote: “It's an effective sales technique in America to put a foolish projection on a desk.” (p. 437) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 437)
- **HIM — suspicion (a purchase that will pay for itself in three years).** The sign: a salesman's pitch for a new loom. What it meant: the saving goes to the customers, not the owner. Quote: “It'll pay for itself in three years at current prices because it adds so much efficiency to the production of textiles.” And the result: “you keep making four percent on capital; you never go anywhere.” (p. 436) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 436)
- **HIM — suspicion (a system that rewards the cheat).** The sign: the workers' compensation system. What it meant: “Gaming has been raised to an art form.” “In the course of gaming the system, people learn to be crooked.” (p. 437) And the sign in grading: “And, therefore, by custom, and as would be predicted from the psychological force called ‘reciprocity tendency,’ in a really advanced graduate course, the professors always gave an A.” (p. 439) Niederhoffer took only such courses; “That's a ridiculous story, but the scheme will work still.” (p. 439) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 437–439)
- **HIM — suspicion (a place where everybody steals).** The sign: a Latin American community in which “a culture had arisen wherein everybody stole everything.” What it meant: “the economy came practically to a halt.” (p. 434) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 434)
- **HIM — suspicion (a promise with no funding).** The sign: the unfunded pension plans at major law firms. What it meant: he calls it the tenth defect of economics, “Not Enough Attention to the Effects of Embedded Ponzi Schemes at the Microeconomic Level.” “a clear and enormous potential future impact goes unnoticed-and blissfully ignored.” (pp. 425–426) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 425–426)
- **HIM — suspicion (a derivatives book).** The sign: the books of derivatives houses. What it meant: “Running off a derivative book is agony and takes time. And you saw what happened when they tried to run off the derivative books at Enron. Its certified net worth vanished. In the derivative books of America, there are a lot of reported profits that were never earned and assets that never existed.” (p. 452) “You would think it was the Mad Hatter's Tea Party” (p. 452) “And the false precision of these people is just unbelievable. They make the worst economics professors look like gods. Moreover, there is depravity augmenting the folly.” (p. 453) “Read the book F.I.A.S.C.O., by law professor and former derivatives trader Frank Partnoy, an insider account of depravity in derivative trading at one of the biggest and best-regarded Wall Street firms. The book will turn your stomach.” (p. 453) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 452–453)
- **HIM — suspicion (a mutual-fund commission).** The sign: a higher commission paid by the fund. What it meant: “You're bribing the broker to betray his client and put the client's money into the high-commission product. This has worked to produce at least a trillion dollars of mutual fund sales.” (p. 427) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 427) Also in: HIM — suspicion, The Complete Investor, p. 40 (book's label: "UCS Business School, 2003" as printed).
- **HIM — suspicion (a graduate of business school).** The sign: the credential. What it meant, for the hotel-and-store puzzles: “We hire business school graduates, and they're no better at these problems than you were. Maybe that's the reason we hire so few of them.” (p. 421) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 421)
- **HIM — suspicion (the polished talker).** The sign: “They may have a big head of hair. They often have fine timbre in their voices. They make a big impression.” What it meant: “chauffeur knowledge masquerading as real knowledge.” (p. 475) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 475)
- **HIM — suspicion (a text that collects experiments).** The sign: the introductory psychology texts. What it meant: “written as if the authors were collecting psychology experiments as a boy collects butterflies-with a passion for more butterflies and more contact with fellow collectors and little craving for synthesis in what is already possessed.” (p. 490) On the academic who knows one thing: “many an academic is like the truffle hound, an animal so trained and bred for one narrow purpose that it is no good at anything else.” (p. 490; Jacob Viner's observation as Munger gives it) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 490)
- **HIM — suspicion (the salesman who studies the book).** The sign: Cialdini's readers. What it meant: “as an outcome not sought by Cialdini, who is a profoundly ethical man, a huge number of his books were bought by salesmen who wanted to learn how to become more effective in misleading customers.” “Please remember this perverse outcome when my discussion comes to incentive-caused bias as a consequence of the superpower of incentives.” (p. 492) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 492)
- **HIM — trust (a man who gave a year to a textbook).** The sign: Joseph M. Mirra, M.D., who “over years of disciplined work, made himself know more about bone tumor pathology than almost anyone else in the world.” What it meant: values he wanted to be near. Quote: “He worked seventeen hours a day, seven days a week, for a year. Some sabbatical.” “When you're around values like Mirra's, you want to pick up as much as you can.” (pp. 461–462) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 461–462)
- **HIM — trust (people who rise).** The sign: “people rise in life who are not the smartest, sometimes not even the most diligent. But they are learning machines.” (p. 460) “Not much fancy procedure, just totally reliable people correctly trusting one another.” (p. 479) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 461, 479)
- **AUTHOR (the editors; the .txt does not say who spoke the lesson) — suspicion of the clever.** The sign: the cognitive-assessment test Graham gave his followers in San Diego. What it meant, as the editors give it: “No matter how smart you are, there are smart people out there who can fool you if they really want to. So, be sure you can trust the smart people you work with.” The editors introduce it: “Charlie recalls a meeting of the ‘Ben Graham Group’ in San Diego many years ago.” (p. 456) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 456)
- **HIM — suspicion (an advisor who is paid by the answer).** The sign: the surgeon in Lincoln, Nebraska, who “over the years sent bushel baskets full of normal gall bladders down to the pathology lab in the leading hospital in Lincoln, Nebraska” (p. 505) What it meant to him: not a conscious fraud. His question to the doctor friend who helped remove him: “Did this surgeon think, ‘Here's a way for me to exercise my talents’-this guy was very skilled technically-‘and make a high living by doing a few maimings and murders every year in the course of routine fraud?’” The friend's answer, REPORTED within his telling: “Hell no, Charlie. He thought that the gallbladder was the source of all medical evil, and, if you really loved your patients, you couldn't get that organ out rapidly enough.” (pp. 505–506) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 505–506)
- **HIM — suspicion (the presentation of the seller; the consultant's report).** The sign: brokers selling commercial real estate and businesses, and management consultants. What it meant: “Consider the presentations of brokers selling commercial real estate and businesses. I've never seen one that I thought was even within hailing distance of objective truth.” “In my long life, I have never seen a management consultant's report that didn't end with the same advice: “This problem needs more management consulting services.”” (p. 506) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 506)
- **HIM — suspicion (profit recognized at the start of a risky loan).** The sign: Westinghouse's accounting for hotel construction loans, “accounting that made its lending officers look good because it showed extremely high starting income from loans that were very inferior to the loans from which the company had suffered small credit losses in the past.” (p. 510) What it meant: “a nearly insane incentive structure, almost sure to trigger incentive-caused bias in a lending officer” (p. 510) And similarly, “a silly computer program in place that allowed a bond trader to show immense fictional profits” at Kidder Peabody. (p. 511) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 510–511)
- **HIM — suspicion (a group made up of people like themselves).** The sign: an organization that picks new members “very much like themselves.” What it meant: “if the English department at an elite university becomes mentally dysfunctional or the sales department of a brokerage firm slips into routine fraud, the problem will have a natural tendency to get worse and to be quite resistant to change for the better.” (pp. 557–558) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 557–558)
- **HIM — suspicion (excuses for fixable performance).** The sign: a man giving excuses instead of the fix. What it meant: “fixable but unfixed bad performance is bad character and tends to create more of itself” (p. 561) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 561)
- **HIM — suspicion (the polished interviewee).** The sign: “a job candidate who is a marvelous ‘presenter’” What it meant: “often causes great danger under modern executive-search practice.” His example: Hewlett-Packard and Carly Fiorina, “the articulate, dynamic Carly Fiorina”. (p. 560) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 560)
- **HIM — suspicion (the small favor, to the employee who buys with someone else's money).** The sign: “an automobile salesman graciously steers you into a comfortable place to sit and gives you a cup of coffee” (p. 540). What it meant: “you are very likely being tricked, by this small courtesy alone, into parting with an extra five hundred dollars.” For a purchasing agent: “the salesman is often able to maximize his advantage, particularly when government is the purchaser.” (pp. 540–541) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 540–541)
- **HIM — suspicion (a higher price and a new trade dress).** The sign: an ordinary industrial product with a changed “trade dress” and a significantly higher price. What it meant: sellers “hoping that quality-seeking buyers will be tricked into becoming purchasers by mere association of his product and its high price.” (p. 547) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 547)
- **HIM — suspicion (a manager who punishes the messenger).** The sign: Chairman Paley, “hostile to people who brought him bad news.” What it meant: “Paley lived in a cocoon of unreality, from which he made one bad deal after another” (p. 552). The same pattern in a lawyer afraid to give a client an unwelcome settlement, and in “some general counsel's unwillingness to carry bad news to a strong-minded CEO.” (p. 552) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 552)
- **HIM — trust (people who fear not telling you the bad news).** The sign: the Berkshire injunction “Always tell us the bad news promptly. It is only the good news that can wait.” (p. 553) What it meant: “It also helps to be so wise and informed that people fear not telling you bad news because you are so likely to get it elsewhere.” (p. 553) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 553)
- **HIM — suspicion (one's own judgment of a competitor one dislikes).** The sign: appraisals of competitors by people who dislike them. What it meant: “people under appraise both the competency and morals of competitors they dislike.” (p. 551) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 551)
- **HIM — suspicion (a group that rejects all conflicting input).** The sign: “University Liberal arts departments, law schools, and business organizations” that “display plenty of such ideology-based group think that rejects almost all conflicting inputs.” (pp. 566–567) What it meant: “extremes of cognitive dysfunction.” (p. 567) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 566–567)
- **HIM — suspicion (a board that never objects).** The sign: outside directors who “fail to object to anything much short of an axe murder until some public embarrassment of the board finally causes their intervention.” (p. 577) Rosenfield's line on Northwest Bell: “They asked me if I wanted to become a director of Northwest Bell, and it was the last thing they ever asked me.” (p. 577) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 577)
- **HIM — suspicion (a business imitating the others).** The sign: “If one oil company foolishly buys a mine, other oil companies often quickly join in buying mines.” (p. 574) What it meant: “bad decisions that were quickly triggered by discomfort from doubt.” (p. 574) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 574)
- **HIM — suspicion (a sales method of showing three bad houses first).** The sign: “The salesman deliberately shows the customer three awful houses at ridiculously high prices.” What it meant: the “merely bad house at a price only moderately too high” is then an easy sale. (p. 580) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 580)
- **HIM — suspicion (an executive who spends on himself).** The sign: “A psychology Ph. D. once became a CEO of a major company and went wild, creating an expensive new headquarters, with a great wine cellar, at an isolated site.” (p. 594) What it meant: the order “Take the money out of the depreciation reserves,” — “Not too easy because a depreciation reserve is a liability account.” (p. 594) “So strong is undue respect for authority that this CEO, and many even worse examples, have actually been allowed to remain in control of important business institutions for long periods after it was clear they should be removed.” (p. 595) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 594–595)
- **HIM — trust (a boss who gives the reason).** The sign: Carl Braun's rule that every written order carry “Who was to do What, Where, When, and Why.” (p. 597) What it meant: Braun “designed oil refineries with spectacular skill and integrity”, and “was likely to fire you” for leaving out the why. (p. 597) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 597)
- **HIM — trust (a man proud of being trustworthy).** The sign: “justified pride in being trustworthy.” What it meant: “the trustworthy man, even after allowing for the inconveniences of his chosen course, ordinarily has a life that averages our better than he would have if he provided less reliability.” (p. 563; “our” as printed) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 563)
- **HIM — trust (a man who is admired, and loved for it).** The sign: Warren's uncle, Fred Buffett, “who cheerfully did the endless grocery-store work that Warren and I ended up admiring from a safe distance.” (p. 519) What it meant to him: “Even now, after I have known so many other people, I doubt if it is possible to be a nicer man than Fred Buffett was, and he changed me for the better.” (p. 519) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 519)
- **HIM — trust (a purchasing rule with no exceptions).** The sign: Sam Walton, who “wouldn't let purchasing agents accept so much as a hot dog from a vendor.” (p. 541) What it meant: “this policy of Walton's was profoundly correct.” (p. 542) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 541–542)
- **HIM — suspicion (smart people paying up in a liquid market).** The sign: Colin Camerer's experiment: “Colin Camerer of Caltech, who works in "experimental economics," devised an interesting experiment in which he caused high I.Q. students, playing for real money, to pay price A+B for a "security" they knew would turn into A dollars at the end of the day.” (p. 608) What it meant: “This foolish action occurred because the students were allowed to trade with each other in a liquid market for the security. And some students then paid price A+B because they hoped to unload on other students at a higher price before the day was over.” (p. 608) His prediction about the professors: “most economics and corporate finance professors who still believe in the "hard-form efficient market hypothesis" will retain their original belief. If so, this will be one more indication of how irrational smart people can be” (p. 608; the quote stops where the .txt breaks the word “psycho logical”) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 608)
- **HIM — trust (a company that revisits its own acquisitions).** The sign: “The use of autopsy equivalents at Johnson & Johnson.” (p. 606) What it meant to him: “At most corporations, if you make an acquisition and it turns out to be a disaster, all the people, paperwork, and presentations that caused the foolish acquisition are quickly forgotten. Nobody wants to be associated with the poor outcome by mentioning it. But at Johnson & Johnson, the rules make everybody revisit old acquisitions, comparing predictions with outcomes. That is a very smart thing to do.” (p. 606) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 606)
- **HIM — trust (a researcher who destroys her own ideas: Judith Rich Harris).** The .txt of p. 612 is garbled (it reads as dictated, with OCR errors such as “Raigad,” “board,” “miss Harris,” “sides”); quotes are as printed and nothing is repaired. The sign: “her dogged curiosity and Raigad in dealing with this question remind me of board Darwin and Sherlock Holmes” (p. 612). “Well, by miss Harris own account, she was impertinent and skeptical even as a child. And these qualities + patient, determined skill have obviously served her truth seeking well,” (p. 612) “she was also assisted by her enthusiasm in destroying her own ideas as she now demonstrate by apologizing for her former work as a text book writer who repeated wrong notions now outgrown” (p. 612). (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 612)
- **AUTHOR (the editor's list, not Munger's reasons) — trust (books under his name).** The Almanack closes with “Charlie Munger's Recommended Books” (p. 613) and prints his statement “In my whole life, I have known no wise people (over a broad subject matter area) who didn't read all the time-none, zero.” (p. 613; also in the Tilson-notes entry in RULES above) The twenty titles listed under his name, titles only, with no reasons from him: Deep Simplicity (Gribbin); F.I.A.S.C.O. (Partnoy); Ice Age (John & Mary Gribbin); How the Scots Invented the Modern World (Herman); Models of My Life (Simon) (pp. 613–614); A Matter of Degrees (Segre); Andrew Carnegie (Wall); Guns, Germs, and Steel (Diamond); The Third Chimpanzee (Diamond); Influence (Cialdini) (p. 614); The Autobiography of Benjamin Franklin; Living Within Limits (Hardin); The Selfish Gene (Dawkins); Titan (Chernow); The Wealth and Poverty of Nations (Landes); The Warren Buffett Portfolio (Hagstrom); Genome (Ridley); Getting to Yes (Fisher, Ury, Patton); Three Scientists and Their Gods (Wright); Only the Paranoid Survive (Grove) (p. 615). The three after “And a few from your editor...” (Schwab, Firestone, Stone, p. 616) are the editor's, not his. (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 613–616)
- **HIM — suspicion** — The sign: a projection from someone who has a stake in the outcome. What it meant: the number is probably wrong and the forecaster probably believes it. Quote: “[Projections] are put together by people who have an interest in a particular outcome, have a subconscious bias, and its apparent precision makes it fallacious.” “Projections should be handled with care, particularly when they're being provided by someone who has an interest in misleading you.” (The Complete Investor, pp. 27, 60; book's label: Buffett Speaks, 2007; Berkshire Annual Meeting, 1995)
- **HIM — suspicion** — The sign: a smart investor who promises institutional clients 20 percent. What it meant: he knows it is impossible; he says it because a lower number gets him no money. Quote: “I asked him, ‘What returns do you tell your institutional clients you will earn for them?’ He said, ‘20 percent.’ I couldn't believe it, because he knows that's impossible. But he said, ‘Charlie, if I gave them a lower number, they wouldn't give me any money to invest!’ The investment-management business is insane.” (The Complete Investor, pp. 50–51; book's label: Jason Zweig Interview, 2014)
- **HIM — suspicion** — The sign: complexity. What it meant: room for fraud and error. Quote: “Where you have complexity, by nature you can have fraud and mistakes.” (The Complete Investor, p. 45)
- **HIM — suspicion** — The sign: every major company doing the same thing because the others are (oil companies buying fertilizer companies). What it meant: social proof, no reason behind it. Quote: “And there was no more damned reason for all these oil companies to buy fertilizer companies, but they didn't know exactly what to do, and if Exxon was doing it, it was good enough for Mobil, and vice versa.” (The Complete Investor, pp. 64–65; book's label: Harvard University, 1995)
- **HIM — suspicion** — The sign: a good idea pushed to excess, with experts agreeing. What it meant: brains turn to mush. Quote: “It's so easy for us all to push a really good idea to wretched excess, as in the case of the Florida land bubble or the ‘nifty fifty’ corporate stocks. Then mix in a little ‘social-proof’ (from other experts), and brains (including ours) often turn to mush.” (The Complete Investor, p. 65; book's label: Harvard University, 1995)
- **HIM — suspicion** — The sign: a business that earns 12 percent but whose excess cash must all be reinvested. What it meant: there is never any cash. Quote: “The second earns 12 percent, but all the excess cash must be reinvested—there's never any cash. It reminds me of the guy who looks at all of his equipment and says, ‘There's all of my profit.’ We hate that kind of business.” (The Complete Investor, p. 95; book's label: Berkshire Annual Meeting, 2003)
- **HIM — suspicion** — The sign: a great investor buying into a field because others are making money in it (Soros and technology). What it meant: his sentence says only that he “got killed”; the author introduces it as an example of one of the world's greatest investors stepping outside his circle of competence (p. 99). Quote: “Soros couldn't bear to see others make money in the technology sector without him, and he got killed.” (The Complete Investor, p. 99; book's label: Berkshire Annual Meeting, 2000)
- **HIM — suspicion** — The sign: an auction with open outcry. What it meant: social proof, reciprocation and deprival at once. Quote: “Well, the open-outcry auction is just made to turn the brain into mush: you've got social proof, the other guy is bidding, you get reciprocation tendency, you get deprival super-reaction syndrome, and the thing is going away.” (The Complete Investor, p. 73; book's label: Harvard University, 1995)
- **AUTHOR — suspicion** — The sign: academics who love the beauty of mathematics. What it meant, per the author: they ignore that the assumptions are flawed. “Another example that Munger has cited for the psychological denial tendency at work is academics who love the beauty of mathematics.” (The Complete Investor, p. 61)
- **AUTHOR — suspicion (the author's account of Munger's reasons)** — The sign: risk presented as volatility by money managers and academics. Per the author, Munger's reasons: “Munger believes this happens because (1) it helps make their mathematics beautiful, even though it has little tie to reality, and (2) it can lead to attractive consulting contracts with money management firms.” (The Complete Investor, pp. 91–92)
- **HIM — trust** — The sign: a builder who stands under his own arch. What it meant: a really responsible system. Quote: “An example of a really responsible system is the system the Romans used when they built an arch. The guy who created the arch stood under it as the scaffolding was removed. It's like packing your own parachute.” (The Complete Investor, p. 51; book's label: Berkshire Annual Meeting, 1993)
- **HIM — trust** — The sign: high returns on capital, reinvested at high returns. What it meant: the company will do well. Quote: “It's obvious that if a company generates high returns on capital and reinvests at high returns, it will do well.” (The Complete Investor, p. 70; book's label: Wesco Annual Meeting, 2000)
- **HIM — trust** — The sign: a great business with lumpy results. What it meant: in his example, great businesses can have very volatile returns (See's loses money in two quarters of each year) and terrible businesses can have steady results. Quote: “Some great businesses have very volatile returns—for example, See's Candies usually loses money in two quarters of each year—and some terrible businesses can have steady results.” (The Complete Investor, p. 91; book's label: Berkshire Annual Meeting, 1997)
- **AUTHOR — trust (a number)** — The sign: math that is simple, clear and positive. Per the author: “Munger wants the math involved in evaluating an investment to be simple, overpoweringly clear, and positive.” (The Complete Investor, p. 96)
- **HIM — trust (a colleague)** — The sign: somebody trusted to talk to. What it meant: you organize your own thoughts as you try to convince others. Quote: “I hardly know anybody who's done very well in life in terms of cognition that doesn't have somebody trusted to talk to.” (The Complete Investor, p. 88; book's label: CNBC Interview, 2014)

## 5. TEMPERAMENT IN ACTION

- **HIM** — On being a footnote. Epigraph, when first named to the Forbes list in 1993: “I've been associated with Warren (Buffett) so long, I thought I'd be just a footnote.” (Damn Right!, p. 17) Also in: AUTHOR, “While he doesn't mind the wealth, he regrets having his name on any such list. Despite his healthy self-image, Charlie would prefer to be anonymous.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 26)
- **REPORTED (Katharine Graham)** — Letters over about ten years. “He was reassuring me that I was doing better than I thought.” (Damn Right!, p. 18)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — First meeting Buffett, 1959. HIM: “I knew everyone in the Buffett family except Warren,” and “I would have to say that I recognized almost instantly what a remarkable person Warren is.” AUTHOR: “he did not have particularly high expectations about meeting him”; “Charlie was so wrapped up in what he was saying that when he raised his glass to sip his drink, he held his other hand up to stop anyone else from interrupting the conversation.” (Damn Right!, p. 19)
- **HIM** — What he wanted from money. “Not because I wanted Ferraris-I wanted the independence. I desperately wanted it. I thought it was undignified to have to send invoices to other people.” (Damn Right!, p. 20)
- **REPORTED (Buffett's Foreword)** — “In 41 years, I have never seen Charlie try to take advantage of anyone, nor have I seen him claim the least bit of credit for anything that he didn't do.” “He has knowingly let me and others have the better end of a deal, and he has also always shouldered more than his share of the blame when things go wrong and accepted less than his share of credit when the reverse has been true.” p. 8: “Charlie judges himself entirely by an inner scorecard-and he is a tough grader.” “we have never in our entire friendship had an argument nor found disagreement a reason to be disagreeable.” (Buffett on Munger's conduct; no Buffett reasoning recorded.) (Damn Right!, pp. 7–8)
- **AUTHOR** — At the Berkshire meeting: “Charlie sits there looking as if he'd already been chiseled into Mount Rushmore, and gives a brusque reply. “Nothing to add.”” (Damn Right!, p. 10) Also in: AUTHOR (Tilson): “Warren generally takes the first stab at answering a question, but then usually turns and says, ‘Charlie?’” and “Charlie typically replies, ‘I have nothing to add.’” “at the special meeting for the Gen Re acquisition in September 1998, Warren actually showed up with a cardboard cutout of Charlie and a recording of Charlie saying, ‘I have nothing to add.’” REPORTED (Emilie Ogden, p. 51): “His hands are crossed in front of him, as he shakes his head saying, ‘I have nothing to add.’” HIM (as Tilson gives it): “cranky, old fashioned”. (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 51, 92–93)
- **AUTHOR** — The biography. Munger “didn't say much except that he didn't think the book would sell many copies.” Then, in a loud voice at the Wesco meeting: “Is Janet Lowe here?” and “Follow me,” then in an office: the family “didn't want a biography of him.” And: “All right then,” Munger barked. “You can start by reading these books.” (Damn Right!, pp. 10–11)
- **REPORTED (Hal Borthwick)** — On the Berkshire stage persona. “It's true, probably, he doesn't have a lot to add and he wants to get on with it.” and “that isn't the real Charlie Munger that's sitting up there.” (Damn Right!, p. 22)
- **AUTHOR + REPORTED** — Bridge, boats. AUTHOR: “His partners sometimes have a little trouble understanding why he makes certain bids or plays certain cards, though usually he is following some simple logic of his own.” REPORTED (Molly): “My dad is a pushover for anyone with a wild idea for a boat,” (Damn Right!, p. 23)
- **AUTHOR + REPORTED (Buffett)** — Associated Cotton Shops, 1967. “Suddenly Buffett looked around and discovered he was talking to himself. Charlie was gone. Later Buffett learned that Munger remembered he had to catch a plane, and just walked off.” REPORTED (Buffett): “He is a sensational friend. The niceties are not there. None of the superficial acts, but all of the real ones.” “If you talk about ideas, he's not going to get emotional about it. But if he has superior facts or reasoning, he won't back off.” (Damn Right!, p. 25)
- **AUTHOR** — Ordering from television. Munger, “a certified billionaire,” had “newfangled lures” ordered “from a television fishing show.” (Damn Right!, p. 28)
- **HIM** — The rat and the hamsters. “I'd already sensed her aversion to rodents, so I picked it up and said, ‘Mother, what's this?’ and waved it in front of her.” “It was the only time she took out after me with a coat hanger,” “The idea was getting a bigger buck, or a hamster with unusual coloration, or something like that,” AUTHOR: “when one of them died, he wanted to keep it in the refrigerator.” (Damn Right!, p. 32) Also in: AUTHOR, “At home, Charlie developed a fondness for raising hamsters and periodically traded them with other children. Even at an early age, Charlie showed sagacious negotiating ability and usually gained a bigger specimen or one with unusual coloring.” and “When his brood grew to thirty five animals, his mother ordered an end to his hobby because of pungent odor from his basement hamster farm.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 10–11)
- **HIM** — Fishing. Tribute to J.D. Ramsey: “I have seen a lot of peculiar fishermen in my life who, like me, are willing to suffer to fish in promising water,” (Damn Right!, p. 35)
- **REPORTED (Barry, son)** — “He tries to find the best technique day in and day out and will stick with that lure, or whatever, even if others on the boat are having better success with something else.” (Damn Right!, p. 36)
- **REPORTED (Guerin) + HIM** — The sunk boat. REPORTED: Munger insisted on driving; put it in reverse; Guerin yelled and Charlie replied, “I'll take care of that.” and put on full power, still in reverse; the boat sank. HIM: “The borrowed boat, explained Charlie, was not designed so as to keep the water from rushing in when the boat was going backward.” and “Even when I took him fishing in Minnesota and upset the boat and we had to swim to shore, he didn't scream at me,” (Damn Right!, p. 39) Also in: REPORTED (Buffett): “Charlie managed to sink a boat with no outside help whatsoever.” REPORTED (Guerin): “the time to negotiate a reward for rescuing someone is before, not after you save them” and “I call Charlies catamaran ‘Munger's Folly.’ It's the only completely irrational thing Charlie has ever done.” (the .txt prints “Charlies”); HIM (as Broggie gives it): “The boat was fun to design and build. What's irrational about that?” REPORTED (Gates, repeating Buffett's warning): “managed to sink a boat in a totally calm lake with no other traffic by going full speed in reverse with a low transom on the stern.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 26–27, 59)
- **HIM** — After that: “After dunking him in the lake, we tried to cheer him up by making him watch a bunch of high school students perform Moliere in a Bemidji tent.” (Damn Right!, p. 40)
- **AUTHOR** — Home. “They can take the boy out of Omaha but not Omaha out of the boy,” (Munger “paraphrases an old saying”). (Damn Right!, p. 42)
- **HIM** — His grandfather, the judge. “He wanted not to be poor,” “Self-sufficiency and hard work would be his salvation. My grandparents thought Robinson Crusoe was a great moral work.” “That generation admired the conquering of nature through discipline.” (Damn Right!, p. 44) Also in: HIM (Talk Ten): “My grandfather Munger was the only federal judge in his city for nearly forty years. And I admired him. I'm his namesake.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 478)
- **AUTHOR + REPORTED** — Aunt Oofie. AUTHOR: “Her nephew Charlie adored Oofie, partly because her standards were so extreme that she amused him.” REPORTED (Oofie, to Charlie, on her father's death): “It was God's grace to take judge, knowing he wouldn't have wanted to stay on and make errors.” AUTHOR: “even Charlie was floored” by it. (Damn Right!, p. 46)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — His father. AUTHOR: “For years after his father died, Charlie carried Al Munger's briefcase to work.” HIM: “was one of the happiest men who ever lived and achieved exactly what he wished to achieve, no more or less.
