- **HIM (Talk Nine)** — Praise and disagreement in one breath: Krugman. “If you take Paul Krugman and read his essays, you will be impressed by his fluency. I can't stand his politics; I'm on the other side. But I love this man's essays. I think Paul Krugman is one of the best essayists alive.” (p. 410) On Keynes: “whom I quote all the time and who has added a great amount of illumination to my life.” (p. 410) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 410)
- **HIM (Talk Nine)** — George Shultz declines to think about it. Munger had put his China / Ricardo argument to economists three times; “The best answer I ever got on that subject-in three tries-was from George Shultz.” (p. 441) Shultz: “Charlie, the way I figure it is if we stop trading with China, the other advanced nations will do it anyway, and we wouldn't stop the ascent of China compared to us, and we'd lose the Ricardo-diagnosed advantages of trade.” Munger: “Which is obviously correct.” (p. 441) His reply to Shultz: “George, you've just invented a new form of the tragedy of the commons. You're locked in this system, and you can't fix it.” (pp. 441–442) Shultz: “Charlie, I do not want to think about this.” Munger: “I think he's wise. He's even older than I am, and maybe I should learn from him.” (p. 442) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 441–442)
- **HIM (Talk Nine)** — Why he tells true stories. “I don't invent these stories. Reality is always more ridiculous than what I'm going to tell you.” (p. 417) The rustic legislator: “He wanted to pass a law rounding pi to an even 3.2 so it would be easier for the school children to make the computations.” (p. 417) Sharon Stone, asked whether penis envy bothered her: “Absolutely not. I have more trouble than I can handle with what I've got.” (p. 417) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 417)
- **HIM (Talk Nine)** — The Irish priest. “We have a charming Irish Catholic priest in our neighborhood, and he loves to say, ‘Those old Jews may have invented guilt, but we perfected it.’” (p. 444) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 444)
- **HIM (Talk Nine)** — Hyman Liebowitz and his nails, told to end the talk. The Florida retirement, the weekly reports turning terrible, the sign by the airport: “They Used Leibowitz's Nails.” The son: “trust me. I will fix it.” Second visit: “They Didn't Use Leibowitz's Nails.” (p. 450) His comment: “Well, you can laugh at that. It is ridiculous, but it's no more ridiculous than the way a lot of people cling to failed ideas.” (p. 450) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 449–450)
- **HIM (Talk Nine Revisited, 2006)** — Looking back at the talk. “This waggish talk on economics, given in 2003, gave me pleasure as I put it together. But I hope it provided more than harmless fun.” “I even hope that some shred of my ideas eventually gets into academic economics, not because I want recognition, but because I think academic economics needs some improvement.” (p. 454) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 454)
- **AUTHOR (the editors, “A Matter of Trust”)** — The line the editors put under Munger's name. “Capitalism works best when there is trust in the system.” -Munger (p. 455; no source given). The editors on the two partners: “Charlie Munger and Warren Buffett are renowned for their steadfast commitment to unblemished integrity” (p. 455) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 455)
- **HIM (Talk Ten)** — Opening the 2007 USC Gould School of Law commencement. “Well, no doubt many of you why this speaker is so old. the answer is obvious: He hasn't died yet.” (p. 457; the .txt omits a verb after “many of you”) “I see a crowd of older people in the rear, not wearing robes. And I know from having educated an army of descendants, who it is that really deserves a lot of the honors that are being given today to the robe-wearing students in front.” (p. 457) “All my life I have admired Confucius.” “You people who don't think there's anything in this idea, please note how fast Asian people are rising in American life. I think they have something.” (p. 457) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 457)
- **HIM (Talk Ten)** — The funeral nobody could praise. “If the Cathedral is full of people at the funeral ceremony, most of them are there to celebrate the fact that the person is dead.” The Minister asks for something nice about the deceased; nobody comes forward until “one man came up and said, ‘Well, his brother was worse.’” “A life ending in such a funeral is not the life you want to have.” (p. 459) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 459)
- **HIM (Talk Ten)** — Giving offense by being right, and not hiding it. “You can cause enormous offense by being right in a way that causes somebody else to lose face in his own discipline or hierarchy. I never found the perfect way to avoid harm from this serious problem.” “Even though I was a good poker player when I was young, I was not good enough at pretending when I thought I knew more than my supervisors did. And I didn't try as hard at pretending as would have been prudent. So I gave a lot of offense. Now, I'm generally tolerated as a harmless eccentric who will soon be gone. But coming up, I had a difficult period to go through. My advice to you is to be better than I was at keeping insights hidden.” (p. 463) The senior partner's rule, as he relays it: “Listen, Chuck, I want to explain something to you. Your duty is to behave in such a way that the client thinks he's the smartest person in the room. If you have any energy or insight available after that, use it to make your senior partner look like the second smartest person in the room. And only after you've satisfied those two obligations, do you want your light to shine at all.” (pp. 463–464) His own: “Well, that was a good system for rising in many a large law firm. But it wasn't what I did. I usually moved with the drift of my nature, and if some other people didn't like it, well, I didn't need to be adored by everybody” (p. 464) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 463–464)
- **HIM (Talk Ten)** — Telling the graduates his generation failed them. “My generation has failed you to some extent. More and more, we're delivering to you in California a legislature in which mostly the certified nuts from the left, and the certified nuts from the right, are the ones allowed to serve. And none of them are removable. That's what my generation has done for you. But, you wouldn't like it to be too easy, would you?” (p. 476) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 476)
- **HIM (Talk Ten)** — Carrying his grandfather's torch. “My grandfather Munger was the only federal judge in his city for nearly forty years. And I admired him. I'm his namesake. And I'm Confucian enough that even now as I speak I'm thinking, ‘Well, Judge Munger would be pleased to have me here.’” “All these years after my grandfather is dead, I conceive myself as duty bound to carry the torch for my grandfather's values.” (p. 478) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 478)
- **HIM (Talk Ten)** — Anticipating trouble, and being ready for it. Housman, as he quotes it: “Mine were of trouble, And mine were steady, And I was ready when trouble came.” (p. 479) “I've gone through a long life anticipating trouble.” “It didn't make me unhappy to anticipate trouble all the time and be ready to perform adequately if trouble came. It didn't hurt me at all. In fact it helped me.” (p. 479) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 479)
- **HIM (Talk Ten)** — Ending the commencement. “Well, that's enough for one graduation. I hope these ruminations of an old man are useful to you.” (p. 480) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 480)
- **AUTHOR (the editors) + REPORTED (Donald Hall)** — The rewrite of the psychology talk. AUTHOR: “Charlie remarked that one of the most important talks in our list, ‘The Psychology of Human Misjudgment,’ could use ‘a little revising’” and his “‘little’ revision would amount to a full-scale rewrite, with loads of new material, and a ‘stop-the-press’ completion schedule.” (p. 480) REPORTED (Hall, attendee): “Charlie was espousing his well-reasoned views on behavioral finance before the term was even coined.” (p. 480) AUTHOR on the 2005 revision: “made from memory unassisted by any research, occurred because Charlie thought he could do better at age eighty-one than he did more than ten years earlier” (pp. 481–482) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 480–482)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Imitating a grocer's bequest. “I want to imitate to some extent the bequest practices of three characters: the protagonist in John Bunyan's Pilgrim's Progress, Benjamin Franklin, and my first employer, Ernest Buffett.” (p. 484) “Ernest Buffett did the best he could in the same mode when he left behind ‘How to Run a Grocery Store and a Few Things I Have Learned about Fishing.’ Whether or not this last contribution to the genre was the best, I will not say. But I will report that I have now known four generations of Ernest Buffett's descendants and that the results have encouraged my imitation of the founder.” (pp. 484–485) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 484–485)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — How his schools treated psychology. “I was educated in an era wherein the contributions of non-patient-treating psychology to an understanding of misjudgment met little approval from members of the mainstream elite.” “Like the Nietzsche character who was proud of his lame leg, the institutions were proud of their willful avoidance of ‘fuzzy’ psychology and ‘fuzzy’ psychology professors.” (pp. 485–486) “I shared this ignorant mindset for a considerable time. And so did a lot of other people.” (p. 486) “What are we to think, for instance, of the Caltech course catalogue that for years listed just one psychology professor, self-described as a ‘Professor of Psycho analytical Studies,’ who taught both ‘Abnormal Psychology’ and ‘Psychoanalysis in Literature’?” (p. 486; line break in “Psycho analytical” as printed) “Soon after leaving Harvard, I began a long struggle to get rid of the most dysfunctional part of my psychological ignorance.” (p. 486) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 485–486)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Curiosity about cults and insects. “Pure curiosity, somewhat later, made me wonder how and why destructive cults were often able, over a single long weekend, to turn many tolerably normal people into brainwashed zombies and thereafter keep them in that state indefinitely. I resolved that I would eventually find a good answer to this cult question if I could do so by general reading and much musing.” “I also got curious about social insects.” (p. 489) “The extreme success of the ants also fascinated me-how a few behavioral algorithms caused such extreme evolutionary success grounded in extremes of cooperation within the breeding colony” (p. 489) “We are too soon old and too late smart.” (p. 490; the German folk saying, as he gives it, on why he reached the psychology texts so late) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 489–490)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Cialdini's book. “But academic psychology has some very important merits alongside its defects. I learned this eventually, in the course of general reading, from a book, Influence, aimed at a popular audience, by a distinguished psychology professor, Robert Cialdini, at Arizona State, a very big university.” (p. 491) “Incidentally, the sale by Cialdini of hundreds of thousands of copies of a book about social psychology was a huge feat, considering that Cialdini didn't claim that he was going to improve your sex life or make you any money.” (pp. 491–492) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 491–492)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — The Harvard Law joke about Eddie Blanchard. “When I was at Harvard Law School, the professors sometimes talked about an overfocused, Skinner-like professor at Yale Law School. they used to say: “Poor old Eddie Blanchard, he thinks declaratory judgments will cure cancer.”” (p. 504) He uses it to name the tendency: “the man-with-a hammer tendency, after the folk saying” (the .txt prints “chat” for “that” inside the sentence). (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 504)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — What he thought of Harvard's teaching of property law. “And when I got to the Harvard Law School and its professors taught me “property law” with no mention of sibling rivalry in the family business, I appraised the School as a pretty unrealistic place that wore “blinders” like the milk-wagon horses of yore.” (p. 521) “My current guess is that sibling rivalry has not yet made it into property law as taught at Harvard.” (p. 521) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 521)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Letting other drivers in. “And many freeway drivers, including myself, will often let other drivers come in front of them, in lane changes or the like, because that is the courtesy they desire when roles are reversed.” (p. 533) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 533)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — The Irish priest on guilt. “There is a charming Irish Catholic priest in my neighborhood who, with rough accuracy, often says, “The old Jews may have invented guilt, but we Catholics perfected it.”” (p. 546) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 546)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Glotz and the tenant who was supported by “Glotz fellowships.” “I have a friend, whom I will now call “Glotz,” who had an amusing experience in favor-giving. Glotz owned an apartment building that he had bought because he wanted, eventually, to use the land in different development. Pending this outcome, Glotz was very lenient in collecting below-market rents from tenants.” (pp. 553–554) The tenant far behind in his rent at the hearing: “This proposal is outrageous. Glotz doesn't need any more money. I know this because I was supported in college by Glotz fellowships.” (p. 554) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 553–554)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — The Mungers' dog. “The Mungers once owned a tame and good natured dog that displayed the canine version of Deprival-Super Reaction Tendency. There was only one way to get bitten by this dog. And that was to try and take some food away from him after he already had it in his mouth.” (p. 565) “Nothing could be more stupid than for the dog to bite his master. But the dog couldn't help being foolish.” (pp. 565–566) “Humans are much the same as this Munger dog.” (p. 566) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 565–566)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — The golf-club maker who had been a zoning lawyer. “Such bad behavior drives some people from the zoning field. I once bought some golf clubs from an artisan who was formerly a lawyer. When I asked him what kind of law he had practiced, I expected to hear him say, “divorce law.” But his answer was, “zoning law.”” (p. 568) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 568)
- **REPORTED (his father, as Munger tells it) + HIM** — The pheasant licenses. “My father once told me that just after commencing law practice in Omaha, he went with a large group from Nebraska to South Dakota to hunt pheasants.” “All the Nebraska residents, one by one, signed up for South Dakota licenses with phony South Dakota addresses until it was my father's turn. Then, according to him, he barely prevented himself from doing what the others were doing, which was some sort of criminal offense.” (pp. 575–576) HIM, after: “Not everyone so resists the social contagion of bad behavior.” (p. 576) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 575–576)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Fighting with his cousin over one shingle. “One such interaction amused my family for years as people recalled the time when my cousin Russ and I, at ages three and four, fought and howled over a single surplus shingle while surrounded by a virtual sea of surplus shingles.” (p. 577) “But the adult versions of this occasion, boosted by psychological tendencies preserving ideologies, are not funny and can bring down whole civilizations.” (p. 578) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 577–578)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Doubting the bridge partner's frog and using it anyway. “A bridge-playing pal of mine once told me that a frog tossed into very hot water would jump out, but that the same frog would end up dying if placed in room-temperature water that was later heated at a very slow rate. My few shreds of physiological knowledge make me doubt this account.” (p. 581) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 581)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Telling two Freudian psychiatrists and a medical dean about Pavlov. “The two Freudian psychiatrists with whom I tried many years ago to discuss this work had never heard of it. And the dean of a major medical school actually asked me, several years ago, if any of Pavlov's experiments were “repeatable” in experiments of other researchers.” (pp. 584–585) “I first found a description of Pavlov's last work in a popular paperback, written by some Rockefeller-financed psychiatrist, when I was trying to figure out (1) how cults worked their horrible mischief and (2) what should the law say about what parents could do to “deprogram” children who had become brainwashed zombies.” (p. 585) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 584–585)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Losing calculus. “I was a whiz at calculus until age twenty, after which the skill was soon obliterated by total nonuse.” (p. 587) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 587)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Old, and disguising it. “Old people like me get pretty skilled, without working at it, at disguising age-related deterioraion because social convention, like clothing, hides much decline.” (p. 590; “deterioraion” as printed) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 590)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Fishing for tarpon in Costa Rica. “When I once fished in the Rio Colorado in Costa Rica, my guide, in a state of shock, told me a story about an angler who'd earlier come to the river without ever having fished for tarpon.” (pp. 593–594) The angler's rod: “Well, the angler threw his expensive rod at the fish, and when last seen, it was going down the Rio Colorado toward the ocean.” (p. 594) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 593–594)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — His own tact, compared with the Caltech professor's. After quoting the professor: “I include this quotation partly because I long suffered from backlash caused by my version of this professor's conversational manner. After much effort, I was able to improve only slightly, so one of my reasons for supplying the quotation is my hope that, at least in comparison, I will appear tactful.” (p. 596) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 596)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Handing the historical mysteries to others. “I now quitclaim all these historical mysteries to my betters.” (p. 600) Earlier, on the Milgram-era professors: “I will later deal with the subject in a very hesitant fashion.” (p. 593) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 593, 600)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven)** — Buffett and the pilots. “Well, naturally, cases like this one get the attention of careful thinkers like Boss Buffett, who always acts like an over quiet mouse around his pilots.” (p. 591) (Munger's description of Buffett's habit, tied by him to the copilot cases.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 591)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven, Questions and Answers)** — Manipulated at a dinner, and loved it. “I drew this beautiful woman as my dinner partner many years ago. I'd never seen her before. She was married to a prominent Los Angeles man. She sat down next to me, turned her beautiful face up, and said, "Charlie, what one word accounts for your remarkable success in life?" I knew I was being manipulated by a practiced routine, and I just loved it. I never see this woman without a little lift in my spirits. And, by the way, I told her I was rational. You'll have to judge yourself whether that's true. I may be demonstrating some psychological tendency I hadn't planned on demonstrating.” (pp. 607–608) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 607–608)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven, Questions and Answers)** — On his own errors and age as he ends the talk. “Aren't there factual and reasoning errors in this talk The answer is yes, almost surely yes.” (p. 609; question mark missing as printed) “the final revision was made from memory over about fifty hours by a man eighty- one years old, who never took a course in psychology and has read none of it, except one book on developmental psychology, for nearly fifteen years.” (p. 609; the .txt prints an apostrophe before “the,” and a space after “eighty-”) “Even so, I think the totality of my talk will stand up very well, and I hope all my descendants and friends will carefully consider what I have said.” (pp. 609–610) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 609–610)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven, Questions and Answers)** — His closing line to the audience. “Well that ends my talk. If in considering what I have said yo U had ten percent the fun I had saying It you were lucky recipients.” (p. 610; “yo U” and “It” as printed) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 610)
- **HIM (Talk Eleven Revisited)** — Harvard's liberal arts division, and the failed letter. After the Pinker hire he writes: “From this step, we can see one reason why its liberal arts division is more highly regarded than most others. The division's extreme depth often allows partial correction of bonehead errors that would flourish unopposed elsewhere.” (p. 611) (The letter itself is in DECISIONS.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 611)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — On being remembered. AUTHOR: “Munger said once,” “‘I may be remembered as a wise ass,’ whereas his investing partner Warren Buffett will be remembered as a teacher.” (The Complete Investor, p. 7)
- **AUTHOR** — Telling people not to copy him. “Munger has said that, although he has accumulated an outstanding record as a stock picker and accumulated substantial wealth, people should not emulate his example in life in general. He believes his life has been too devoted to improving his own mind and that peculiarities of his personality (including, but not limited to, irreverence) will make people unpopular if they blindly follow his example.” (The Complete Investor, p. 7)
- **HIM** — The answer at a dinner. Recalled by Munger in an interview, as the AUTHOR tells it: “Munger once recalled that a person sitting next to him at a dinner party asked him, ‘Tell me, what one quality accounts for your enormous success?’ Munger replied, ‘I'm rational. That's the answer. I'm rational.’” (The Complete Investor, p. 36)
- **REPORTED (his children, per the author)** — “His own children describe him as a ‘book with legs sticking out.’” (The Complete Investor, p. 11)
- **REPORTED (Buffett, per the author)** — Reading. “Buffett has said that Munger has read hundreds of biographies, as just one example.” Habit of the calendar, AUTHOR: “He is very purposeful in his approach to worldly wisdom, preferring not to fill his calendar with appointments and meetings.” (The Complete Investor, p. 42)
- **AUTHOR** — What he credits for his investing. “Munger ascribes no small amount of his financial success in investing to the time he spent playing poker and bridge.” (The Complete Investor, p. 76)
- **HIM** — Rubbing his nose in his mistakes. “I like people admitting they were complete stupid horses' asses. I know I'll perform better if I rub my nose in my mistakes. This is a wonderful trick to learn.” (The Complete Investor, p. 45; book's label: Berkshire Annual Meeting, 2011)
- **HIM** — Celebrating stupidities. “Why not celebrate stupidities in both categories?” (The Complete Investor, p. 46; book's label: Wesco Annual Meeting, 2006)
- **REPORTED (Li Lu, China Entrepreneur Magazine, 2010)** — How he thinks, in the words of a friend who invested with him. “When Charlie thinks about things, he starts by inverting. To understand how to be happy in life Charlie will study how to make life miserable; to examine how a business becomes big and strong, Charlie first studies how businesses decline and die; most people care more about how to succeed in the stock market, Charlie is most concerned about why most have failed in the stock market.” (The Complete Investor, p. 100)
- **REPORTED (Li Lu, China Entrepreneur Magazine, 2010; Li Lu's wording, not Munger's)** — What Munger told Li Lu about the limits of insight. “The true insights a person can get in life are still very limited, so correct decision making must necessarily be confined to your ‘circle of competence.’ A ‘competence’ that has no defined borders cannot be called a true competence.” (The Complete Investor, p. 100)
- **AUTHOR** — Money and objects. “Munger does not collect Ferraris or have a huge mansion.” (The Complete Investor, p. 8)

Covered: Damn Right!, pages 1–100, 101–200, 201–300, 301–400, 401–415; Poor Charlie's Almanack, pages 1–100, 101–200, 201–300, 301–400, 401–500, 501–600, 601–618; The Complete Investor, pages 1–100
